She therefore graciously smiled, assented, and did all she could to make time pass imperceptibly.

At two o'clock Sam and Andy brought the horses up to the posts, apparently greatly refreshed by the scamper of the morning.

"Your master, I s'pose, don't keep no dogs," said Haley, as he prepared to mount.

"Heaps on 'em," said Sam, triumphantly; "thar's Bruno—he's a roarer!"

"Poh!" said Haley. "But your master don't keep no dogs for trackin' out niggers."

Sam knew exactly what he meant, but kept on a look of earnest and desperate simplicity. "Our dogs all smells round considable sharp. They's _far_ dogs at most anything, if you'd get 'em started."

"You go hang!" said Haley. "Come, tumble up now."

"I shall take the straight road to the river," said Haley, when they had come to the boundaries of the estate. "I know the way of all of 'em,—they makes tracks for the underground."

"Sartin," said Sam, "dat's de idee. Now, der's two roads to de river,—de dirt road and der pike,—which Mas'r mean to take?"

Andy looked up innocently at Sam, surprised at hearing this new geographical fact, but instantly confirmed what he said by a vehement reiteration.

"'Cause," said Sam, "I'd rather be 'clined to 'magine that Lizy 'd take de dirt road, bein' it's the least travelled."

Haley, notwithstanding that he was a very old bird, was rather brought up by this view of the case. "If yer warn't both on yer such cussed liars, now!" he said contemplatively.

The pensive tone amused Andy prodigiously, while Sam's face was immovably composed into the most doleful gravity.

"Course," said Sam, "Mas'r can do as he'd ruther. Now, when I study 'pon it, I think de straight road de best, _deridedly_."

"She would naturally go a lonesome way," said Haley, thinking aloud.

"Dar an't no sayin'," said Sam; "gals is pecular; they never does nothin' ye thinks they will; mose gen'lly the contrary. Now, my private 'pinion is, Lizy took der road; so I think we'd better take de straight one."

This profound generic view did not dispose Haley to the straight road, and he announced he should go the other.

"Now I think on 't, I think I hearn 'em tell that dat ar road was all fenced up and down by der creek, and thar, an't it, Andy?"

Andy was strictly noncommittal.

Haley, accustomed to strike the balance between lies of greater or lesser magnitude, thought that it lay in favor of the dirt road. The mention of the thing he thought he perceived was involuntary on Sam's part at first, and his confused attempts to dissuade he set down to a desperate lying on second thoughts. When Sam indicated the road, Haley plunged briskly into it.

The road, in fact, was an old one, abandoned for many years after the laying of the new pike. It was open for about an hour's ride, and after that cut across by various farms and fences. Sam knew this perfectly well; Andy had never heard of it. He rode along with an air of dutiful submission, only groaning that 't was "desp't rough, and bad for Jerry's foot."

"Now, I jest give yer warning," said Haley, "I know yer; yer won't get me to turn off this road, with all yer fussin'—so you shet up!"

"Mas'r will go his own way!" said Sam, with rueful submission, winking most portentously to Andy.

Sam was in wonderful spirits—at one time exclaiming that he saw "a gal's bonnet" on the top of some distant eminence, or calling to Andy "if that thar wasn't 'Lizy' down in the hollow"; always making these exclamations in some rough or craggy part of the road, where the sudden quickening of speed was a special inconvenience to all parties concerned, and thus keeping Haley in a state of constant commotion.

After riding about an hour, the whole party made a precipitate and tumultuous descent into a barn-yard belonging to a large farming establishment. Not a soul was in sight; and as the barn stood plainly square across the road, it was evident their journey in that direction had reached a decided finale.

"Wan't dat ar what I telled Mas'r?" said Sam, with an air of injured innocence. "How does strange gentleman spect to know more about a country dan de natives born and raised?"

"You rascal!" said Haley, "you knew all about this."

"Didn't I tell yer I _knowd_, and yer wouldn't believe me? I telled Mas'r 't was all shet up, and fenced up, and I didn't spect we could get through,—Andy heard me."

It was all too true to be disputed, and the unlucky man had to pocket his wrath, and all three faced about for the highway.

In consequence of all the various delays, it was about three-quarters of an hour after Eliza had laid her child to sleep that the party came riding into the same village. Eliza was standing by the window, looking out in another direction, when Sam's quick eye caught a glimpse of her. At this crisis, Sam contrived to have his hat blown off, and uttered a loud and characteristic ejaculation, which startled her at once; she drew suddenly back; the whole train swept by the window, round to the front door.

A thousand lives seemed to be concentrated in that one moment to Eliza. Her room opened by a side door to the river. She caught her child, and sprang down the steps towards it. The trader caught a full glimpse of her just as she was disappearing down the bank; and throwing himself from his horse, and calling loudly on Sam and Andy, he was after her like a hound after a deer. In that dizzy moment her feet to her scarce seemed to touch the ground, and a moment brought her to the water's edge. Right on behind they came; and, nerved with strength such as God gives only to the desperate, with one wild cry and flying leap, she vaulted sheer over the turbid current by the shore, on to the raft of ice beyond. It was a desperate leap—impossible to anything but madness and despair; and Haley, Sam, and Andy, instinctively cried out, and lifted up their hands, as she did it.

The huge green fragment of ice on which she alighted pitched and creaked as her weight came on it, but she staid there not a moment. With wild cries and desperate energy she leaped to another and still another cake; stumbling—leaping—slipping—springing upwards again! Her shoes are gone—her stockings cut from her feet—while blood marked every step; but she saw nothing, felt nothing, till dimly, as in a dream, she saw the Ohio side, and a man helping her up the bank.

"Yer a brave gal, now, whoever ye ar!" said the man, with an oath.

Eliza recognized the voice and face for a man who owned a farm not far from her old home.

"O, Mr. Symmes!—save me—do save me—do hide me!" said Eliza.

"Why, what's this?" said the man. "Why, if 'tan't Shelby's gal!"

"My child!—this boy!—he'd sold him! There is his Mas'r," said she, pointing to the Kentucky shore. "O, Mr. Symmes, you've got a little boy!"

"So I have," said the man, as he roughly, but kindly, drew her up the steep bank. "Besides, you're a right brave gal. I like grit, wherever I see it."

When they had gained the top of the bank, the man paused. "I'd be glad to do something for ye; but then there's nowhar I could take ye. The best I can do is to tell ye to go _thar_," said he, pointing to a large white house which stood by itself, off the main street. "Go thar; they're kind folks. Thar's no kind o' danger but they'll help you,—they're up to all that sort o' thing."

"The Lord bless you!" said Eliza, earnestly.

"No 'casion, no 'casion in the world. Come, now, go along like a likely, sensible gal, as you are. You've arnt your liberty, and you shall have it, for all me."

The woman folded her child to her bosom, and walked firmly and swiftly away. The man stood and looked after her.

"Shelby, now, mebbe won't think this yer the most neighborly thing in the world; but what's a feller to do? Somehow I never could see no kind o' critter a strivin' and pantin', and trying to clar theirselves, with the dogs arter 'em and go agin 'em. Besides, I don't see no kind of 'casion for me to be hunter and catcher for other folks, neither."

So spoke this poor, heathenish Kentuckian, who had not been instructed in his constitutional relations, and consequently was betrayed into acting in a sort of Christianized manner, which, if he had been better situated and more enlightened, he would not have been left to do.

Haley had stood a perfectly amazed spectator till Eliza had disappeared up the bank, when he turned a blank, inquiring look on Sam and Andy.

"That ar was a tolable fair stroke of business," said Sam.

"The gal 's got seven devils in her, I believe!" said Haley. "How like a wildcat she jumped!"

"Wal, now," said Sam, scratching his head, "I hope Mas'r'll 'scuse us trying dat ar road. Don't think I feel spry enough for dat ar, no way!" and Sam gave a hoarse chuckle.

"_You_ laugh!" said the trader, with a growl.

"Lord bless you, Mas'r, I couldn't help it now," said Sam, giving way to the long pent-up delight of his soul. "She looked so curi's, a leapin' and springin'—ice a crackin'—and only to hear her,—plump! ker chunk! ker splash! Spring! Lord! how she goes it!" and Sam and Andy laughed till the tears rolled down their cheeks.

"I'll make ye laugh t' other side yer mouths!" said the trader, laying about their heads with his riding-whip.

Both ducked, and ran shouting up the bank, and were on their horses before he was up.

"Good-evening, Mas'r!" said Sam, with much gravity. "I berry much spect Missis be anxious 'bout Jerry. Mas'r Haley won't want us no longer. Missis wouldn't hear of our ridin' the critters over Lizy's bridge tonight"; and, with a facetious poke into Andy's ribs, he started off, followed by the latter, at full speed,—their shouts of laughter coming faintly on the wind.

Eliza made her desperate retreat across the river just at dusk. The gray mist rose from the Ohio, and she vanished up the farther bank while the swollen current and floundering ice made a hopeless barrier between her and the man who hunted her. Haley, the trader, turned back, sour and baffled, to the little tavern.

A woman showed him into a parlor with a rag carpet, a black shining oil-cloth on the table, lank high-backed chairs, plaster saints on the mantel above a dimly-smoking grate, and a long settle by the chimney. Here Haley sat down to meditate on the instability of human hopes, and to curse himself in a style too select for rehearsal.

He started at the harsh voice of a man dismounting at the door, and hurried to the window.

“By the land! if this yer an’t the nearest to Providence,” said he. “I do b’lieve that ar’s Tom Loker.”

He hastened out. At the bar stood a brawny, bull-dog sort of fellow in a buffalo-skin coat, hair outward,—Tom Loker by name,—every feature speaking brutal, unhesitating violence. With him came a small, lithe companion, sleek, black-haired, sharp-nosed, with keen, mousing eyes and a dry, cautious air. The great man tossed off raw spirits; the little man ordered a julep, smelt and tasted it with exquisite circumspection, and sipped it in well-advised drops.

“Wal, now, who’d a thought this luck ’ad come to me? Why, Loker, how are ye?” cried Haley, advancing with his hand.

“The devil!” was the civil reply. “What brought you here, Haley?”

The small man ceased sipping, and poked his head forward to survey the newcomer, like a cat watching a fluttering leaf.

“I say, Tom, this yer’s the luckiest thing in the world. I’m in a hobble, and you must help me out.”

“Ugh? aw! like enough!” grunted Loker. “A body may be pretty sure o’ that, when you’re glad to see ’em; something to be made off of ’em. What’s the blow now?”

“You’ve got a friend here?” said Haley, glancing at the small man; “partner, perhaps?”

“Yes, I have. Here’s that ar feller I was in with in Natchez.”

“Shall be pleased with his acquaintance,” said the little one, thrusting out a hand like a raven’s claw. “Mr. Haley, I believe?”

“The same, sir. And now, gentlemen, seein’ as we’ve met so happy, I’ll stand treat in this yer parlor. Hot water, sugar, cigars, and plenty o’ the real stuff!” he called to the landlord. “We’ll have a blow-out.”

Candles were lighted, the grate urged to a glow, and the three worthies sat with their bottles and tumblers. Haley began a pathetic recital of his wrongs; Loker listened, mouth shut, with surly attention; the small man, while compounding his punch with anxious ceremony, from time to time thrust nose and chin almost into Haley’s face, to lose not a word. The end of the tale threw him into a silent shaking laugh; he perked up his thin lips, shoulders heaving with inward enjoyment.

“So, then, ye’r fairly sewed up, an’t ye?” he said. “He! he! It’s neatly done.”

“This young-un business makes lots of trouble in the trade,” groaned Haley.

“If we could raise a breed o’ gals that didn’t care for their young uns,” said the little man; “’t would be ’bout the greatest mod’rn improvement I knows on.” He patronized the joke with a dry sniggle.

“Jes so,” said Haley. “The more trouble a young un is, and the more good for nothin’, as a gen’l thing, the tighter they sticks.”

“Wal, jest so with me,” said the small man. “Once I bought a gal,—tight, likely wench, and considerable smart,—and she had a young un, mis’able sickly, crooked-backed, or somethin’. I gin it away to a man that thought he’d chance raisin’ on ’t, bein’ it didn’t cost nothin’. I never thought o’ the gal’s takin’ on about it,—but, Lord! yer oughter seen her. She cried and lopped round as if she’d lost every friend she had. It re’lly was droll to think on ’t; there ain’t no end to women’s notions.”

“Wal, down on Red River,” put in Haley, “I got a gal traded off on me with a likely lookin’ child. Eyes bright as yourn; but, come to look, stone blind,—fact. So I passes him along, not sayin’ nothin’, and got him swapped for a keg o’ whiskey; but, come to get him away, the gal was jest like a tiger. ’T was afore we started, and I hadn’t my gang chained; so what should she do but ups on a cotton bale, like a cat, ketches a knife, and made all fly for a minnit; and, when she seed ’t warn’t no use, she pitches head first, young un and all, into the river,—went down plump, and never ris.”

“Bah!” growled Loker, who had listened with ill-suppressed disgust; “shif’less, both on ye! My gals don’t cut up no such shines.”

“Indeed! how do you help it?” said the small man, briskly.

“Help it? why, I buys a gal,—if she’s got a young un to be sold, I jest walks up, puts my fist to her face, an’ says, ‘Look here, now, if you give me one word out o’ your head, I’ll smash yer face in. I won’t hear one word—not the beginnin’ of a word. This young un’s mine, and not yourn; I’m goin’ to sell it first chance; mind ye don’t cut up none o’ yer shines, or I’ll make ye wish ye’d never been born.’ They sees ’t an’t no play when I gets hold. I makes ’em as whist as fishes.” He brought down his fist with a thump that explained his meaning.

“That ar’s emphasis,” said the little man, with another giggle. “An’t Tom peculiar? If you an’t the devil, Tom, you ’s his twin brother, I’ll say that for ye!”

Tom received the compliment with a rough complacency.

Haley, warmed by much drink, began to elevate and enlarge his moral faculties.

“Wal, now, Tom,” said he, gesturing from his chair, “ye re’lly is too bad, as I’ve al’ays told ye. Ye know, down in Natchez we used to talk, and I proved to ye we made full as much, and was as well off for this world, by treatin’ on ’em well; besides, keepin’ a better chance for comin’ in the kingdom at last, when wust comes to wust. I b’lieve in religion; and one o’ these days, when I’ve got matters tight and snug, I calculates to tend to my soul; and so what’s the use o’ doin’ any more wickedness than ’s re’lly necessary?—’t an’t prudent.”

“Tend to yer soul!” returned Tom, with contempt. “Take a bright lookout to find a soul in you. If the devil sifts you through a hair sieve, he won’t find one.”

“Why, Tom, you’re cross,” said Haley. “Why can’t ye take it pleasant, when a feller’s talkin’ for yer good?”

“Stop that ar pious jaw,” said Tom, gruffly. “After all, what’s the odds ’twixt me and you? ’Tan’t that you care more,—it’s clean, dog meanness,—wantin’ to cheat the devil and save your own skin. Your gettin’ religion arter all is too p’isin mean; run up a bill with the devil all your life, and then sneak out when pay time comes!”

“Come, come, gentlemen,” interposed the small man; “this isn’t business. There’s different ways o’ lookin’. Mr. Haley’s a nice man, and has his conscience; and, Tom, you have your ways; but quarrellin’ won’t answer. Let’s go to business. Now, Mr. Haley, you want us to undertake to catch this yer gal?”

“The gal’s no matter o’ mine,—she’s Shelby’s. It’s only the boy. I was a fool for buyin’ the monkey.”

“You’re gen’rally a fool,” growled Tom.

“Come, Loker, none o’ your huffs,” purred the small man. “Mr. Haley’s puttin’ us in the way of a job. This yer gal—how is she? what is she?”

“Wal! white and handsome,—well brought up. I’d a gin Shelby eight hundred or a thousand, and made well on her.”

“White and handsome—well brought up!” The small man’s sharp features all started with enterprise. “Look here, now, Loker,—beautiful openin’. We’ll do a business here on our own account; we does the catchin’; the boy, o’ course, goes to Mr. Haley; we takes the gal to Orleans to speculate on. An’t it beautiful?”

Loker’s heavy mouth, that had stood ajar, snapped to like a dog’s on a bone. He brought his fist down till all rang. “It’ll do!”

“Lord bless ye, Tom, save your fist for time o’ need,” said the other. “Now, ye see,” he continued to Haley, stirring his punch, “we has justices convenient at all p’ints, that does up little jobs in our line reasonable. Tom does the knockin’ down; I comes in all dressed—shinin’ boots—first chop—when the swearin’s to be done. One day, I’m Mr. Twickem, from New Orleans; ’nother, a planter from Pearl River, workin’ seven hundred niggers; then a distant relation of Henry Clay. Talents is different. Tom’s a roarer for thumpin’; but at lyin’ he an’t good,—don’t come natural. But as for swearin’ to anything and puttin’ in the circumstances with a long face, I’d like to see the feller beat me. Sometimes I rather wish justices was more particular; ’t would be more relishin’, more fun, yer know.”

“But, gentlemen, an’t I to come in for a share?” said Haley.

“An’t it enough we catch the boy for ye?” said Loker. “What more d’ye want?”

“Wal, if I gives you the job, say ten per cent. on the profits, expenses paid,” ventured Haley.

“Don’t I know you, Dan Haley?” thundered Tom, with an oath, and another blow on the table. “Don’t think to come it over me! S’pose we’ve took up the catchin’ trade jest to ’commodate gentlemen like you, and git nothin’ for ourselves? Not by a long chalk! We’ll have the gal out and out; and you keep quiet, or we’ll have both. What’s to hinder? Han’t you showed us the game? It’s as free to us as you.”

“O, wal, let it go at that,” said Haley, scared. “You catch the boy; you allers did trade far with me, Tom, and was up to yer word.”

“Ye know that,” said Tom. “I won’t lie in my ’counts with the devil himself. What I ses I’ll do, I will do—you know that, Dan Haley.”

“Jes so. Promise to have the boy for me in a week, at any p’int you’ll name,—that’s all I want.”

“It an’t all I want, by a long jump,” said Tom. “I’ve learned to hold an eel, when I catch him. You’ve got to fork over fifty dollars, flat down, or this child don’t start a peg.”

“Why, when the job may bring you a clean profit of a thousand or sixteen hundred, you’re onreasonable,” said Haley.

“Yes—and hasn’t we business booked five weeks ahead? S’pose we leaves all and goes bush-whackin’ arter yer young uns, and don’t catch the gal,—and gals allers is the devil to catch,—what then? Would you pay us a cent? No, no; flap down your fifty. If we get the job, and it pays, I’ll hand it back; if we don’t, it’s for our trouble. That’s far,—an’t it?” said the small man, soothingly. “It’s only a retainin’ fee,—we lawyers, yer know. Keep easy. Tom’ll have the boy for ye anywhere ye’ll name.”

“If I find the young un,” said Loker, “I’ll bring him on to Cincinnati, and leave him where you can call.”

“Now, to the particulars,” resumed the small man, pulling from a greasy pocket-book a long paper, and mumbling over sundry cases of “man and wife—six hundred;” “wench Polly and two children—six hundred for her or her head;” “boy Jim—three hundred, dead or alive.” “Them easy cases can wait. Now, Mr. Haley, you saw the gal land?”

“To be sure,—as plain as I see you.”

“And a man helpin’ her up the bank?” said Tom.

“To be sure, I did.”

“Most likely she’s took in somewheres,” mused the small man; “but where’s the question. Tom?”

“We must cross the river to-night—no mistake,” said Tom.

“But there’s no boat about,” objected the small one. “The ice is runnin’ awfully; an’t it dangerous?”

“Don’ know nothin’ ’bout that—only it’s got to be done,” said Tom.

“Dear me—it’s—” he said, going to the window; “it’s dark as a wolf’s mouth, and, Tom—”

“The long and short is, you’re scared,” said Tom. “But you’ve got to go. Lie by a day, and the gal’ll be on the underground line and up to Sandusky ’fore you start.”

“O, no; I an’t a grain afraid—only—”

“Only what?”

“Well, about the boat. There an’t any.”

“I heard the woman say there was one comin’ along this evenin’, and a man goin’ to cross in it. Neck or nothin’, we must go with him,” said Tom.

“I s’pose you’ve got good dogs,” said Haley.

“First rate,” said the small man; “but what’s the use? you han’t got nothin’ o’ hers to smell on.”

“Yes, I have,” said Haley, triumphantly. “Here’s her shawl; she left it on the bed in her hurry. Her bonnet, too.”

“That ar’s lucky,” said Loker. “Fork over.”

“Though the dogs might damage the gal, if they come on her unawares,” muttered Haley.

“That ar’s a consideration,” said the other. “Our dogs tore a feller half to pieces once, down in Mobile, ’fore we could get ’em off.”

“For this sort that’s to be sold for their looks, that ar won’t answer,” said Haley.

“I do see,” returned the small man. “Besides, if she’s took in, ’tan’t no go neither. Dogs is no ’count up in these yer States; they only does in plantations, where niggers does their own runnin’.”

Loker had stepped to the bar to inquire. “They say the man’s come with the boat,” he reported. The small man cast a rueful look at the warm room, but rose. After a word or two more, Haley, with visible reluctance, counted out the fifty dollars into Tom’s hard palm; and the trio separated for the night.

If any refined and Christian reader object to the company here shown, let him conquer his squeamishness betimes. The catching business, we remind him, rises, under certain constitutions, to the dignity of a lawful and patriotic profession; and if human property persists in its locomotive tendencies, the trader and the catcher may yet sit among our aristocracy.

While this went on at the tavern, Sam and Andy—Shelby’s men—rode home in high felicitation. Sam was in feathers, venting his exultation in whoops, somersets on the saddle, and solemn lectures to Andy for laughing. He kept the horses to their best pace till, between ten and eleven, their heels clattered under the balcony. Mrs. Shelby, the mistress, flew to the railing; Mr. Shelby, her husband, followed.

“Is that you, Sam? Where are they?” she cried.

“Mas’r Haley ’s a-restin’ at the tavern; he’s drefful fatigued, Missis.”

“And Eliza, Sam?”

“Wal, she’s clar ’cross Jordan,—as a body may say,—in the land o’ Canaan.”

“Why, Sam, what do you mean?” gasped Mrs. Shelby, the possible sense of those words blanching her.

“Wal, Missis, de Lord he persarves his own. Lizy’s done gone over de river into ’Hio, as ’markably as ef de Lord took her over in a charrit o’ fire and two hosses.”

“Come up here, Sam,” said Mr. Shelby, passing his arm about his wife, who stood shivering. “You allow yourself to feel too much, Emily.”

“Feel too much! Am not I a woman,—a mother? Are we not both responsible to God for this poor girl? My God! lay not this sin to our charge.”

“What sin, Emily? You see we have only done what we were obliged to.”

“There’s an awful feeling of guilt about it, though,” she said.

“Now, Sam,” said Mr. Shelby, as Sam appeared at the parlor-door, palm-leaf in hand, “tell us distinctly. Where is Eliza?”

“Wal, Mas’r, I saw her, with my own eyes, a crossin’ on de floatin’ ice. She crossed most ’markably; it wasn’t no less nor a miracle; and I saw a man help her up de ’Hio side; den she was lost in de dusk.”

“Sam, I think this rather apocryphal,—this miracle. Crossing on floating ice isn’t so easily done,” said Mr. Shelby.

“Easy! couldn’t nobody a done it without de Lord,” said Sam. “’T was jis dis yer way. Mas’r Haley, and me, and Andy, we comes up to de little tavern by de river. I rides a leetle ahead,—(I’s so zealous to be a cotchin’ Lizy, I couldn’t hold in, no way),—and, when I comes by de winder, sure enough dere she was, right in plain sight, and dey diggin’ on behind. Wal, I loses off my hat, and sings out nuff to raise de dead. Course Lizy she hars, and she dodges back, when Mas’r Haley goes past; and den she clars out de side door, down to de river. Dere was de current runnin’ ten feet wide by de shore, and over t’other side ice a sawin’ and a jiggling up and down, kinder like a great island. We come right behind her; and I thought my soul he’d got her sure,—when she gin sich a screech as I never hearn, and dere she was, clar over t’other side o’ de current, on de ice! and den on she went, a screechin’ and a jumpin’;—de ice went crack! c’wallop! crackin’! chunk!—and she a boundin’ like a buck! Lord, de spring dat ar gal’s got in her an’t common, I’m o’ ’pinion.”

Mrs. Shelby sat white and still, hands clasped. “God be praised, she isn’t dead! But where is the poor child now?”

“De Lord will pervide,” said Sam, rolling up his eyes. “As I’s been a sayin’, dis yer’s a providence. Thar’s allers instruments ris up to do de Lord’s will. Now, if ’t hadn’t been for me to-day, she’d a been took a dozen times. Warn’t it I started off de hosses dis mornin’, and kep ’em chasin’ till nigh dinner? And didn’t I car Mas’r Haley nigh five miles out o’ de road dis evenin’, or else he’d a come up wid Lizy as easy as a dog arter a coon? Dese yer’s all providences.”

“They are a kind of providences you’ll have to be sparing of, Master Sam. I allow no such practices with gentlemen on my place,” said Mr. Shelby, with such sternness as he could command.

There is little use in making believe be angry with a negro or a child; both instinctively see through the veil. Sam, not disheartened, drew down the corners of his mouth in penitential gravity.

“Mas’r quite right,—quite; ’t was ugly on me; and course Mas’r and Missis wouldn’t ’courage no such works. But a poor nigger’s ’mazin’ tempted to act ugly when fellers cuts up sich shines as dat ar Mas’r Haley; he an’t no gen’l’man, no way; anybody’s been raised as I’s been can’t help seein’ dat ar.”

“Well, Sam,” said Mrs. Shelby, “as you appear to have a proper sense of your errors, go now and tell Aunt Chloe she may get you some of that cold ham left from dinner. You and Andy must be hungry.”

“Missis is a heap too good for us,” said Sam, bowing, and departed.

Sam had a native talent for making capital of everything that turned up for his own especial glory. Having done up his piety and humility to the parlor, he cocked his palm-leaf with a rakish air, and proceeded to Aunt Chloe’s dominions, to flourish in the kitchen. There existed between him and Aunt Chloe, the cook, a decided coolness; but, meditating designs on the provision department, Sam came eminently conciliatory,—dwelt on the hardship he had suffered in behalf of a persecuted fellow-creature, and how Missis herself had ordered him to Aunt Chloe for supplies,—thus unequivocally acknowledging her sovereignty in culinary matters.

It took accordingly. No simple, virtuous body was ever more easily cajoled by an electioneering politician than Aunt Chloe was won by Sam’s suavities. In a trice he sat happy and glorious over a large tin pan containing a sort of olla podrida of all that had appeared for two or three days,—savory ham, golden corn-cake, fragments of pie, chicken wings, gizzards, drumsticks,—Sam, monarch of all he surveyed, palm-leaf rakishly askew, patronizing Andy at his right.

The kitchen filled. From cabins round about flocked compeers to hear the day’s exploits. Now was Sam’s hour of glory. The story was rehearsed with all ornament and varnish fit to heighten effect, for Sam never let a tale lose gilding in his hands. Roars of laughter attended; the smaller fry sprawled on floor and perched in corners, squealing; Sam, in the midst, kept an immovable gravity, rolling up his eyes and darting droll glances, without dropping the sententious elevation of his oratory.

“Yer see, fellow-countrymen,” said Sam, elevating a turkey’s leg, “yer see what dis yer chile’s up ter, for fendin’ yer all,—yes, all on yer. For him as tries to git one o’ our people is as good as tryin’ to git all; de principle’s de same,—dat ar’s clar. And any o’ dese yer drivers comes smellin’ arter any our people, why, he’s got me in his way; I’m de feller he’s got to set in wid;—I’ll stand up for yer rights; I’ll fend ’em to de last breath!”

“Why, but, Sam, yer telled me jis dis mornin’ you’d help Mas’r to cotch Lizy; seems to me yer talk don’t hang together,” said Andy.

“I tell you now, Andy,” said Sam, with awful superiority, “don’t yer be a talkin’ ’bout what yer don’t know nothin’ on. Boys like you means well, but dey can’t be spected to collusitate de great principles o’ action.”

Andy looked rebuked, particularly by “collusitate,” which the younger listeners took as a settler, while Sam went on.

“Dat ar was conscience, Andy. When I fust thought o’ gwine arter Lizy, I spected Mas’r was sot dat way. When I found Missis was sot contrar, dat ar was conscience more yet,—’cause fellers gits more by stickin’ to Missis’ side. So yer see I’s persistent either way,—sticks up to conscience, and holds on to principles. Yes, principles,” cried Sam, tossing a chicken’s neck. “What’s principles good for, if we isn’t persistent? Thar, Andy, you may have dat ar bone; ’tan’t picked quite clean.”

“Dis yer matter ’bout persistence,” continued Sam, with the air of entering an abstruse theme, “dis yer ’sistency an’t seed inter very clar by mos’ anybody. Folks ses,—and nat’rally dey ses,—when a feller stan’s up for a thing one day and de contrar de next, why he an’t persistent. Hand me dat bit o’ corn-cake, Andy. But let’s look inter it. S’pose I’s tryin’ to git top o’ de hay. I puts up my larder dis side; ’tan’t no go. Den, ’cause I don’t try dere no more, but puts my larder right de contrar side,—an’t I persistent? I’s persistent in wantin’ to git up, which ary side my larder is. Don’t ye see, all on yer?”

“It’s the only thing ye ever was persistent in, Lord knows!” muttered Aunt Chloe, whom so much merriment affected like vinegar upon nitre.

“Yes, indeed!” said Sam, rising, full of supper and glory, for a closing effort. “Yes, my feller-citizens and ladies o’ de other sex, I has principles,—I’s proud to ’oon ’em; dey’s perquisite to dese yer times, and to all times. I has principles, and I sticks to ’em like forty; jest anythin’ I thinks is principle, I goes in to ’t;—I wouldn’t mind if dey burnt me ’live; I’d walk right up to de stake, and say, ‘Here I comes to shed my last blood fur my principles, fur my country, fur de gen’l interests o’ society.’”

“Well,” said Aunt Chloe, “one o’ yer principles’ll have to be to git to bed some time to-night, and not keep everybody up till mornin’. Now, every one o’ you young uns that don’t want to be cracked had better be scase, mighty sudden.”

“Niggers! all on yer,” said Sam, waving his palm-leaf with benignity, “I give yer my blessin’. Go to bed now, and be good boys.”

With this pathetic benediction, the assembly broke up.

In Which It Appears That a Senator Is But a Man

The light of the cheerful fire shone on the rug and carpet of a cosey parlor, and glittered on the tea-cups and well-brightened tea-pot, as Senator Bird drew off his boots to put on a pair of new handsome slippers his wife had been working for him while he was away. Mrs. Bird, the very picture of delight, superintended the table and, ever and anon, admonished a set of frolicsome juveniles who effervesced in untold gambol and mischief.

“Tom, let the door-knob alone,—there’s a man! Mary! Mary! don’t pull the cat’s tail,—poor pussy! Jim, you mustn’t climb on that table,—no, no!—You don’t know, my dear, what a surprise it is to us all, to see you here tonight!” she cried at last, finding space to speak to her husband.

“Yes, yes, I thought I’d just make a run down, spend the night, and have a little comfort at home. I’m tired to death, and my head aches!”

Mrs. Bird cast a glance at the camphor-bottle in the half-open closet, but he interposed.

“No, no, Mary, no doctoring! A cup of your good hot tea, and some of our good home living, is what I want. It’s a tiresome business, this legislating!”

He smiled, as if he rather liked to consider himself a sacrifice to his country.

“Well,” said his wife, when the tea-table business grew slack, “and what have they been doing in the Senate?”

Mr. Bird opened his eyes in surprise, for it was unusual that gentle little Mrs. Bird should trouble her head with state matters. “Not very much of importance.”

“Well; but is it true they have been passing a law forbidding people to give meat and drink to those poor colored folks that come along? I heard they were talking of some such law, but I didn’t think any Christian legislature would pass it!”

“Why, Mary, you are getting to be a politician, all at once.”

“No, nonsense! I wouldn’t give a fig for all your politics, generally; but I think this is something downright cruel and unchristian. I hope, my dear, no such law has been passed.”

“There has been a law passed forbidding people to help off the slaves that come over from Kentucky, my dear; so much of that thing has been done by these reckless Abolitionists, that our brethren in Kentucky are very strongly excited, and it seems necessary, and no more than Christian and kind, that something should be done by our state to quiet the excitement.”

“And what is the law? It don’t forbid us to shelter those poor creatures a night, does it, and to give ’em something comfortable to eat, and a few old clothes, and send them quietly about their business?”

“Why, yes, my dear; that would be aiding and abetting, you know.”

Mrs. Bird was a timid, blushing little woman, with mild blue eyes and the gentlest voice in the world; as for courage, a cock-turkey could put her to rout at the first gobble, and a stout house-dog would bring her into subjection by the mere show of his teeth. Yet there was one thing that could rouse her,—anything in the shape of cruelty. Then the general softness of her nature flashed into something vehement and resolute.

On the present occasion, her cheeks went very red, which improved her appearance; she walked up to her husband with a resolute air.

“Now, John, I want to know if you think such a law as that is right and Christian?”

“You won’t shoot me, now, Mary, if I say I do!”

“I never could have thought it of you, John; you didn’t vote for it?”

“Even so, my fair politician.”

“You ought to be ashamed, John! Poor, homeless, houseless creatures! It’s a shameful, wicked, abominable law, and I’ll break it, for one, the first time I get a chance; and I hope I shall have a chance, I do! Things have got to a pretty pass, if a woman can’t give a warm supper and a bed to poor, starving creatures, just because they are slaves, and have been abused and oppressed all their lives, poor things!”

“But, Mary, just listen to me. Your feelings are all quite right, dear, and interesting, and I love you for them; but, then, dear, we mustn’t suffer our feelings to run away with our judgment; you must consider it’s a matter of private feeling,—there are great public interests involved,—there is such a state of public agitation rising, that we must put aside our private feelings.”

“Now, John, I don’t know anything about politics, but I can read my Bible; and there I see that I must feed the hungry, clothe the naked, and comfort the desolate; and that Bible I mean to follow.”

“But in cases where your doing so would involve a great public evil—”

“Obeying God never brings on public evils. I know it can’t. It’s always safest, all round, to do as He bids us.”

“Now, listen to me, Mary, and I can state to you a very clear argument, to show—”

“O, nonsense, John! you can talk all night, but you wouldn’t do it. I put it to you, John,—would you now turn away a poor, shivering, hungry creature from your door, because he was a runaway? Would you, now?”

He hemmed and coughed, and wiped his glasses, and did various things that gave time to a humane nature thus pressed in a sore point, for this was not his forte, as his wife well knew.

“I should like to see you doing that, John—I really should! Turning a woman out of doors in a snow-storm, for instance; or maybe you’d take her up and put her in jail, wouldn’t you? You would make a great hand at that!”

“Of course, it would be a very painful duty,” began Mr. Bird.

“Duty, John! don’t use that word! You know it isn’t a duty—it can’t be a duty! If folks want to keep their slaves from running away, let ’em treat ’em well,—that’s my doctrine. I tell you folks don’t run away when they are happy; and when they do run, poor creatures! they suffer enough with cold and hunger and fear, without everybody’s turning against them; and, law or no law, I never will, so help me God!”

“Mary! Mary! My dear, let me reason with you.”

“I hate reasoning, John,—especially reasoning on such subjects. There’s a way you political folks have of coming round and round a plain right thing; and you don’t believe in it yourselves, when it comes to practice. I know you well enough, John. You don’t believe it’s right any more than I do; and you wouldn’t do it any sooner than I.”

At this critical juncture, old Cudjoe, the black man-of-all-work, put his head in at the door and wished “Missis would come into the kitchen;” and our senator, tolerably relieved, looked after his little wife with a whimsical mixture of amusement and vexation, and seated himself to read the papers.

“John! John! I do wish you’d come here, a moment,” his wife’s quick, earnest voice called presently.

He started at the sight in the kitchen:—A young, slender woman, garments torn and frozen, one shoe gone, the stocking torn from a cut and bleeding foot, lay back in a deadly swoon on two chairs. There was the despised race in her face, yet none could help feeling its mournful, pathetic beauty; its stony sharpness and cold, fixed aspect struck a chill over him. Mrs. Bird and their only colored domestic, old Aunt Dinah, were busy with restoratives; and old Cudjoe had got the boy on his knee, chafing his cold feet.

“Sure, now, if she an’t a sight to behold!” said Dinah, compassionately. “‘Pears like ’t was the heat made her faint. She was tol’able peart when she cum in, and asked if she couldn’t warm herself a spell; and I was jest a-askin’ her where she cum from, and she fainted right down.”

“Poor creature!” said Mrs. Bird, as the woman slowly unclosed large, dark eyes and looked vacantly about; then, with agony crossing her face, sprang up.

“O, my Harry! Have they got him?”

The boy leaped from Cudjoe’s knee and ran to her. “O, he’s here! he’s here!” she cried, gathering him.

“O, ma’am!” she said wildly to Mrs. Bird, “do protect us! don’t let them get him!”

“Nobody shall hurt you here, poor woman,” said Mrs. Bird, encouragingly. “You are safe; don’t be afraid.”

“God bless you!” said the woman, covering her face and sobbing, while the boy tried to climb into her lap. She would not yield him to any hand; even in sleep, when, at last, a bed was made by the fire and she sank down with him, her arm encircled him with an unrelaxing clasp, as if scarcely in dreams would she be beguiled of her hold.

Mr. and Mrs. Bird returned to the parlor and, strange as it may appear, made no reference to their previous conversation. She busied herself with her knitting; he pretended to read.

“I wonder who and what she is!” said Mr. Bird at last.

“When she wakes up and feels a little rested, we will see,” said Mrs. Bird.

“I say, wife!”

“Well, dear!”

“She couldn’t wear one of your gowns, could she, by any letting down, or such matter? She seems to be rather larger than you are.”

Mrs. Bird smiled. “We’ll see.”

“And there’s that old bombazin cloak you keep to put over me in my naps; you might as well give her that,—she needs clothes.”

Dinah came to say the woman was awake and wished to see “Missis.”

The woman sat by the fire, looking into the blaze with a calm, heart-broken expression.

“Did you want me?” said Mrs. Bird, gently. “I hope you feel better now, poor woman!”

A shivering sigh was the only answer; then the dark eyes were lifted with such a forlorn, imploring expression that tears came into the little woman’s eyes.

“You needn’t be afraid of anything; we are friends here. Tell me where you came from, and what you want,” said she.

“I came from Kentucky,” said the woman.

“When?” asked Mr. Bird.

“Tonight.”

“How did you come?”

“I crossed on the ice.”

“Crossed on the ice!” all exclaimed.

“Yes. God helping me, I crossed on the ice; for they were behind me—right behind—and there was no other way!”

“Law, Missis,” said Cudjoe, “the ice is all in broken-up blocks, a swingin’ and a teterin’ up and down in the water!”

“I know it was—I know it!” she cried; “but I did it! I could but die, if I didn’t. The Lord helped me; nobody knows how much the Lord can help ’em, till they try.”

“Were you a slave?” said Mr. Bird.

“Yes, sir; I belonged to a man in Kentucky.”

“Was he unkind?”

“No, sir; he was a good master.”

“And your mistress?”

“No, sir—no! my mistress was always good to me.”

“What could induce you to leave a good home, then, and run away, and go through such dangers?”

The woman looked at Mrs. Bird and saw she was in deep mourning.

“Ma’am,” she said suddenly, “have you ever lost a child?”

Mrs. Bird burst into tears. “Why do you ask that? I have lost a little one.”

“Then you will feel for me. I have lost two, one after another,—left ’em buried there when I came away; and I had only this one left. I never slept a night without him; he was all I had. And, ma’am, they were going to take him away from me,—to sell him,—sell him down south, ma’am, to go all alone,—a baby that had never been away from his mother in his life! I couldn’t stand it, ma’am. I knew I never should be good for anything, if they did; and when I knew the papers were signed, and he was sold, I took him and came off in the night; and they chased me,—the man that bought him, and some of Mas’r’s folks,—and they were coming right behind me, and I heard ’em. I jumped right on to the ice; and how I got across, I don’t know,—but, first I knew, a man was helping me up the bank.”

“How came you to tell me you had a kind master?” the senator burst out, resolutely gulping down a rising in his throat.

“Because he was a kind master; I’ll say that of him, any way;—and my mistress was kind; but they couldn’t help themselves. They were owing money; and there was some way, I can’t tell how, that a man had a hold on them, and they were obliged to give him his will. I listened, and heard him telling mistress that, and she begging and pleading for me,—and he told her he couldn’t help himself, and that the papers were all drawn;—and then it was I took him and left my home, and came away.”

“Have you no husband?”

“Yes, but he belongs to another man. His master is real hard to him, and won’t let him come to see me, hardly ever; and he threatens to sell him down south;—it’s like I’ll never see him again!”

“And where do you mean to go, my poor woman?” said Mrs. Bird.

“To Canada, if I only knew where that was. Is it very far off, is Canada?” she said, with a simple, confiding air.

“Much further than you think, poor child!” said Mrs. Bird. “But we will try to think what can be done for you. Dinah, make her up a bed in your own room, close by the kitchen, and I’ll think what to do in the morning. Meanwhile, never fear; put your trust in God; He will protect you.”

Back in the parlor, Mrs. Bird sat swaying thoughtfully in her little rocker, while the senator strode and grumbled, “Pish! pshaw! confounded awkward business!” At length, striding up, he said,

“I say, wife, she’ll have to get away from here, this very night. That fellow will be down on the scent bright and early tomorrow morning: if ’t was only the woman, she could lie quiet till it’s over; but that little chap can’t be kept still by a troop of horse and foot. A pretty kettle of fish it would be for me to be caught with them both here, just now! No; they’ll have to be got off tonight.”

“Tonight! How is it possible?—where to?”

“Well, I know pretty well where to,” said the senator, drawing on his boots, and embracing his knee with both hands, going off in deep meditation. “There’s my old client, Van Trompe, come over from Kentucky, set all his slaves free; he’s bought a place seven miles up the creek, back in the woods, where nobody goes unless they go on purpose. There she’d be safe enough; but nobody could drive a carriage there tonight but me.”

“Why not? Cudjoe is an excellent driver.”

“Ay, ay; but the creek has to be crossed twice; the second crossing is dangerous unless one knows it as I do. Cudjoe must put in the horses as quietly as may be, about twelve o’clock, and I’ll take her over; and then, to give color to the matter, he must carry me on to the next tavern to take the stage for Columbus, that comes by about three or four, and so it will look as if I’d had the carriage only for that. I shall feel rather cheap there, after all that’s been said and done; but, hang it, I can’t help it!”

“Your heart is better than your head, in this case, John,” said the wife, laying her little white hand on his. “Could I ever have loved you, had I not known you better than you know yourself?”

At the door he stopped, hesitated, and came back.

“Mary, I don’t know how you’d feel about it, but there’s that drawer full of things—of—of—poor little Henry’s.”

She took the candle into the little bed-room, unlocked the drawer, and paused. Ah! mother, has there never been in your house a drawer, the opening of which was like the opening again of a little grave? There were little coats, piles of aprons, rows of small stockings, even a pair of little shoes rubbed at the toes; a toy horse and wagon, a top, a ball,—memorials gathered with many a tear. She sat down and wept until the tears fell into the drawer; then, with nervous haste, she selected the plainest and most substantial articles, and gathered them in a bundle.

“Mamma,” said one of the boys, gently, “you going to give away those things?”

“My dear boys,” she said softly, “if our dear, loving little Henry looks down from heaven, he would be glad to have us do this. I could not give them to any common person,—to anybody that was happy; but I give them to a mother more heart-broken than I am; and I hope God will send His blessings with them!”

From a wardrobe she took a plain, serviceable dress or two, and, with needle, scissors, and thimble, quietly commenced the “letting down” process, and continued till the old clock struck twelve and wheels rattled at the door.

“Mary,” said her husband, “you must wake her up now; we must be off.”

Mrs. Bird hastily deposited the articles in a small plain trunk; Mr. Bird took it to the carriage; and the woman—Eliza, the fugitive mother—appeared at the door, arrayed in cloak, bonnet, and shawl that had belonged to her benefactress, her child in her arms. Mr. Bird hurried her in; Mrs. Bird pressed out after. Eliza leaned from the carriage and put out her hand,—a hand as soft and beautiful as that given in return. She fixed large, dark eyes, full of earnest meaning, on Mrs. Bird’s face; her lips moved, once or twice,—there was no sound; she pointed upward, with a look never to be forgotten, fell back, and covered her face. The door shut; the carriage drove on.

What a situation, now, for a patriotic senator, that had been all the week before spurring up the legislature of his state to pass more stringent resolutions against escaping fugitives, their harborers and abettors! He had been as bold as a lion about it; the image of a fugitive had been but the letters that spelt the word—or, at the most, a little newspaper picture of a man with a stick and bundle, with “Ran away from the subscriber” under it. The magic of the real presence of distress,—the imploring human eye, the frail, trembling hand, the despairing appeal of helpless agony,—these he had never tried. He had never thought a fugitive might be a hapless mother, a defenceless child, like that one now wearing his lost boy’s little well-known cap. And as our poor senator was not stone or steel,—as he was a man, and a downright noble-hearted one, too,—he was, as everybody must see, in a sad case for his patriotism.

Be that as it may, if our good senator was a political sinner, he was in a fair way to expiate it by his night’s penance. There had been long rainy weather; the soft, rich earth of Ohio is admirably suited to the manufacture of mud; and the road was an Ohio railroad of the good old times,—a corduroy causeway of round rough logs, with chasms and ruts of black mud between. Over such a road our senator went stumbling along, making moral reflections as continuously as might be,—the carriage proceeding thus,—bump! bump! bump! slush!—down in the mud!—the senator, woman, and child reversed in a twinkling against the down-hill window; carriage sticks fast; Cudjoe outside musters the horses; after pullings and twitchings, just as patience fails, the carriage rights with a bounce,—two front wheels down into another abyss, and all tumble on the front seat,—the senator’s hat jammed over eyes and nose; child cries; Cudjoe cracks the whip; carriage springs up with another bounce,—down go the hind wheels; elbows encounter bonnet; both her feet are jammed into his hat, which flies off; then a pause,—the “slough” is passed; horses pant; the senator finds his hat; the woman straightens her bonnet and hushes her child; they brace themselves anew.

At last, with a square plunge that sets all on their feet and then down again with incredible quickness, the carriage stops. After much commotion, Cudjoe appears.

“Please, sir, it’s powerful bad spot, this ’yer. I don’t know how we’s to get clar out. I’m a thinkin’ we’ll have to be a gettin’ rails.”

The senator steps out gingerly; down goes one foot immeasurably; he tries to pull it up, loses balance, tumbles over into the mud, and is fished out by Cudjoe. But we forbear, out of sympathy to our readers’ bones.

It was full late in the night when the carriage emerged, dripping and bespattered, out of the creek, and stood at the door of a large farmhouse. It took perseverance to arouse the inmates; at last the proprietor appeared and undid the door,—a great, tall, bristling Orson of a fellow, full six feet and some inches in his stockings, in a red flannel hunting-shirt; a heavy mat of sandy hair and a beard of some days, giving him an appearance not prepossessing. He stood, candle aloft, blinking on our travellers with a mystified expression that was truly ludicrous.

“Are you the man that will shelter a poor woman and child from slave-catchers?” said the senator, explicitly.

“I rather think I am,” said honest John Van Trompe, with emphasis.

“I thought so,” said the senator.

“If there’s anybody comes,” said the good man, stretching his tall form, “why here I’m ready for him: and I’ve got seven sons, each six foot high, and they’ll be ready for ’em. Give our respects to ’em. Tell ’em it’s no matter how soon they call,—make no kinder difference to us,” said John, running his fingers through the shock that thatched his head, and bursting into a great laugh.

Weary, jaded, spiritless, Eliza dragged herself up to the door, with her child lying in a heavy sleep on her arm. The rough man held the candle to her face; uttering a compassionate grunt, he opened a small bed-room off the kitchen, set a light upon the table, and addressed her.

“Now, I say, gal, you needn’t be a bit afeard, let who will come here. I’m up to all that sort o’ thing,” he said, pointing to two or three goodly rifles over the mantel; “and most people that know me know that ’t wouldn’t be healthy to try to get anybody out o’ my house when I’m agin it. So now you jist go to sleep, as quiet as if yer mother was a rockin’ ye,” said he, shutting the door.

“Why, this is an uncommon handsome un,” he said to the senator. “Ah, well; handsome uns has the greatest cause to run, sometimes, if they has any kind o’ feelin’, such as decent women should. I know all about that.”

The senator briefly explained Eliza’s history.

“O! ou! aw! now, I want to know?” said the good man, pitifully; “sho! now sho! That’s natur now, poor crittur! hunted down now like a deer,—hunted down, jest for havin’ natural feelin’s, and doin’ what no kind o’ mother could help a doin’! I tell ye what, these yer things make me come the nighest to swearin’, now, o’ most anything,” said honest John, wiping his eyes with the back of a great, freckled hand. “I tell yer what, stranger, it was years and years before I’d jine the church, ’cause the ministers round in our parts used to preach that the Bible went in for these ’ere cuttin’s up,—and I couldn’t be up to ’em with their Greek and Hebrew, and so I took up agin ’em, Bible and all. I never jined till I found a minister that was up to ’em all in Greek and all that, and he said right the contrary; and then I took right hold, and jined,—fact,” said John, uncorking some very frisky bottled cider, which he presented.

“Ye’d better jest put up here, now, till daylight,” said he, heartily; “I’ll call up the old woman, and have a bed ready for you in no time.”

“Thank you, my good friend,” said the senator, “I must be along, to take the night stage for Columbus.”

“Ah! well, then, if you must, I’ll go a piece with you, and show you a cross road that’ll take you there better than the road you came on. That road’s mighty bad.”

Honest John’s history might be told in a word. Once a considerable land-owner and slave-owner in Kentucky, having “nothing of the bear about him but the skin,” and a great, honest, just heart equal to his frame, he had for years watched, with repressed uneasiness, a system equally bad for oppressor and oppressed. At last his heart swelled too big for its bonds; so he took his pocket-book, crossed into Ohio, bought a quarter township of good land, made out free papers for all his people—men, women, and children—packed them in wagons, sent them off to settle, and then turned up the creek to a snug farm, to enjoy his conscience and reflections.

He equipped himself now with a lantern and guided the senator’s carriage toward a road that ran down in a hollow, back of his dwelling. When they parted, the senator put into his hand a ten-dollar bill.

“It’s for her,” he said, briefly.

“Ay, ay,” said John, with equal conciseness.

They shook hands, and parted.

The February morning looked gray and drizzling into Uncle Tom’s cabin. The little table stood before the fire under an ironing-cloth; a coarse but clean shirt or two hung on a chair-back; and Aunt Chloe, wiping tears with the back of her hand, rubbed and ironed every fold with scrupulous exactness.

Tom sat by with his Testament open on his knee, head leaning on his hand. It was early; the children still slept in their rude trundle-bed. He rose softly to look at them.

“It’s the last time,” he said.

Aunt Chloe did not answer; she ironed the same seam over and over, set the iron down with a plunge, then sat and lifted up her voice and wept.

“S’pose we must be resigned; but, oh Lord! how ken I? If I know’d anything whar you’s goin’, or how they’d sarve you! Missis says she’ll try and ’deem ye in a year or two; but Lor! nobody never comes up that goes down thar! They kills ’em! I’ve hearn ’em tell how dey works ’em up on dem ar plantations.”

“There’ll be the same God there, Chloe, that there is here.”

“Well,” said Aunt Chloe, “s’pose dere will; but de Lord lets drefful things happen, sometimes. I don’t seem to get no comfort dat way.”

“I’m in the Lord’s hands,” said Tom; “nothin’ can go no furder than he lets it;—and thar’s one thing I can thank him for. It’s me that’s sold and going down, and not you nur the chil’en. Here you’re safe;—what comes will come only on me; and the Lord, he’ll help me,—I know he will.”

“Marcies!” said Aunt Chloe, when he tried to count them; “don’t see no marcy in’t! ’tan’t right! Mas’r never ought ter left it so ye could be took for his debts. Ye’ve arnt him all he gets for ye, twice over. He owed ye yer freedom. Sich a faithful crittur as ye’ve been,—and allers sot his business ’fore yer own! Them as sells heart’s love and heart’s blood, to get out thar scrapes, de Lord’ll be up to ’em!”

“Chloe! if ye love me, ye won’t talk so, when perhaps jest the last time we’ll ever have together! And it goes agin me to hear one word agin Mas’r. Wan’t he put in my arms a baby?—it’s natur I should think a heap of him. Mas’rs is used to havin’ all these yer things done for ’em; nat’lly they don’t think so much on ’t. Set him ’longside other Mas’rs—who’s had the treatment I’ve had? And he never would a let this come on me, if he could a seed it aforehand. I know he wouldn’t.”

“Wal, any way, thar’s wrong about it somewhar,” said Aunt Chloe, sturdy in justice. “I can’t make out whar ’t is, but thar’s wrong somewhar, I’m clar o’ that.”

“Yer ought ter look up to the Lord above—he’s above all—thar don’t a sparrow fall without him.”

“It don’t seem to comfort me, but I spect it orter,” she said; then, forcing herself to bustle, “I’ll jes wet up de corn-cake, and get ye one good breakfast, ’cause nobody knows when you’ll get another.”

To those sold south, every home-love was a chain of pain. “Down river” was told from childhood as the last severity; ignorance peopled it with horrors; and men and women, patient and timid by nature, were nerved to all perils rather than go.

The simple morning meal smoked on the table; Mrs. Shelby had excused Chloe from the great house. She had killed her choicest chicken, baked corn-cake to Tom’s very taste, and brought out long-saved preserves.

“Lor, Pete,” cried Mose, “han’t we got a buster of a breakfast!” and caught at a bit of chicken.

Aunt Chloe boxed his ear. “Thar now! crowin’ over de last breakfast yer poor daddy’s gwine to have to home!”

“O, Chloe!” said Tom, gently.

“Wal, I can’t help it,” she sobbed, hiding her face. “I’s so tossed about, it makes me act ugly.”

The baby clamored to be taken; she wiped her eyes, caught the child up.

“Now I’s done, I hope,—now do eat somethin’. This yer’s my nicest chicken. Thar, boys, ye shall have some, poor critturs! Yer mammy’s been cross to yer.”

After breakfast she put up his clothes.

“Jest like as not, he’ll take ’em all away. Yer flannels for rhumatis is in this corner; be careful, ’cause there won’t nobody make ye no more. Here’s yer old shirts; these yer is new ones. I toed off these yer stockings last night, and put de ball in ’em to mend with. But Lor! who’ll ever mend for ye?” She laid her head on the box-side and sobbed. “To think on ’t! no crittur to do for ye, sick or well! I don’t railly think I ought ter be good now!”

The boys, having eaten all there was, began to whimper; Tom set the baby to crowing and scratching his face, taking what sport he might of her little hands.

“Ay, crow away, poor crittur!” said Chloe. “Ye’ll have to come to it, too! ye’ll live to see yer husband sold, or mebbe be sold yerself; and these yer boys, they’s to be sold, I s’pose, too, when dey gets good for somethin’!”

“Thar’s Missis a-comin’ in!” cried one.

Mrs. Shelby entered; Chloe set a chair for her, gruff and crusty. The lady, pale and anxious, looked on the silent group, sat, and, covering her face, sobbed.

“Lor, now, Missis, don’t—don’t!” burst out Chloe; and for a few moments they all wept together; and in those tears the high and the lowly melted all bitterness.

“My good fellow,” said Mrs. Shelby, “I can’t give you anything to do you any good. If I give you money, it will only be taken from you. But I tell you solemnly, before God, I will keep trace of you, and bring you back as soon as I can command the money;—and, till then, trust in God!”

The boys cried out that Mas’r Haley was coming. An unceremonious kick pushed open the door. Haley stood there, ill-humored from a hard ride and last night’s ill success.

“Come,” said he, “ye nigger, ye’r ready? Servant, ma’am!” He lifted his hat to the lady.

Chloe shut and corded the box, then stood looking at the trader, her tears turned to sparks. Tom rose meekly, shouldered the box. His wife took the baby to go with him to the wagon; the children trailed behind.

Mrs. Shelby detained the trader on the verandah a moment, speaking earnestly; meanwhile all hands from the place gathered about the wagon to bid farewell to their old associate. Tom had been head servant and Christian teacher; honest grief showed most among the women.

“Why, Chloe, you bar it better ’n we do!” said one, who had wept freely, seeing Chloe’s gloomy calm.

“I’s done my tears!” she said, looking grimly at the trader. “I does not feel to cry ’fore dat ar old limb, no how!”

“Get in!” said Haley, striding through the scowling ring. He drew a heavy pair of shackles from under the seat and made them fast around each ankle.

A smothered groan ran through the circle.

“Mr. Haley,” called Mrs. Shelby from the verandah, “that precaution is entirely unnecessary.”

“Don’ know, ma’am; I’ve lost one five hundred dollars from this yer place, and I can’t afford to run no more risks.”

“What else could she spect on him?” muttered Chloe, while the boys clung to her gown, sobbing.

“I’m sorry,” said Tom, “that Mas’r George happened to be away. Give my love to Mas’r George.”

Haley whipped up; Tom’s steady, mournful look clung to the old place till it was lost.

Mr. Shelby was not at home. He had sold Tom under pressing necessity, and, at first, felt relief; then his wife’s expostulations and Tom’s manly disinterestedness made his feelings sore. He told himself he had a right—that everybody did it; yet he could not satisfy his heart, and he had gone up the country on business to be away till all was over.

They rattled along the open pike; after a mile Haley drew up at a blacksmith’s shop, and, taking in a pair of handcuffs, asked a little alteration.

“These yer’s a little too small for his build,” said Haley.

“Lor! now, if thar an’t Shelby’s Tom. He han’t sold him, now?” said the smith.

“Yes, he has,” said Haley.

“Now, ye don’t! Reely—who’d a thought it! Why, ye needn’t go to fetterin’ him this yer way. He’s the faithfullest, best crittur—”

“Yes, yes; but your good fellers is the very critturs to want ter run off. Them stupid ones as doesn’t care whar they go, and shifless, drunken ones, they’ll stick by; but these yer prime fellers, they hates it like sin. No way but to fetter ’em; got legs,—they’ll use ’em.”

“Well,” said the smith, hunting his tools, “them plantations down thar an’t jest the place a Kentuck nigger wants to go. They dies thar tol’able fast, don’t they?”

“Wal, yes, tol’able fast, ther dying is; what with the ’climating and one thing and another, they dies so as to keep the market up brisk,” said Haley.

“Wal, now, a feller can’t help thinkin’ it’s a mighty pity to have a likely, quiet feller, as good un as Tom, go down to be fairly ground up on one o’ them sugar plantations.”

“Wal, he’s got a fa’r chance. I promised to do well by him. I’ll get him in house-servant in some good old family; and if he stands the fever and ’climating, he’ll have a berth good as any nigger ought ter ask for.”

“He leaves his wife and chil’en up here, s’pose?”

“Yes; but he’ll get another thar. Lord, thar’s women enough everywhar,” said Haley.

Tom sat mournfully outside. Suddenly he heard the click of hoofs; young Master George sprang into the wagon, threw his arms round his neck, sobbing and scolding.

“I declare, it’s real mean! I don’t care what they say! It’s a nasty, mean shame! If I was a man, they shouldn’t do it,—they should not, so!” cried George.

“O! Mas’r George! this does me good!” said Tom. “I couldn’t bar to go off without seein’ ye!” He moved his fettered feet; George saw.

“What a shame!” he cried. “I’ll knock that old fellow down—I will!”

“No you won’t, Mas’r George; and you mustn’t talk so loud. It won’t help me any, to anger him.”

“Well, I won’t, for your sake; but only to think of it—isn’t it a shame? They never sent for me; and, if it hadn’t been for Tom Lincon, I shouldn’t have heard it. I tell you, I blew ’em up well, all of ’em, at home!”

“That ar wasn’t right, I’m ’feard, Mas’r George.”

“Can’t help it! I say it’s a shame! Look here, Uncle Tom,” he added low, turning his back to the shop, “I’ve brought you my dollar!”

“O! I couldn’t think o’ takin’ on ’t, Mas’r George, no ways in the world!” said Tom, moved.

“But you shall take it! I told Aunt Chloe I’d do it; and she advised me to make a hole and put a string through, so you could hang it round your neck, and keep it out of sight; else this mean scamp would take it away. I tell ye, Tom, I want to blow him up! it would do me good!”

“No, don’t, Mas’r George, for it won’t do me any good.”

“Well, I won’t, for your sake,” said George, tying the dollar and buttoning Tom’s coat over it. “Keep it; and remember, every time you see it, that I’ll come down after you, and bring you back. Aunt Chloe and I have been talking about it. I’ll tease father’s life out, if he don’t do it.”

“O! Mas’r George, ye mustn’t talk so ’bout yer father!”

“Lor, Uncle Tom, I don’t mean anything bad.”

“And now, Mas’r George,” said Tom, “ye must be a good boy; ’member how many hearts is sot on ye. Al’ays keep close to yer mother. Don’t be gettin’ into them foolish ways boys has o’ gettin’ too big to mind their mothers. The Lord gives good many things twice over; but he don’t give ye a mother but once. Ye’ll never see sich another woman, Mas’r George, if ye live to be a hundred years old. So, hold on to her, and grow up, and be a comfort to her,—thar’s my own good boy,—you will now, won’t ye?”

“Yes, I will, Uncle Tom,” said George, seriously.

“And be careful of yer speaking, Mas’r George. Young boys, when they comes to your age, is wilful, sometimes; but real gentlemen never lets fall words that isn’t ’spectful to thar parents. Ye an’t ’fended, Mas’r George?”

“No, indeed; you always did give me good advice.”

“I’s older,” said Tom, stroking the curly head with a hand as gentle as a woman’s, “and I sees all that’s bound up in you. O, Mas’r George, you has everything,—l’arnin’, privileges, readin’, writin’,—and you’ll grow up a great, learned, good man; and all the people on the place, and your mother and father, ’ll be so proud on ye! Be a good Mas’r, like yer father; and be a Christian, like yer mother. ’Member yer Creator in the days o’ yer youth, Mas’r George.”

“I’ll be real good, Uncle Tom,” said George. “I’m going to be a first-rater; and don’t you be discouraged. I’ll have you back yet. I’ll build our house all over, and you shall have a room for a parlor with a carpet on it, when I’m a man. O, you’ll have good times yet!”

Haley came to the door with the handcuffs.

“Look here, now, Mister,” said George, drawing himself up as he got out, “I shall let father and mother know how you treat Uncle Tom!”

“You’re welcome,” said the trader.

“I should think you’d be ashamed to spend all your life buying men and women, and chaining them, like cattle! I should think you’d feel mean!”

“So long as your grand folks wants to buy men and women, I’m as good as they is,” said Haley; “‘tan’t any meaner sellin’ on ’em than ’t is buyin’!”

“I’ll never do either, when I’m a man,” said George. “I’m ashamed, this day, that I’m a Kentuckian. I always was proud of it before.”

“Well, good-by, Uncle Tom; keep a stiff upper lip.”

“Good-by, Mas’r George,” said Tom, looking fondly at him. “God Almighty bless you! Ah! Kentucky han’t got many like you!”

Away went the boy; Tom looked till the clatter of hoofs died—the last sound or sight of home. Over his heart there seemed a warm spot where the young hands had hung the precious dollar. He put his hand and held it close.

“Now, I tell ye what, Tom,” said Haley, throwing in the handcuffs, “I mean to start fa’r with ye, as I gen’ally do with my niggers. You treat me fa’r, and I’ll treat you fa’r; I an’t never hard on my niggers. If niggers is quiet, and don’t try to get off, they has good times with me; and if they don’t, why, it’s thar fault, and not mine.”

Tom assured him he had no thought of running; the assurance sat superfluous on a man with iron on his feet. But Haley, from habit, commenced his relations with such exhortations, to inspire cheerfulness, as he deemed, and prevent unpleasant scenes.

And here, for the present, we take our leave of Tom, to pursue the fortunes of other characters in our story.

In Which Property Gets into an Improper State of Mind

It was late in a drizzly afternoon when a traveler alighted at the door of a small country hotel, in the village of N——, in Kentucky. Within, a miscellany of storm-drifted company lounged about the barroom; rifles stood in the corner; shot-pouches and game-bags lay with hunting-dogs and little negroes rolled together in the angles. At each end of the wide fireplace a long-legged gentleman tipped back in his chair, hat on head, muddy heels enthroned upon the mantel, in that posture of meditation peculiar to western taverns.

Mine host, great of stature and good nature, with a shock of hair and a great hat above that, presided behind the bar. In truth, every man in the place bore on his head this emblem of sovereignty—felt hats rakishly tipped for your jolly fellows; jammed down for your hard characters; set far back for the wide-awake; and shaken about by the careless—quite a Shakespearean study. Divers negroes, in very free-and-easy pantaloons and scant of shirt, scuttled to and fro, producing less result than zealous stir, while a rollicking fire went rejoicingly up the great chimney, the outer door and every window set open to a raw damp air, the calico curtain flapping.

Your Kentuckian, descendant of mighty hunters, keeps his house as camp; wears his hat, tumbles himself about, puts heels on mantels as his fathers on logs; keeps doors and windows open winter and summer, calls everybody "stranger," and is the frankest, easiest, most jovial creature living.

Into such company our traveller came—a short, thick-set man, carefully dressed, round and good-natured of countenance, but fussy and particular in his ways. He brought in valise and umbrella with his own hands, repulsed officious offers, nestled both under the chair nearest the fire, and sat down, looking apprehensively at the gentleman whose artillery of tobacco-juice played right and left.

"I say, stranger, how are ye?" said that worthy, firing an honorary salute.

"Well, I reckon," returned the little man, dodging the threatening honor.

"Any news?"

"Not that I know of."

"Chaw?" said the first speaker, offering a brotherly slice of tobacco.

"No, thank ye—it don't agree with me," said the new-comer, edging off.

The long-sided brother good-naturedly slewed his battery to bear on a fire-iron, and proceeded to storm it with zeal sufficient to take a city. The old gentleman started at every discharge; but his attention was soon drawn to a group around a large handbill.

"Nigger advertised!" said one.

Mr. Wilson—for such was the old gentleman's name—erected his spectacles, and read, slowly, as if studying:

"Ran away from the subscriber, my mulatto boy, George. Said George six feet in height, a very light mulatto, brown curly hair; is very intelligent, speaks handsomely, can read and write, will probably try to pass for a white man, is deeply scarred on his back and shoulders, has been branded in his right hand with the letter H.
    "I will give four hundred dollars for him alive, and the same sum
    for satisfactory proof that he has been _killed."

The long-legged veteran took down his length, strode up, and, with deliberation, discharged a full mouthful on the paper.

"There's my mind upon that!" said he, and sat down again.

"Why, now, stranger, what's that for?" asked mine host.

"I'd do it all the same to the writer o' that ar' paper, if he was here," said the long man, resuming his tobacco. "Any man that owns a boy like that, and can't find no better way o' treatin' on him, _deserves_ to lose him. Such papers is a shame to Kentucky; that's my mind right out, if anybody wants to know!"

"Well, now, that's a fact," said the landlord, entering it in his book as if it were a charge.

"I've got a gang o' boys," continued the long man, "and I jest tells 'em—'Boys,' says I,—'_run_ now! dig! put! jest when ye want to! I never shall come to look arter ye!' That's the way I keeps mine. Let 'em know they're free to run any time, and it jest breaks up their wantin' to. More 'n all, I've got free papers recorded for 'em, in case I gets keeled up; and I tell ye, stranger, there an't a fellow in our parts gets more out o' his niggers than I do. Treat 'em like dogs, and you'll have dogs' works and dogs' actions. Treat 'em like men, and you'll have men's works." And, in warmth, he fired a perfect feu de joie at the fireplace.

"I think you're altogether right, friend," said Mr. Wilson. "And this boy described here _is_ a fine fellow—no mistake. He worked for me half-a-dozen years in my bagging-factory—my best hand. He invented a machine for cleaning hemp; a valuable affair; gone into use in several factories. His master holds the patent."

"I'll warrant ye," said the drover, "holds it, makes money out on 't, and then turns round and brands the boy's right hand! If I had a fair chance, I'd mark _him_ so he'd carry it one while."

"These yer knowin' boys is allers aggravatin' and sarcy," said a coarse fellow, entrenched in his own obtuseness; "that's why they gets cut up and marked. If they behaved, they wouldn't."

"That is to say," returned the drover, dryly, "the Lord made 'em men, and it's a hard squeeze gettin' 'em down into beasts."

"Bright niggers isn't no kind o' 'vantage," persisted the other; "what's the use o' talents if you can't get the use on 'em? All they make on 't is to get round ye. I jest sold 'em down river. I knew I'd got to lose 'em, first or last."

"Better send orders up to the Lord to make ye a set, and leave out their souls entirely," said the drover.

Here a small one-horse buggy drew up. A tall, gentlemanly man, dark and Spanish-looking, with glossy black curls, aquiline nose, straight thin lips, and admirable contour, entered, a colored servant driving. He sauntered to the bar with hat in hand, gave his name—Henry Butler, Oaklands, Shelby County—then turned, with indifference, to read the handbill.

"Jim," he said to his man, "seems to me we met a boy something like this, up at Beman's, didn't we?"

"Yes, Mas'r," said the servant, "only I an't sure 'bout the hand."

"Well, I didn't look, of course," said the stranger, yawning; and then requested a private apartment.

The landlord was all obsequious. A covey of negroes, old and young, bustled and tumbled over each other to get Mas'r's room ready; the new-comer seated himself easily, and conversed with the man next him.

Mr. Wilson had from the first regarded the stranger with disturbed curiosity. He had seen him somewhere. When the man spoke, or smiled, he started, fixed his eyes, and withdrew them as the bright black eyes met his with unconcern. At last a flash of recollection sent alarm through his good old face; the stranger rose and came to him.

"Mr. Wilson, I think," said he, extending his hand. "I beg your pardon—I didn't recollect you before. I see you remember me,—Mr. Butler, of Oaklands, Shelby County."

"Ye—yes—yes, sir," said Mr. Wilson, like one in a dream.

A negro boy announced that Mas'r's room was ready.

"Jim, see to the trunks," said the gentleman, negligently. Then to Mr. Wilson: "I should like a few moments' business in my room, if you please."

Mr. Wilson followed as a sleep-walker. In the large upper chamber, when the servants had done and gone, the young man locked the door, put the key in his pocket, folded his arms, and faced him.

"George!" said Mr. Wilson.

"Yes—George," said the young man, smiling.

"I couldn't have thought it!"

"I am pretty well disguised, I fancy. A little walnut-bark for my yellow skin, dyed hair, and—behold—no answer to the advertisement."

"O, George! this is a dangerous game. I could not have advised you."

"I can do it on my own responsibility," returned George, with the same proud smile.

He was, by his father's side, of white descent; from one of Kentucky's proudest families he had inherited fine European features and a high, indomitable spirit. From his mother, one of those unfortunates marked by beauty to be the slave of passion, he had received only a slight mulatto tinge, redeemed by a rich dark eye. Grace of movement and gentlemanly manners sat naturally upon him; a change of tint and hair had metamorphosed him into the Spanish-looking fellow who now played boldly the gentleman travelling with his domestic.

Mr. Wilson, good-natured, fidgety, cautious, shuffled up and down, much "tumbled up and down in his mind," divided between helping George and maintaining law and order.

"Well, George, I s'pose you're running away—leaving your lawful master—(I don't wonder)—but I'm sorry, George—decidedly—I think I must say that—it's my duty."

"Why are you sorry, sir?" said George, calmly.

"Why, to see you setting yourself in opposition to the laws of your country."

"_My_ country!" said George, with strong, bitter emphasis. "What country have I but the grave,—and I wish to God I were laid there!"

"Why, no—no—this way of talking is wicked—unscriptural. You've got a hard master—reprehensible, I can't defend him. But you know the angel bade Hagar return and submit; and the apostle sent back Onesimus to his master."

"Don't quote Bible at me that way, Mr. Wilson," said George, his eye flashing. "Don't! My wife is a Christian, and I mean to be, if ever I get where I can; but to quote Bible to a fellow in my circumstances is enough to make him give it up. I appeal to God Almighty. I'm willing to go with the case to Him, and ask if I do wrong to seek my freedom."

"These feelings are natural," said the good man, blowing his nose. "Yes; but it is my duty not to encourage 'em. The apostle says, 'Let every one abide in the condition wherein he is called.' We must submit to Providence, George,—don't you see?"

George drew his head back, folded his arms over his broad breast, a bitter smile on his lips.

"I wonder, Mr. Wilson, if the Indians should come and take you a prisoner from your wife and children, and keep you all your life hoeing corn for them, if you'd think it your duty to abide in the condition wherein you were called. I rather think the first stray horse would look like an indication of Providence—shouldn't you?"

The little old gentleman stared with both eyes; and, having the sense of saying nothing where nothing could be said, stroked his umbrella and patted its creases, and exhorted in a general way.
