"You see, George, I've always stood your friend; and whatever I've said, I've said for your good. You're running an awful risk. You can't hope to carry it out. If you're taken, it will be worse than ever; they'll abuse you, half kill you, and sell you down river."

"I know all this," said George. "I _do_ run a risk; but—" He threw open his overcoat, and showed two pistols and a bowie-knife. "There! I'm ready for 'em! Down south I never _will_ go. No! if it comes to that, I can earn myself at least six feet of free soil,—the first and last I shall ever own in Kentucky!"

"Why, George, this state of mind is awful; really desperate. Going to break the laws of your country!"

"My country, again! You have a country; but what country have _I_, or any one like me, born of slave mothers? What laws are there for us? We don't make them,—we don't consent to them; all they do is to crush us, and keep us down. Haven't I heard your Fourth-of-July speeches? Don't you tell us, once a year, that governments derive their just powers from the consent of the governed? Can't a fellow _think_ that hears such things? Can't he put this and that together, and see what it comes to?"

Mr. Wilson's mind might be fairly represented by a bale of cotton—downy, soft, benevolently fuzzy and confused. He pitied George with all his heart, had a dim perception of the feeling that agitated him; but deemed it duty to go on talking _good_ to him, with infinite pertinacity.

"George, this is bad. As a friend, you'd better not meddle with such notions; bad for boys in your condition,—very," and he sat and nervously chewed the umbrella-handle.

"See here, now," said George, drawing a chair resolutely before him. "Look at me. Don't I sit here every way just as much a man as you are? Look at my face—my hands—my body." He drew himself up proudly. "Why am I _not_ a man, as much as anybody? Hear me. I had a father—one of your Kentucky gentlemen—who didn't think enough of me to keep me from being sold with his dogs and horses to satisfy the estate when he died. I saw my mother put up at sheriff's sale with her seven children; they were sold before her eyes, one by one, all to different masters; I was the youngest. She kneeled to old Mas'r to buy her with me, that she might have one child; he kicked her away with his heavy boot. I saw him do it; and the last I heard was her moans and screams, when I was tied to his horse's neck to be carried off.

"Well, then? My master traded with a man and bought my oldest sister. She was pious—a member of the Baptist church—and handsome as my poor mother had been. I was glad at first, for I had one friend near me. I was soon sorry. Sir, I have stood at the door and heard her whipped, when every blow cut into my naked heart, and I couldn't help her; and she was whipped, sir, for wanting to live a decent Christian life, such as your laws give no slave girl a right to live; and at last I saw her chained with a trader's gang, to be sent to Orleans—sent for nothing else but that—and that's the last I know of her.

"I grew up—years and years—no father, no mother, no sister, not a living soul that cared for me more than a dog; nothing but whipping, scolding, starving. I've been so hungry I was glad to take the bones thrown to the dogs; and yet, when I was a little fellow, and lay awake whole nights and cried, it wasn't hunger nor whipping I cried for. No, sir—it was for _my mother_ and _my sisters_; because I hadn't a friend to love me on earth. I never knew peace or comfort. I never had a kind word till I came to your factory. You treated me well; encouraged me to learn to read and write, to make something of myself; and God knows how grateful I am. Then I found my wife; you've seen her—you know how beautiful she is. When I found she loved me, when I married her, I scarcely believed I was alive, I was so happy; and she is as good as she is beautiful. But now what? My master takes me from my work, my friends, all I like, and grinds me into the dirt! Why? Because, he says, I forgot who I was; he says, to teach me that I am only a nigger! After all, and last of all, he comes between me and my wife, and says I shall give her up, and live with another woman. And all this your laws give him power to do, in spite of God or man.

"Look at it! There isn't _one_ of all these things, that have broken the hearts of my mother, my sister, my wife, and myself, but your laws allow, and give every man power to do, in Kentucky, and none can say nay! Do you call these the laws of _my_ country? Sir, I haven't any country, any more than I have any father. But I'm going to have one. I don't want anything of _your_ country, except to be let alone—to go peaceably out of it; and when I get to Canada, where the laws will own me and protect me, _that_ shall be my country, and its laws I will obey. But if any man tries to stop me, let him take care; for I am desperate. I'll fight for my liberty to the last breath. Your fathers did it; if it was right for them, it is right for me!"

This, delivered partly seated, partly walking with tears, flashing eyes, and despairing gesture, was too much for the good old soul, who mopped his face with a great yellow silk handkerchief.

"Blast 'em all!" he suddenly broke out. "Haven't I always said so—the infernal old cusses! I hope I an't swearing. Well! go ahead, George, go ahead; but be careful; don't shoot anybody, unless—well—you'd _better_ not shoot, I reckon; at least, I wouldn't _hit_ anybody, you know. Where is your wife?"

"Gone—gone, with her child in her arms; gone after the north star; the Lord only knows where; and whether we ever meet on earth, no creature can tell."

"Astonishing! from such a kind family?"

"Kind families get in debt," said George, bitterly, "and the laws of _our_ country allow them to sell the child out of its mother's bosom to pay its master's debts."

"Well, well," said the honest old man, fumbling. "I s'pose I an't followin' my judgment—hang it, I _won't_ follow my judgment! Here, George." He drew out a roll of bills. "Take 'em."

"No, my kind, good sir! You have done much for me, and this might bring you into trouble. I have money enough to take me as far as I need."

"No; but you must. Money's a great help everywhere; can't have too much, if you get it honestly. Take it,—_do_ take it,—do, my boy!"

"On condition I may repay it," said George, taking it.

"And how long are you going to travel so?" said Mr. Wilson. "Not long, I hope. It's well carried on, but too bold. And this black fellow—who is he?"

"A true fellow, who went to Canada a year ago. He heard, after, that his master, in anger, whipped his poor old mother; and he has come all the way back to comfort her, and get a chance to carry her off."

"Has he got her?"

"Not yet. He has hung about, and found no chance. He goes with me as far as Ohio, to put me among friends that helped him, and then comes back."

"Dangerous, very dangerous!" said the old man.

George drew himself up, and smiled disdainfully.

"Something has brought you out wonderfully," said Mr. Wilson, eyeing him with innocent wonder. "You hold up your head, and speak and move like another man."

"Because I'm a _freeman_!" said George, proudly. "Yes, sir; I've said Mas'r for the last time to any man. _I'm free!_"

"Take care! You are not sure—you may be taken."

"All men are free and equal _in the grave_, if it comes to that," said George.

"I'm dumb-founded with your boldness! To come right here to the nearest tavern!"

"It is _so_ bold, and this tavern _so_ near, that they will never think of it. They will look for me on ahead; and you yourself wouldn't know me. Jim's master don't live in this county; he isn't known here. He's given up; nobody's looking after him; and nobody will take me up from the advertisement, I think."

"But the mark in your hand?"

George drew off his glove, and showed a newly-healed scar.

"That is a parting proof of Mr. Harris' regard," he said, scornfully. "A fortnight ago, he took it into his head to give it to me, because he believed I should try to get away. Looks interesting, doesn't it?" He drew on his glove.

"I declare, my very blood runs cold when I think of it—your condition and your risks!" said Mr. Wilson.

"Mine has run cold many years; at present it's about up to the boiling point," said George.

"Well, my good sir," he added after a pause, "I saw you knew me; I thought I'd have this talk, lest your surprise should betray me. I leave early to-morrow, before daylight; to-morrow night I hope to sleep safe in Ohio. I shall travel by daylight, stop at the best hotels, go to dinner with the lords of the land. So, good-by, sir; if you hear I'm taken, you may know I'm dead!"

He stood like a rock, and put out his hand with the air of a prince. The friendly little old man shook it heartily; after a shower of caution, took his umbrella, and fumbled out.

George looked after him; a thought flashed; he opened the door.

"Mr. Wilson—one word more."

The old gentleman reëntered; George locked the door, and stood looking down, irresolute. At last, with a sudden effort—

"Mr. Wilson, you have shown yourself a Christian to me; I want one last deed of Christian kindness."

"Well, George."

"What you said was true. I _am_ running a dreadful risk. There isn't, on earth, a living soul to care if I die,"—he drew his breath hard,—"I shall be kicked out and buried like a dog, and nobody'll think of it a day after,—_only my poor wife!_ Poor soul! she'll mourn; and if you'd contrive to send this little pin to her." He took it out. "She gave it to me for a Christmas present, poor child! Give it to her, and tell her I loved her to the last. Will you? _Will_ you?"

"Yes, certainly—poor fellow!" said the old gentleman, with watery eyes.

"Tell her one thing," said George. "It's my last wish: if she _can_ get to Canada, to go there. No matter how kind her mistress is, no matter how she loves her home; beg her not to go back,—for slavery always ends in misery. Tell her to bring up our boy a free man, and he won't suffer as I have. Tell her this—will you?"

"Yes, George. I'll tell her; but I trust you won't die; take heart,—you're a brave fellow. Trust in the Lord. I wish in my heart you were safe through,—that's what I do."

"_Is_ there a God to trust in?" said George, in a tone of bitter despair that arrested speech. "O, I've seen things all my life that make me feel there can't be a God. You Christians don't know how these things look to us. There's a God for you; but is there any for us?"

"O, now, don't—don't, my boy!" said the old man, almost sobbing. "There is—there is; clouds and darkness are round about him, but righteousness and judgment are the habitation of his throne. There's a _God_, George—believe it; trust in Him, and I'm sure He'll help you. Everything will be set right,—if not in this life, in another."

The simple piety and benevolence of the old man clothed him, for the moment, with dignity. George stopped his distracted walk, stood a moment thoughtfully, and then said, quietly,

"Thank you for saying that, my good friend; I'll _think of that_."

Select Incident of Lawful Trade

"In Ramah there was a voice heard,—weeping, and lamentation, and great mourning; Rachel weeping for her children, and would not be comforted."

Jer. 31:15.

Mr. Haley and Tom jogged onward in their wagon, each, for a time, absorbed in his own reflections. Now, the reflections of two men sitting side by side are a curious thing,—seated on the same seat, having the same eyes, ears, hands, and organs of all sorts, and having pass before their eyes the same objects,—it is wonderful what a variety we shall find in these same reflections!

As, for example, Mr. Haley: he thought first of Tom's length, and breadth, and height, and what he would sell for, if he was kept fat and in good case till he got him into market. He thought of how he should make out his gang; he thought of the respective market value of certain supposititious men and women and children who were to compose it, and other kindred topics of the business; then he thought of himself, and how humane he was, that whereas other men chained their "niggers" hand and foot both, he only put fetters on the feet, and left Tom the use of his hands, as long as he behaved well; and he sighed to think how ungrateful human nature was, so that there was even room to doubt whether Tom appreciated his mercies. He had been taken in so by "niggers" whom he had favored; but still he was astonished to consider how good-natured he yet remained!

As to Tom, he was thinking over some words of an unfashionable old book, which kept running through his head, again and again, as follows: "We have here no continuing city, but we seek one to come; wherefore God himself is not ashamed to be called our God; for he hath prepared for us a city." These words of an ancient volume, got up principally by "ignorant and unlearned men," have, through all time, kept up, somehow, a strange sort of power over the minds of poor, simple fellows, like Tom. They stir up the soul from its depths, and rouse, as with trumpet call, courage, energy, and enthusiasm, where before was only the blackness of despair.

Mr. Haley pulled out of his pocket sundry newspapers, and began looking over their advertisements, with absorbed interest. He was not a remarkably fluent reader, and was in the habit of reading in a sort of recitative half-aloud. In this tone he slowly recited the following paragraph:

"EXECUTOR'S SALE,—NEGROES!—Agreeably to order of court, will be sold, on Tuesday, February 20, before the Court-house door, in the town of Washington, Kentucky, the following negroes: Hagar, aged 60; John, aged 30; Ben, aged 21; Saul, aged 25; Albert, aged 14. Sold for the benefit of the creditors and heirs of the estate of Jesse Blutchford, Esq.

"SAMUEL MORRIS,
THOMAS FLINT,
Executors."

"This yer I must look at," said he to Tom, for want of somebody else to talk to.

"Ye see, I'm going to get up a prime gang to take down with ye, Tom; it'll make it sociable and pleasant like,—good company will, ye know. We must drive right to Washington first and foremost, and then I'll clap you into jail, while I does the business."

Tom received this agreeable intelligence quite meekly; simply wondering, in his own heart, how many of these doomed men had wives and children, and whether they would feel as he did about leaving them. It is to be confessed, too, that the off-hand information that he was to be thrown into jail by no means produced an agreeable impression on a poor fellow who had always prided himself on a strictly honest and upright course of life. Yes, Tom, we must confess it, was rather proud of his honesty, poor fellow,—not having very much else to be proud of. However, the day wore on, and the evening saw Haley and Tom comfortably accommodated in Washington,—the one in a tavern, and the other in a jail.

About eleven o'clock the next day, a mixed throng was gathered around the court-house steps,—smoking, chewing, spitting, swearing, and conversing, according to their respective tastes and turns,—waiting for the auction to commence. The men and women to be sold sat in a group apart, talking in a low tone to each other. The woman advertised as Hagar was a regular African in feature and figure,—older than sixty by hard work and disease, partially blind, and somewhat crippled with rheumatism. By her side stood her only remaining son, Albert, a bright-looking little fellow of fourteen years. The boy was the only survivor of a large family, who had been successively sold away from her to a southern market. The mother held on to him with both her shaking hands, and eyed with intense trepidation every one who walked up to examine him.

"Don't be feard, Aunt Hagar," said the oldest of the men; "I spoke to Mas'r Thomas 'bout it, and he thought he might manage to sell you in a lot both together."

"Dey needn't call me worn out yet," said she, lifting her shaking hands. "I can cook yet, and scrub, and scour,—I'm wuth a buying, if I do come cheap;—tell 'em dat ar,—you tell 'em," she added, earnestly.

Haley forced his way into the group, walked up to the old man, pulled his mouth open and looked in, felt of his teeth, made him stand and straighten himself, bend his back, and perform various evolutions to show his muscles; then passed on to the next, and put him through the same trial. Walking up last to the boy, he felt of his arms, straightened his hands, looked at his fingers, and made him jump, to show his agility.

"He an't gwine to be sold widout me!" said the old woman, with passionate eagerness; "he and I goes in a lot together; I 's rail strong yet, Mas'r, and can do heaps o' work,—heaps on it, Mas'r."

"On plantation?" said Haley, with a contemptuous glance. "Likely story!" He walked out, and stood with his hands in his pockets, his cigar in his mouth, and his hat cocked on one side, ready for action.

"What think of 'em?" said a man who had been following Haley's examination.

"Wal," said Haley, spitting, "I shall put in, I think, for the youngerly ones and the boy."

"They want to sell the boy and the old woman together," said the man.

"Find it a tight pull;—why, she's an old rack o' bones,—not worth her salt."

"Some buys up these yer old critturs, and ses there's a sight more wear in 'em than a body 'd think," said the man, reflectively.

"No go, 't all," said Haley; "wouldn't take her for a present,—fact; I've seen, now."

"Wal, 't is kinder pity, now, not to buy her with her son,—her heart seems so sot on him."

"Them that's got money to spend that ar way, it's all well enough. I shall bid off on that ar boy for a plantation-hand;—wouldn't be bothered with her, no way, not if they'd give her to me," said Haley.

"She'll take on desp't," said the man.

"Nat'lly, she will," said the trader, coolly.

The auctioneer, a short, bustling, important fellow, elbowed his way into the crowd. The old woman drew in her breath, and caught instinctively at her son.

"Keep close to yer mammy, Albert,—close,—dey'll put us up togedder," she said.

"O, mammy, I'm feard they won't," said the boy.

"Dey must, child; I can't live, no ways, if they don't," said the old creature, vehemently.

The bidding began. The different men on the list were soon knocked off at prices which showed a brisk demand in the market; two of them fell to Haley.

"Come, now, young un," said the auctioneer, giving the boy a touch with his hammer, "be up and show your springs, now."

"Put us two up togedder,—do please, Mas'r," said the old woman, holding fast to her boy.

"Be off," said the man, pushing her hands away; "you come last. Now, darkey, spring." He pushed the boy toward the block, while a deep, heavy groan rose behind him. The boy dashed the tears from his large, bright eyes, and was up in a moment.

His fine figure, alert limbs, and bright face raised an instant competition; bids clattered now here, now there, till the hammer fell. Haley had got him. He was pushed from the block toward his new master, but stopped one moment, and looked back, when his poor old mother, trembling in every limb, held out her shaking hands toward him.

"Buy me too, Mas'r, for de dear Lord's sake!—buy me,—I shall die if you don't!"

"You'll die if I do,—that's the kink of it," said Haley,—"no!" And he turned on his heel.

The man who had addressed Haley, and who seemed not destitute of compassion, bought her for a trifle, and the spectators began to disperse. The poor victims, brought up together for years, gathered round the despairing old mother.

"Couldn't dey leave me one? Mas'r allers said I should have one,—he did," she repeated, in heart-broken tones.

"Trust in the Lord, Aunt Hagar," said the oldest of the men, sorrowfully.

"What good will it do?" said she, sobbing.

"Mother, mother,—don't! don't!" said the boy. "They say you 's got a good master."

"I don't care,—I don't care. O, Albert! oh, my boy! you 's my last baby. Lord, how ken I?"

"Come, take her off, can't some of ye?" said Haley, dryly. The old men, partly by persuasion and partly by force, loosed her last despairing hold, and led her to her new master's wagon.

"Now!" said Haley, pushing his three purchases together, and producing a bundle of handcuffs, which he proceeded to put on their wrists; and fastening each to a long chain, he drove them before him to the jail.

A few days saw Haley, with his possessions, safely deposited on one of the Ohio boats. It was the commencement of his gang, to be augmented, as the boat moved on, by various other merchandise of the same kind.

The La Belle Riviere, as brave and beautiful a boat as ever walked the waters of her namesake river, was floating gayly down the stream, under a brilliant sky, the stripes and stars of free America waving and fluttering over head; the guards crowded with well-dressed ladies and gentlemen walking and enjoying the delightful day. All was buoyant and rejoicing;—all but Haley's gang, who were stored, with other freight, on the lower deck, and who, somehow, did not seem to appreciate their various privileges, as they sat in a knot, talking in low tones.

"Boys," said Haley, briskly, "I hope you keep up good heart, and are cheerful. Now, no sulks; keep stiff upper lip, boys; do well by me, and I'll do well by you."

"Yes, Mas'r," came the invariable answer; but they had their prejudices in favor of wives, mothers, sisters, and children, seen for the last time,—and though "they that wasted them required of them mirth," it was not instantly forthcoming.

"I've got a wife," spoke out the article enumerated as "John, aged thirty," laying his chained hand on Tom's knee; "and she don't know a word about this, poor girl!"

"Where does she live?" said Tom.

"In a tavern a piece down here," said John; "I wish, now, I could see her once more in this world," he added. The tears fell as naturally as if he had been a white man. Tom drew a long breath from a sore heart, and tried, in his poor way, to comfort him.

Over head, in the cabin, sat fathers and mothers; husbands and wives; and merry, dancing children moved round among them like so many little butterflies.

"O, mamma," said a boy, who had just come up from below, "there's a negro trader on board, and he's brought four or five slaves down there."

"Poor creatures!" said the mother.

"What's that?" said another lady.

"Some poor slaves below," said the mother.

"And they've got chains on," said the boy.

"What a shame to our country that such sights are to be seen!" said another lady.

"O, there's a great deal to be said on both sides," said a genteel woman, sewing at her state-room door, while her little girl and boy were playing round her. "I've been south, and I must say I think the negroes are better off than they would be to be free."

"In some respects, some of them are well off, I grant," said the first lady. "The most dreadful part of slavery, to my mind, is its outrages on the feelings and affections,—the separating of families, for example."

"That is a bad thing, certainly," said the other, holding up a baby's dress and looking intently on its trimmings; "but then, I fancy, it don't occur often."

"O, it does," said the first, eagerly; "I've lived many years in Kentucky and Virginia both, and I've seen enough to make any one's heart sick. Suppose, ma'am, your two children, there, should be taken from you, and sold?"

"We can't reason from our feelings to those of this class of persons," said the other, sorting out some worsteds.

"Indeed, ma'am, you can know nothing of them, if you say so," answered the first, warmly. "I was born and brought up among them. I know they do feel, just as keenly,—even more so, perhaps,—as we do."

"After all," yawned the lady, looking out the window, "I think they are better off than they would be to be free."

"It's undoubtedly the intention of Providence that the African race should be servants,—kept in a low condition," said a grave-looking gentleman in black, a clergyman, seated by the cabin door. "'Cursed be Canaan; a servant of servants shall he be,' the scripture says."

"I say, stranger, is that ar what that text means?" said a tall man, standing by.

"Undoubtedly. It pleased Providence, for some inscrutable reason, to doom the race to bondage, ages ago; and we must not set up our opinion against that."

"Well, then, we'll all go ahead and buy up niggers," said the man, "if that's the way of Providence,—won't we, Squire?" said he, turning to Haley, who had been standing, with his hands in his pockets, by the stove, intently listening.

"Yes," continued the tall man, "we must all be resigned to the decrees of Providence. Niggers must be sold, and trucked round, and kept under; it's what they's made for. 'Pears like this yer view 's quite refreshing, an't it, stranger?" said he to Haley.

"I never thought on 't," said Haley. "I ha'nt no larning. I took up the trade just to make a living; if 'tan't right, I calculated to 'pent on 't in time, ye know."

"And now you'll save yerself the trouble, won't ye?" said the tall man. "See what 't is, now, to know scripture."

A tall, slender young man, with a face expressive of great feeling and intelligence, here broke in, and repeated, "'All things whatsoever ye would that men should do unto you, do ye even so unto them.' I suppose that is scripture, as much as 'Cursed be Canaan.'"

"Wal, it seems quite as plain a text, stranger," said the long-legged drover—the same frank Kentuckian who had spat upon the handbill in the tavern at N——,—and smoked on like a volcano.

The young man paused, looked as if he was going to say more, when suddenly the boat stopped, and the company made the usual steamboat rush. A black woman came running wildly up the plank, darted into the crowd, flew up to where the slave gang sat, and threw her arms round that unfortunate piece of merchandise before enumerated—"John, aged thirty,"—and with sobs and tears bemoaned him as her husband.

But what needs tell the story, told too oft,—every day told,—of heart-strings rent and broken,—the weak torn for the profit of the strong!

The young man who had spoken for the cause of humanity and God stood with folded arms, looking on. He turned, and Haley was standing at his side.

"My friend," he said, speaking with thick utterance, "how can you, how dare you, carry on a trade like this? Look at those poor creatures! Here I am, rejoicing in my heart that I am going home to my wife and child; and the same bell which is a signal to carry me onward towards them will part this poor man and his wife forever. Depend upon it, God will bring you into judgment for this."

The trader turned away in silence.

"I say, now," said the drover, touching his elbow, "there's differences in parsons, an't there? 'Cussed be Canaan' don't seem to go down with this 'un, does it? Mabbee it won't go down with the Lord, neither."

Haley walked reflectively to the other end of the boat. "If I make pretty handsomely on one or two next gangs," he thought, "I reckon I'll stop off this yer; it's really getting dangerous." He took out his pocket-book, and began adding over his accounts,—a specific for an uneasy conscience.

One day, when she lay to at a small town in Kentucky, Haley went up into the place on a little matter of business. Tom, whose fetters did not prevent his taking a moderate circuit, stood listlessly gazing over the railing. After a time, he saw the trader returning, with an alert step, in company with a colored woman, bearing in her arms a young child. She was dressed quite respectably, and a colored man followed, bringing a small trunk. The woman came cheerfully onward, talking with the man, and so passed up the plank into the boat. The bell rung, the steamer whizzed, the engine groaned and coughed, and away swept the boat down the river.

The woman walked forward among the boxes and bales of the lower deck, and, sitting down, busied herself with chirruping to her baby. Haley made a turn or two, then seated himself near her, and began saying something in an undertone. Tom noticed a heavy cloud pass over the woman's brow; she answered rapidly, and with great vehemence.

"I don't believe it,—I won't believe it!" he heard her say. "You're jist a foolin' with me."

"If you won't believe it, look here!" said the man, drawing out a paper; "this yer's the bill of sale, and there's your master's name to it; and I paid down good solid cash for it, too,—so, now!"

"I don't believe Mas'r would cheat me so; it can't be true!" said the woman, with increasing agitation.

"You can ask any of these men here, that can read writing. Here!" he said to a passer, "jist read this yer, won't you! This yer gal won't believe me."

"Why, it's a bill of sale, signed by John Fosdick," said the man, "making over to you the girl Lucy and her child. It's all straight enough, for aught I see."

"He told me that I was going down to Louisville, to hire out as cook to the same tavern where my husband works,—that's what Mas'r told me, his own self; and I can't believe he'd lie to me," said the woman.

"But he has sold you, my poor woman," said a good-natured looking man, who had been examining the papers.

"Then it's no account talking," said the woman, suddenly growing quite calm; and, clasping her child tighter, she sat down on her box, turned her back round, and gazed listlessly into the river.

"Going to take it easy, after all!" said the trader. "Gal's got grit, I see."

The beautiful soft summer breeze passed like a compassionate spirit over her head,—the gentle breeze, that never inquires whether the brow is dusky or fair that it fans. Sunshine sparkled on the water; gay voices, full of ease and pleasure, talked around her; but her heart lay as if a great stone had fallen on it. Her baby raised himself up, stroked her cheeks with his little hands; sprang up and down, crowing and chatting; she strained him suddenly and tightly in her arms; slowly one tear after another fell on his wondering face; gradually she grew calmer, and busied herself with tending him.

The child, a boy of ten months, was uncommonly large and strong of his age, and very vigorous in his limbs.

"That's a fine chap!" said a man, stopping opposite, with his hands in his pockets. "How old is he?"

"Ten months and a half," said the mother.

The man whistled to the boy, offered him part of a stick of candy, and walked on. When he had got to the other side of the boat, he came across Haley, who was smoking on top of a pile of boxes.

"Decentish kind o' wench you've got round there, stranger," said the man.

"Why, I reckon she is tol'able fair," said Haley.

"Taking her down south?" said the man.

Haley nodded.

"Plantation hand?"

"Wal," said Haley, "I'm fillin' out an order for a plantation, and I think I shall put her in. They telled me she was a good cook; and they can use her for that, or set her at the cotton-picking. She's got the right fingers; I looked at 'em. Sell well, either way."

"They won't want the young 'un on the plantation," said the man.

"I shall sell him, first chance I find," said Haley, lighting another cigar.

"S'pose you'd be selling him tol'able cheap," said the stranger, mounting the pile and sitting down comfortably.

"Don't know 'bout that," said Haley; "he's a pretty smart young 'un, straight, fat, strong; flesh as hard as a brick!"

"Very true, but then there's the bother and expense of raisin'."

"Nonsense!" said Haley; "they is raised as easy as any kind of critter there is going; they an't a bit more trouble than pups. This yer chap will be running all around, in a month."

"I've got a good place for raisin', and I thought of takin' in a little more stock," said the man. "One cook lost a young 'un last week,—got drownded in a washtub,—and I reckon it would be well enough to set her to raisin' this yer."

"You wouldn't think of wantin' more than ten dollars for that ar chap, seeing you must get him off yer hand, any how?" he ventured, after a silence.

Haley shook his head, and spit. "That won't do, no ways."

"Well, stranger, what will you take?"

"Well, now," said Haley, "I could raise that ar chap myself. He's oncommon likely and healthy, and he'd fetch a hundred dollars, six months hence; and, in a year or two, he'd bring two hundred, if I had him in the right spot; I shan't take a cent less nor fifty for him now."

"O, stranger! that's ridiculous."

"Fact!" said Haley.

"I'll give thirty for him," said the stranger, "but not a cent more."

"I'll split the difference, and say forty-five; and that's the most I will do."

"Well, agreed!" said the man.

"Done!" said Haley. "Where do you land?"

"At Louisville."

"Very fair; we get there about dusk. Chap will be asleep,—all fair,—get him off quietly, and no screaming,—happens beautiful,—I like to do everything quietly,—I hates all kind of agitation and fluster."

It was a bright, tranquil evening when the boat stopped at the wharf at Louisville. The woman had been sitting with her baby in her arms, now wrapped in a heavy sleep. When she heard the name of the place called out, she hastily laid the child down in a little cradle formed by the hollow among the boxes, first carefully spreading under it her cloak; then she sprung to the side of the boat, in hopes that, among the various hotel-waiters who thronged the wharf, she might see her husband. In this hope, she pressed forward to the front rails, straining her eyes intently on the moving heads on the shore, and the crowd pressed in between her and the child.

"Now's your time," said Haley, taking the sleeping child up, and handing him to the stranger. "Don't wake him up, and set him to crying, now; it would make a devil of a fuss with the gal." The man took the bundle carefully, and was soon lost in the crowd.

When the boat had loosed from the wharf, and was beginning slowly to strain herself along, the woman returned to her old seat. The trader was sitting there,—the child was gone!

"Why, why,—where?" she began, in bewildered surprise.

"Lucy," said the trader, "your child's gone; you may as well know it first as last. You see, I know'd you couldn't take him down south; and I got a chance to sell him to a first-rate family, that'll raise him better than you can."

The trader had arrived at that stage of perfection lately recommended, in which he had completely overcome every humane weakness and prejudice. The wild look of anguish and utter despair that the woman cast on him might have disturbed one less practised; but he was used to it. He only calculated whether she was going to scream, and get up a commotion; for, like other supporters of our peculiar institution, he decidedly disliked agitation.

But the woman did not scream. The shot had passed too straight and direct through the heart for cry or tear. Dizzily she sat down. Her slack hands fell lifeless. Her eyes looked straight forward, but she saw nothing. The groaning of the machinery mingled dreamily to her bewildered ear; and the poor, dumb-stricken heart had neither cry nor tear to show for its utter misery. She was quite calm.

"I know this yer comes kinder hard, at first, Lucy," said he; "but such a smart, sensible gal as you are, won't give way to it. You see it's necessary, and can't be helped!"

"O! don't, Mas'r, don't!" said the woman, with a voice like one that is smothering.

"You're a smart wench, Lucy," he persisted; "I mean to do well by ye, and get ye a nice place down river; and you'll soon get another husband,—such a likely gal as you—"

"O! Mas'r, if you only won't talk to me now," said the woman, in such quick and living anguish that the trader felt there was, at present, something beyond his style of operation. He got up, and the woman turned away, and buried her head in her cloak.

Tom had watched the whole transaction, and had a perfect understanding of its results. To him, it looked like something unutterably horrible and cruel; because, poor, ignorant black soul! he had not learned to generalize, and to take enlarged views. If he had only been instructed, he might have seen in it an every-day incident of a lawful trade. But Tom, whose reading had been confined entirely to the New Testament, could not comfort himself with views like these. His very soul bled within him for what seemed to him the wrongs of the poor suffering thing that lay like a crushed reed on the boxes; the feeling, living, bleeding, yet immortal thing, which state law coolly classes with the bundles and bales among which she lay.

Tom drew near, and tried to say something; but she only groaned. Honestly, and with tears running down his own cheeks, he spoke of a heart of love in the skies, of a pitying Jesus, and an eternal home; but the ear was deaf with anguish, and the palsied heart could not feel.

Night came on,—night calm, unmoved, and glorious, shining down with her innumerable and solemn angel eyes. One after another, the voices died away; all on the boat were sleeping, and the ripples at the prow were plainly heard. Tom stretched himself on a box, and there, as he lay, he heard, ever and anon, a smothered sob or cry from the prostrate creature,—"O! what shall I do? O Lord! O good Lord, do help me!"—until the murmur died away in silence.

At midnight, Tom waked, with a sudden start. Something black passed quickly by him to the side of the boat, and he heard a splash in the water. He raised his head,—the woman's place was vacant! He sought about him in vain. The poor bleeding heart was still, at last, and the river rippled and dimpled just as brightly as if it had not closed above it.

Patience! patience! ye whose hearts swell indignant at wrongs like these. Not one throb of anguish, not one tear of the oppressed, is forgotten by the Man of Sorrows, the Lord of Glory. Bear thou, like him, in patience, and labor in love; for sure as he is God, "the year of his redeemed shall come."

The trader waked up bright and early, and came out to see to his live stock. It was now his turn to look about in perplexity.

"Where alive is that gal?" he said to Tom.

Tom, who had learned the wisdom of keeping counsel, said he did not know.

"She surely couldn't have got off in the night at any of the landings, for I was awake, and on the lookout, whenever the boat stopped. I never trust these yer things to other folks."

After a fruitless search among boxes, bales, and barrels, around the machinery, by the chimneys, in vain, he came back.

"Now, I say, Tom, be fair about this yer. You know something about it, now. Don't tell me,—I know you do. I saw the gal stretched out here about ten o'clock, and ag'in at twelve, and ag'in between one and two; and then at four she was gone, and you was a sleeping right there all the time."

"Well, Mas'r," said Tom, "towards morning something brushed by me, and I kinder half woke; and then I hearn a great splash, and then I clare woke up, and the gal was gone. That's all I know on 't."

The trader was not shocked nor amazed; he was used to a great many things that you are not used to. Even the awful presence of Death struck no solemn chill upon him. He only swore that the gal was a baggage, and that he was devilish unlucky, and that, if things went on in this way, he should not make a cent on the trip. He sat down, with his little account-book, and put down the missing body and soul under the head of losses.

"He's a shocking creature, isn't he,—this trader? so unfeeling! It's dreadful, really!"

"O, but nobody thinks anything of these traders! They are universally despised,—never received into any decent society."

But who, sir, makes the trader? Who is most to blame? The enlightened, cultivated, intelligent man, who supports the system of which the trader is the inevitable result, or the poor trader himself? You make the public sentiment that calls for his trade, that debauches and depraves him, till he feels no shame in it; and in what are you better than he? Are you educated and he ignorant, you high and he low, you refined and he coarse, you talented and he simple? In the day of a future judgment, these very considerations may make it more tolerable for him than for you.

And lest any should infer that American legislators are entirely destitute of humanity, let it be remembered how our great men outdo themselves in declaiming against the foreign slave-trade. Trading negroes from Africa, dear reader, is so horrid! It is not to be thought of! But trading them from Kentucky,—that's quite another thing! We have a perfect host of Clarksons and Wilberforces for the first; for the second, the La Belle Riviere goes gayly down, with the stripes and stars fluttering over head, and on the lower deck a knot of human hearts, counted and entered in a little book, under the proper heads,—gains and losses.

A quiet scene now rises before us. A large, neatly-painted kitchen, the yellow floor glossy and smooth, not a particle of dust; a well-blacked cooking-stove; rows of shining tin, suggestive of unmentionable good things; old, glossy green chairs; a small flag-bottomed rocker with a patch-work cushion, and a larger one, motherly and wide, whose hospitable arms seconded the solicitation of its feather cushions,—a real comfortable, persuasive old chair, worth, in the way of honest, homely enjoyment, a dozen of your plush drawing-room gentry. In it, gently swaying, her eyes bent on fine sewing, sat our friend Eliza—paler and thinner than in her Kentucky home, with a world of quiet sorrow under the shadow of her long eyelashes, the girlish heart grown firm beneath heavy discipline; and when, anon, her large dark eye rose to follow the gambols of little Harry, sporting, like some tropical butterfly, hither and thither over the floor, there was in it a depth of steady resolve unknown to her earlier, happier days.

By her sat a woman with a bright tin pan in her lap, sorting dried peaches. Fifty-five or sixty, perhaps; but hers was one of those faces that time touches only to brighten. The snowy lisse crape cap of the strait Quaker pattern, the plain white muslin handkerchief lying in placid folds, the drab shawl and dress, told their community. Round and rosy, with a soft, healthful bloom; smooth-parted hair, partially silvered, over a high, placid brow where time had written only peace on earth, good will to men; beneath, a large pair of clear, honest, loving brown eyes, into which to look was to see to the bottom of a true heart. So much is said and sung of beautiful young girls; why not of beautiful old women? We refer the doubter to our good friend Rachel Halliday, as she sits in her little rocking-chair. It had a turn for quacking and squeaking,—a subdued “creechy crawchy,”—but old Simeon declared it was as good as any music to him, and the children avowed they would not miss mother’s chair for anything. For why? For twenty years and more, nothing but loving words, gentle moralities, motherly kindness had come from it; head-aches and heart-aches cured there; difficulties, spiritual and temporal, solved there,—all by one good, loving woman,—God bless her!

“And so thee still thinks of going to Canada, Eliza?” said Rachel, quietly turning her peaches.

“Yes, ma’am,” said Eliza, firmly. “I must go onward. I dare not stop.”

“And what’ll thee do when thee gets there? Thee must think about that, my daughter.”

“My daughter” came naturally from Rachel’s lips; hers was the face and form that made “mother” the most natural word in the world. Eliza’s hands trembled; tears fell on her work; but she answered, firm still,—

“I shall do—anything I can find. I hope I can find something.”

“Thee knows thee can stay here, as long as thee pleases,” said Rachel.

“O, thank you,” said Eliza, “but—” she pointed to Harry—“I can’t sleep nights; I can’t rest. Last night I dreamed I saw that man coming into the yard,” she shuddered.

“Poor child!” said Rachel, wiping her eyes. “But thee mustn’t feel so. The Lord hath ordered it so that never hath a fugitive been stolen from our village. I trust thine will not be the first.”

The door opened, and a little short, round, pin-cushiony woman came in, with a cheery, blooming face, like a ripe apple,—dressed, like Rachel, in sober gray. This was Ruth Stedman. She dusted her little drab bonnet, coaxed a wayward curl beneath the Quaker cap, and turned, well pleased,—as most might be who looked at her; for she was a wholesome, whole-hearted, chirruping little woman.

“Ruth, this friend is Eliza Harris; and this is the little boy I told thee of.”

“I am glad to see thee, Eliza,—very,” said Ruth, shaking hands as if with an old acquaintance. “And this is thy dear boy,—I brought a cake for him.”

“Where’s thy baby, Ruth?” said Rachel.

“O, he’s coming; but thy Mary caught him as I came in, and ran off with him to the barn, to show him to the children.”

Mary, an honest, rosy girl, with large brown eyes like her mother’s, soon appeared with a great, white, fat fellow in her arms.

“Ah! ha!” said Rachel, taking him. “How good he looks, and how he does grow!”

Ruth untied hood and wrappers with brisk fingers, kissed him, set him on the floor to collect his thoughts; the little thumb went into the little mouth, as if it were quite a thing of course; and the mother, seating herself, drew out a long stocking of mixed blue and white yarn and fell to knitting.

“Mary, thee’d better fill the kettle, hadn’t thee?” suggested Rachel.

Mary filled it; soon it purred and steamed, a censer of hospitality; the peaches, by gentle hint, were set to stew; Rachel took down a snowy moulding-board, tied on an apron, and fell quietly to making biscuits. “Mary, hadn’t thee better tell John to get a chicken ready?” and Mary disappeared accordingly. Compassion and neighborly care ran, like a spring, through their speech: who had sat up with the sick, who would bake and mend on the morrow; and, “John can come in here to his meals, if thee needs to stay all day,” said Rachel.

Simeon Halliday, tall, straight, muscular, in drab coat and broad-brimmed hat, now entered.

“How is thee, Ruth?” he said, warmly, offering his broad hand. “And how is John?”

“Any news, father?” said Rachel, putting biscuits into the oven.

“Peter Stebbins told me they should be along tonight with friends,” said Simeon, significantly, washing at the neat back-porch sink.

“Indeed!” said Rachel, thoughtfully, glancing at Eliza.

“Did thee say thy name was Harris?” said Simeon to Eliza, reentering.

Eliza tremulously answered, “Yes.”

“Mother!” said Simeon, calling Rachel into the porch.

“What does thee want, father?” said Rachel, rubbing floury hands.

“This child’s husband is in the settlement, and will be here tonight.”

“Now, thee doesn’t say that, father?” and all Rachel’s face grew radiant.

“It’s really true. Peter was down yesterday to the other stand, and found an old woman and two men; one said his name was George Harris; and, from what he told of his history, I am certain. He is a bright, likely fellow, too.”

“Shall we tell her now?” said Simeon.

“Let’s tell Ruth,” said Rachel. “Here, Ruth,—come here.”

“Ruth, what does thee think? Father says Eliza’s husband is in the last company, and will be here tonight.”

At that, the little Quakeress gave such a bound, clapping her hands, that two stray curls fell on her white neckerchief.

“Hush thee, dear!” said Rachel. “Hush, Ruth! Tell us, shall we tell her now?”

“Now! to be sure,—this very minute. Why, now, suppose ’t was my John, how should I feel? Do tell her, right off.”

“Thee uses thyself only to learn how to love thy neighbor, Ruth,” said Simeon, beaming.

“To be sure. Isn’t it what we are made for? If I didn’t love John and the baby, I should not know how to feel for her. Come, now do tell her,—do! Take her into thy bed-room, there, and let me fry the chicken while thee does it.”

Rachel went in to Eliza, and, opening the door of a small bed-room, said, gently, “Come in here with me, my daughter; I have news to tell thee.”

The blood flushed in Eliza’s pale face; she rose, trembling; looked toward her boy.

“No, no,” cried little Ruth, darting up, seizing her hands. “Never thee fear; it’s good news, Eliza,—go in, go in!” And, turning, she caught Harry and began kissing him. “Thee’ll see thy father, little one. Does thee know it? Thy father is coming.”

Within, Rachel drew Eliza toward her, and said, “The Lord hath had mercy on thee, daughter; thy husband hath escaped from the house of bondage.”

The blood flushed to Eliza’s cheek, and ebbed to her heart. She sat down, pale and faint.

“Have courage, child,” said Rachel, laying a hand on her head. “He is among friends, who will bring him here tonight.”

“Tonight!” Eliza repeated. “Tonight!” The word lost meaning; all went misty.

When she awoke, she found herself snugly tucked up, a blanket over her, little Ruth rubbing her hands with camphor. She lay in a dreamy, delicious languor, such as one who has long borne a load and now feels it gone, and would rest. The tension, never ceased since the first hour of her flight, gave way; a strange feeling of security and rest came over her; and as she lay, eyes open, she followed, as in a quiet dream, the motions about her: the door open into the other room; the supper-table, snowy cloth; the dreamy murmur of the singing tea-kettle; Ruth tripping with plates of cake and saucers of preserves, stopping to put a cake into Harry’s hand, to pat his head, to twine a curl round her snowy fingers; the ample, motherly form of Rachel, who ever and anon came to smooth something, to give a tuck here and there, expressing good-will; the sunshine of her clear brown eyes. Ruth’s husband came in; she flew to whisper him, pointing toward the room; they all sat at tea; little Harry in a high chair under Rachel’s ample wing; low murmurs of talk; gentle tinkling of tea-spoons; musical clatter of cups,—and Eliza slept, as she had not slept since that fearful midnight when she fled through the frosty starlight.

She dreamed of a beautiful country,—a land of rest,—green shores, pleasant islands, glittering water; and there, in a house kind voices told her was a home, she saw her boy free and happy. She heard her husband’s footsteps; felt him coming nearer; his arms were round her, his tears falling on her face,—and she awoke! It was no dream. The daylight had long faded; her child lay calmly sleeping; a candle burned dimly on the stand; and her husband was sobbing by her pillow.

The next morning was a cheerful one at the Quaker house. “Mother” was up betimes, surrounded by busy girls and boys, obedient to Rachel’s gentle “Thee had better,” or more gentle “Hadn’t thee better?” in the getting of breakfast; for a breakfast in the luxurious valleys of Indiana is a thing complicated and multiform. While John ran to the spring for water, Simeon the second sifted meal for corn-cakes, and Mary ground coffee, Rachel moved quietly,—making biscuits, cutting up chicken,—diffusing a sort of sunny radiance. If zeal jostled zeal, her “Come! come!” or “I wouldn’t, now,” allayed it. We have read of the cestus of Venus that turned heads; we had rather have the cestus of Rachel Halliday, that kept heads from being turned, and made everything go on harmoniously.

While all went sociably and well, Simeon the elder stood in his shirt-sleeves before a little glass, engaged in the anti-patriarchal operation of shaving. Even the knives and forks had a social clatter; the chicken and ham a cheerful fizzle in the pan, as if they rather enjoyed being cooked than otherwise;—and when George and Eliza and little Harry came out, such a hearty, rejoicing welcome met them, no wonder it seemed like a dream.

At last, they were all seated, while Mary stood at the stove, baking griddle-cakes, which, as they gained the exact golden-brown tint, were transferred to the table.

Rachel never looked so benignly happy as at the head of her table. There was so much motherliness even in the way she passed a plate or poured a cup, it seemed to put a spirit into the food and drink.

It was the first time George had sat down on equal terms at any white man’s table; he sat at first with some constraint, which melted off, like fog, in the genial rays of this simple kindness. This, indeed, was a home,—home,—a word George had never yet known a meaning for; and a belief in God, and trust in his providence, began to encircle his heart, as, with a golden cloud of protection and confidence, dark, misanthropic, pining doubts and fierce despair melted away before the light of a living Gospel, breathed in living faces, preached by a thousand unconscious acts of love and good will.

“Father, what if thee should get found out again?” said Simeon second, buttering his cake.

“I should pay my fine,” said Simeon, quietly.

“But what if they put thee in prison?”

“Couldn’t thee and mother manage the farm?” said Simeon, smiling.

“Mother can do almost everything,” said the boy. “But isn’t it a shame to make such laws?”

“Thee mustn’t speak evil of thy rulers, Simeon,” said his father, gravely. “The Lord only gives us our worldly goods that we may do justice and mercy; if our rulers require a price of us for it, we must deliver it up.”

“Well, I hate those old slaveholders!” broke out the boy.

“I am surprised at thee, son,” said Simeon. “Thy mother never taught thee so. I would do even the same for the slaveholder as for the slave, if the Lord brought him to my door in affliction.”

Simeon second blushed scarlet; his mother only smiled. “Simeon is my good boy; he will grow older, by and by, and then he will be like his father.”

“I hope, my good sir, that you are not exposed to any difficulty on our account,” said George, anxiously.

“Fear nothing, George, for therefore are we sent into the world. If we would not meet trouble for a good cause, we were not worthy of our name.”

“But, for me,” said George, “I could not bear it.”

“Fear not, then, friend George; it is not for thee, but for God and man, we do it,” said Simeon. “And now thou must lie by quietly this day, and tonight, at ten o’clock, Phineas Fletcher will carry thee onward to the next stand,—thee and the rest of thy company. The pursuers are hard after thee; we must not delay.”

“If that is the case, why wait till evening?” said George.

“Thou art safe here by daylight,” said Simeon; “for every one in the settlement is a Friend, and all are watching. It has been found safer to travel by night.”

Evangeline

“A young star! which shone
O’er life—too sweet an image, for such glass!
A lovely being, scarcely formed or moulded;
A rose with all its sweetest leaves yet folded.”

The Mississippi! Once a river of mighty solitudes, it now bears along its broad bosom to the sea the wealth and hurry of a land whose products run from tropic to pole. Turbid waters, tearing, foaming, headlong,—an image of the vehement race that drives its business on their wave; and, alas! they bear, too, a darker freight,—the tears of the oppressed, the sighs of helpless hearts to an unknown God—unknown, unseen, and silent, yet who will “come out of his place to save all the poor of the earth!”

The slanting sun kindled the sea-like sweep; the shivery cane and tall cypress, hung with funereal moss, glowed in the golden ray, as the heavily-laden steamboat marched onward. Piled with cotton, up over deck and sides, she loomed like a square, gray block, moving slowly toward the mart.

High on the upper deck, in a nook among the bales, sat our humble friend Tom. Partly through Mr. Shelby’s letters, partly through Tom’s quiet, inoffensive ways, even Haley, the trader, had ceased to fetter him at night, and, trusting his parole of honor, let him come and go on the boat. Ever obliging, with a hearty good will in any need among the hands, he had won general favor; and when there was nothing to do, he climbed to his nook and bent over his Bible.

For a hundred miles above New Orleans, the river, higher than the land, rolls between levees like walls. From the deck, as from a floating castle, one overlooks miles of country; and so, plantation after plantation, Tom read in silence the life he was approaching. He saw gangs at their toil; villages of huts, long and low, beyond the stately house and pleasure-grounds; and, as the moving picture slid by, his poor, foolish heart turned back to the Kentucky farm,—the old shadowy beeches, the master’s cool halls, and, near by, the little vine-overgrown cabin; the familiar faces of those who grew with him; his busy wife; the merry laugh of his boys; the chirrup of the baby at his knee,—till, with a start, all faded again into canebrakes and cypress, the creak and groan of engines, and the stern certainty that all that phase of life was gone forever.

He could not write—no letter, no sign bridged the gulf. Is it strange, then, that some tears fell on his Bible, as with patient finger he threaded his slow way from word to word? He had learned late, and read laboriously; but here was a book whose words are ingots of gold, best weighed singly. Pointing and pronouncing half aloud, he read:

“Let—not—your—heart—be—troubled.
In—my—Father’s—house—are—many—mansions.
I—go—to—prepare—a—place—for—you.”

His Bible, though lacking learned notes, was scored with bold marks of his own devising—way-marks that Master’s children had helped him set as they read to him; so he could seize his favorites without spelling wearily through all between. Every passage breathed of some old home-scene; it was to him all of this life that remained, and the promise of another.

Among the passengers was a young gentleman of fortune from New Orleans, St. Clare by name, with a little daughter of five or six, and a lady connected with them who had the charge of the child.

Tom had often caught glimpses of the little girl,—one of those tripping creatures no more to be contained than a sunbeam. Her form had the airy grace of a myth. Not her features so much as the dreamy earnestness of expression held the dullest; her head and neck were nobly set; long golden-brown hair floated like a cloud; deep, violet-blue eyes, shaded with heavy fringes, made all turn and look as she glided here and there. Yet she was not grave; an innocent playfulness flickered, summer-like, over her, and she seemed always in motion, singing to herself, moving as in a happy dream. Dressed in white, she passed like a shadow without spot, and there was not a nook above or below where her fairy foot had not glided and that visionary head fleeted by. Rough voices blessed her; rough hands reached to smooth her path; the fireman paused, seeing those eyes look into the raging furnace with pity, as if for him; the steersman smiled when the picture-like head gleamed through his window.

Tom, with his soft, impressible nature, yearned toward the childlike, and watched her with growing interest. He half believed her an angel out of his New Testament, whenever that golden head and those deep eyes peeped at him from behind the dusky bales. Often she walked mournfully round where Haley’s gang sat in chains; she would glide in, lift a chain with her little hands, sigh woefully, and pass on; and, again, she came with hands full of candy, nuts, and oranges, distributing them joyfully, and vanishing.

He knew many simple arts to invite little people—baskets cut from cherry-stones, faces carved on hickory-nuts, jumping figures from elder-pith, whistles of many sorts,—treasures he had hoarded for his master’s children, and now, with prudent economy, displayed, one by one. For all her busy interest, she was shy; a while she perched like a canary on some box near him, gravely taking what he offered; but at last they were on confidential terms.

“What’s little missy’s name?” said Tom, when the moment seemed ripe.

“Evangeline St. Clare,” said the little one; “though papa and everybody else call me Eva. Now, what’s your name?”

“My name’s Tom; the little chil’en used to call me Uncle Tom, way back thar in Kentuck.”

“Then I mean to call you Uncle Tom, because, you see, I like you,” said Eva. “So, Uncle Tom, where are you going?”

“I don’t know, Miss Eva.”

“Don’t know?” said Eva.

“No, I am going to be sold to somebody. I don’t know who.”

“My papa can buy you,” said Eva, quickly; “and if he buys you, you will have good times. I mean to ask him, this very day.”

“Thank you, my little lady,” said Tom.

The boat stopped at a wooding-place, and hearing her father’s voice, Eva bounded away. Tom went forward to help in wooding, and was busy among the hands. Presently, as the boat started again, Eva and her father stood by the rail; the great wheel churned twice, and, by some sudden movement, the child lost her balance and fell sheer over the side. Her father sprang, but strong arms held him; more efficient help had followed the child.

Tom, standing beneath on the lower deck, saw her strike the water and sink. He was after her in a moment. Broad-chested and strong-armed, he kept afloat easily till she rose; then he caught her, and, swimming to the side, handed her up, dripping, to the grasp of a hundred hands, which, as if one man, were stretched to receive her. A few moments more and her father bore her senseless to the ladies’ cabin, where ensued, as is usual, a kindly, bustling strife of well-meaning hands that did everything but help.

The next day was sultry and close, as the steamer drew near New Orleans. Expectation and preparation stirred through the boat; in cabin and gangway things were gathered and set in order. On the lower deck sat Tom, arms folded, looking anxiously, from time to time, toward a group across the way.

There stood fair Eva, a little paler, but otherwise unhurt; and at her side a graceful, elegantly-formed young man, leaning carelessly on a bale, a large pocket-book open before him. Plainly he was her father. The same noble head, the same blue eyes and golden-brown hair; but the expression was wholly other. The eyes, though in form and color similar, wanted that dreamy depth; all was clear, bold, bright, with a light of this world; the beautifully cut mouth had a proud, somewhat sarcastic turn; a free-and-easy superiority sat not ungracefully on his fine person. He listened, with good-humored negligence, half comic, half contemptuous, to Haley, the trader, who volubly expatiated on the quality of the article for which they bargained.

“All the moral and Christian virtues bound in black Morocco, complete!” he said, when Haley had finished. “Well, now, my good fellow, what’s the damage, as they say in Kentucky; in short, what’s to be paid out for this business? How much are you going to cheat me, now? Out with it!”

“Wal,” said Haley, “if I should say thirteen hundred dollars for that ar fellow, I shouldn’t but just save myself; I shouldn’t, now, re’ly.”

“Poor fellow!” said the young man, fixing his keen, mocking eye on him; “but I suppose you’d let me have him for that, out of a particular regard for me.”

“Well, the young lady here seems to be sot on him, and nat’lly enough.”

“O! certainly; there’s a call on your benevolence, my friend. Now, as a matter of Christian charity, how cheap could you afford to let him go, to oblige a young lady that’s particular sot on him?”

“Wal, now, just think on ’t,” said the trader; “look at them limbs,—broad-chested, strong as a horse. Look at his head; them high forrads allays shows calculatin niggers. Now, a nigger of that heft is worth considerable, just for his body, supposin he’s stupid; but come to put in his calculatin faculties, which I can show he has oncommon, why, ’course, it makes him come higher. Why, that ar fellow managed his master’s hull farm. He has a strornary talent for business.”

“Bad, bad,—very bad; knows altogether too much!” said the other, with the same smile. “Never will do. Your smart fellows are always running off, stealing horses, and raising the devil generally. I think you’ll have to take off a couple of hundred for his smartness.”

“Wal, there might be suthin in that ar, if ’t warnt for his character; but I can show recommends from his master and others, to prove he is one o’ your real pious,—the most humble, prayin, pious crittur ye ever did see. Why, he’s been called a preacher in them parts he came from.”

“And I might use him for a family chaplain, possibly,” added the young man, dryly. “That’s quite an idea. Religion is a remarkably scarce article at our house.”

“You’re joking, now.”

“How do you know I am? Didn’t you just warrant him for a preacher? Has he been examined by any synod or council? Come, hand over your papers.”

Haley, secure in the good-humored twinkle that promised cash at last, laid down a greasy pocket-book and fumbled among papers. The young man looked down with easy drollery.

“Papa, do buy him! It’s no matter what you pay,” whispered Eva, softly, climbing upon a package and putting her arm about her father’s neck. “You have money enough, I know. I want him.”

“What for, pussy? Are you going to use him for a rattle-box, or a rocking-horse, or what?”

“I want to make him happy.”

“An original reason, certainly.”

Here the trader handed up a certificate signed by Mr. Shelby, Tom’s former master. The young man took it with the tips of his long fingers and glanced over it carelessly.

“A gentlemanly hand,” he said, “and well spelt, too. Well—now, I’m not sure, after all, about this religion,” he added, the wicked light returning to his eye; “the country is almost ruined with pious white people; such pious politicians just before elections,—such pious goings on in church and state, that a fellow doesn’t know who’ll cheat him next. I don’t know, either, about religion’s being up in the market, just now. How many hundred dollars, now, do you put on for this religion?”

“You like to be jokin, now,” said the trader; “but there’s sense under that ar. I know there’s differences in religion. Some kinds is mis’rable: your meetin pious; your singin, roarin pious; them ar an’t no account. But there’s your rail softly, quiet, stiddy, honest, pious, that the hull world couldn’t tempt to do nothing they thinks is wrong; and ye see in this letter what Tom’s old master says.”

“Now,” said the young man, stooping gravely over his bills, “if you can assure me that I really can buy this kind of pious, and that it will be set down to my account in the book up above as something belonging to me, I wouldn’t care if I did go a little extra for it. How d’ye say?”

“Wal, raily, I can’t do that,” said the trader. “I’m a thinkin every man’ll have to hang on his own hook in them ar quarters.”

“Rather hard on a fellow that pays extra on religion, and can’t trade with it in the state where he wants it most, an’t it, now?” said St. Clare, making out a roll of bills. “There, count your money, old boy!”

“All right,” said Haley, beaming, and, pulling out an old inkhorn, he filled a bill of sale and handed it to the purchaser.

“I wonder, now, if I was divided up and inventoried,” said the latter, running over the paper, “how much I might bring. So much for the shape of my head, so much for a high forehead, so much for arms and legs, and so much for education, talent, honesty, religion! Bless me! there would be small charge on that last, I’m thinking. But come, Eva.”

He took his daughter’s hand, stepped across the boat, and, carelessly putting the tip of his finger under Tom’s chin, said, good-humoredly, “Look up, Tom, and see how you like your new master.”

Tom looked up. It was not in nature to see that gay, handsome face without pleasure. Tears started, and he said, heartily, “God bless you, Mas’r!”

“Well, I hope he will. What’s your name? Tom? Quite as likely to do it for your asking as mine, from all accounts. Can you drive horses, Tom?”

“I’ve been allays used to horses,” said Tom. “Mas’r Shelby raised heaps of ’em.”

“Well, I think I shall put you in coachy, on condition you won’t be drunk more than once a week, unless in cases of emergency, Tom.”

Tom looked surprised and hurt. “I never drink, Mas’r.”

“I’ve heard that story before, Tom; but then we’ll see. It will be a special accommodation to all concerned, if you don’t. Never mind, my boy,” he added, good-humoredly, seeing Tom still grave; “I don’t doubt you mean to do well.”

“I sartin do, Mas’r,” said Tom.

“And you shall have good times,” said Eva. “Papa is very good to everybody, only he always will laugh at them.”

“Papa is much obliged to you for his recommendation,” said St. Clare, laughing, as he turned on his heel and walked away.

Of Tom’s New Master, and Various Other Matters

Since the thread of our humble hero’s life has now become interwoven with that of higher ones, it is necessary to give some brief introduction to them.

Augustine St. Clare was the son of a wealthy planter of Louisiana. The family had its origin in Canada. Of two brothers, very similar in temperament and character, one had settled on a flourishing farm in Vermont, and the other became an opulent planter in Louisiana. The mother of Augustine was a Huguenot French lady, whose family had emigrated to Louisiana during the days of its early settlement. Augustine and another brother were the only children of their parents. Having inherited from his mother an exceeding delicacy of constitution, he was, at the instance of physicians, during many years of his boyhood, sent to the care of his uncle in Vermont, to brace his health in a severer climate.

In childhood, he was remarkable for an extreme and marked sensitiveness of character, more akin to the softness of woman than the ordinary hardness of his own sex. Time overgrew this with the rough bark of manhood, yet few knew how living it lay at the core. His talents were of the first order, though his mind always preferred the ideal and the æsthetic, with a repugnance to the actual business of life which often attends that balance of faculties. Soon after college, his whole nature was kindled into one intense effervescence of romantic passion. His hour came,—the hour that comes only once; his star rose,—that star that rises so often in vain, to be remembered only as a thing of dreams; and it rose for him in vain. To drop the figure,—he saw and won the love of a high-minded and beautiful woman, in one of the northern states, and they were affianced. He returned south to make arrangements for their marriage, when, most unexpectedly, his letters were returned to him, with a short note from her guardian, stating that ere it reached him the lady would be the wife of another.

Stung to madness, he vainly hoped, as many another has done, to fling the whole thing from his heart by one desperate effort. Too proud to supplicate or seek explanation, he threw himself at once into a whirl of fashionable society, and in a fortnight from the time of the fatal letter was the accepted lover of the reigning belle of the season; and, as soon as arrangements could be made, he became the husband of a fine figure, a pair of bright dark eyes, and a hundred thousand dollars; and, of course, everybody thought him a happy fellow.

In the full tide of gay company, a letter in that well-remembered hand was brought to him. He changed color, preserved his composure, finished his playful badinage, and then was missed. Alone, he read the letter, now worse than idle and useless to be read. It told how fraud had stood between them,—letters intercepted, importunities urged, health failing, hope dying,—till the truth, at last, was discovered. It ended with hope, thankfulness, undying affection,—more bitter than death to him. He wrote:

“I have received yours,—but too late. I believed all I heard. I was desperate. _I am married_, and all is over. Only forget,—it is all that remains for either of us.”

And thus ended the romance and ideal of life for Augustine St. Clare. But the _real_ remained,—the _real_, like the flat, bare, oozy tide-mud, when the blue sparkling wave, with all its company of gliding boats and white-winged ships, its music of oars and chiming waters, has gone down, and there it lies, flat, slimy, bare,—exceedingly real.

In novels, people’s hearts break, and they die, and that is the end; and in a story this is very convenient. But in life we do not die when all that makes life bright dies to us. The busy round of eating, drinking, dressing, walking, visiting, buying, selling, talking, reading, must be gone through; and this remained to Augustine.

Had his wife been a whole woman, she might, as woman can, have done something to mend the broken threads. But Marie St. Clare could not even see that they had been broken. As before stated, she consisted of a fine figure, a pair of splendid eyes, and a hundred thousand dollars; and none of these ministered to a mind diseased. When Augustine, pale as death, pleaded sick-headache, she recommended hartshorn; when the paleness and headaches recurred, she said it was unfortunate for her, because he didn’t like going into company with her, and it seemed odd to go so much alone, when they were just married.

As the honeymoon gloss wore away, he discovered that a beautiful young woman, brought up to be caressed and waited on, might prove quite a hard mistress at home. Marie never had much capability of affection or sensibility, and the little she had was merged in a most intense unconscious selfishness,—the more hopeless from its quiet obtuseness. From infancy, servants had studied her caprices; the idea that they had feelings or rights had never dawned upon her. Her father, whose only child she was, had never denied her anything; so, entering life, beautiful, accomplished, and an heiress, she saw all the eligibles and non-eligibles sigh at her feet, and had no doubt Augustine was most fortunate in having obtained her. It is a mistake to suppose that a woman with no heart will be an easy creditor. There is not on earth a more merciless exactor of love than a thoroughly selfish woman; and the more unlovely she grows, the more jealously she exacts it. When St. Clare dropped off the gallantries which had flowed from the habitude of courtship, he found his sultana nothing ready to resign her slave. There were tears, poutings, small tempests, discontents, upbraidings. He, good-natured and self-indulgent, sought to buy off with presents and flatteries; and when Marie became mother to a beautiful daughter, he felt, for a time, something like tenderness awakened.

St. Clare’s mother had been a woman of uncommon elevation and purity; he gave his child her name, fondly fancying a reproduction of her image. This had been remarked with petulant jealousy by Marie, who regarded his devotion to the child with suspicion; all that was given to her seemed so much taken from herself. From the birth of the child, her health gradually sunk. A life of bodily and mental inaction,—the friction of ceaseless ennui and discontent,—with ordinary weakness of maternity,—in a few years changed the blooming belle into a faded, sickly woman, whose time was divided among a variety of fanciful diseases, and who considered herself, in every sense, the most ill-used person in existence.

Her complaints were endless; but her forte lay in sick-headache, confining her to her room perhaps three days in six. As, of course, all family arrangements fell to servants, St. Clare found his menage anything but comfortable. His only daughter was exceedingly delicate, and he feared that, with no one to look after her, her health might fall a sacrifice to her mother’s inefficiency. He had taken her to Vermont, and persuaded his cousin, Miss Ophelia St. Clare, to return with him to his southern residence; and they are now returning on this boat, where we introduce them.

And now, while the distant domes and spires of New Orleans rise to our view, there is yet time for an introduction to Miss Ophelia.

Whoever has travelled in New England will remember, in some cool village, the large farmhouse, with its clean-swept grassy yard, shaded by the massive sugar-maple; the air of order and stillness, of perpetuity, that seemed to breathe over the whole place. Nothing lost, or out of order; not a picket loose in the fence, not a particle of litter in the yard, with its clumps of lilac under the windows. Within, wide, clean rooms, where nothing ever seems to be doing or going to be done; everything forever rigidly in place; all household arrangements moving with the punctual exactness of the old clock in the corner. In the family keeping-room, the staid, respectable old book-case, with glass doors, where Rollin, Milton, Bunyan, and Scott’s Family Bible stand side by side in decorous order, with multitudes of other books, solemn and respectable. There are no servants; the lady in the snowy cap, with spectacles, sits sewing every afternoon among her daughters, as if nothing ever had been done, or were to be done,—she and her girls, in some long-forgotten fore part of the day, “did up the work,” and, for the rest of the time, probably, at all hours when you would see them, it is “done up.” The old kitchen floor never seems stained; tables, chairs, various utensils, never disordered; though meals are got there, washing and ironing there performed, and though pounds of butter and cheese somehow there come into existence.

On such a farm, in such a house and family, Miss Ophelia had spent a quiet existence of some forty-five years, when her cousin invited her to his southern mansion. The eldest of a large family, she was still considered, by father and mother, as one of “the children,” and the proposal that she should go to Orleans was momentous to the family circle. The old father took down the atlas and looked out the latitude and longitude, and read travels to make up his mind as to the country. The good mother inquired anxiously “if Orleans wasn’t an awful wicked place,” seeming to think it most equal to going among heathen.

It was known at the minister’s, the doctor’s, and at Miss Peabody’s milliner shop, that Ophelia St. Clare was “talking about” going away down to Orleans; and of course the whole village could do no less than help this important process. The minister, inclining to abolitionist views, doubted whether such a step might not encourage southerners; while the doctor, a stanch colonizationist, held that Miss Ophelia ought to go, to show the Orleans people that we don’t think hardly of them, after all; southern people needed encouraging. When it was fully known she had resolved to go, she was solemnly invited to tea by all friends and neighbors for a fortnight, and her plans duly canvassed. The dress-maker, who came in to help, acquired daily accessions of importance from the developments with regard to Miss Ophelia’s wardrobe. It was credibly ascertained that Squire Sinclare, as his name was commonly contracted, had counted out fifty dollars, and told her to buy any clothes she thought best; that two silk dresses and a bonnet had been sent for from Boston; and, what divided opinion as to propriety, there had been no such parasol seen in those parts; there were rumors of a hemstitched pocket-handkerchief,—even that one had lace all around; but this last point remains unsettled to this day.

Miss Ophelia, as you now behold her, stands in a shining brown linen travelling-dress, tall, square-formed, angular. Her face thin and rather sharp; the lips compressed, as if she made up her mind definitely on all subjects; the keen, dark eyes with a peculiarly searching, advised movement, travelling over everything, as if looking for something to take care of. All her movements were sharp, decided, energetic; and, though never much of a talker, her words were remarkably direct, and to the purpose, when she spoke. In habits, she was a living impersonation of order, method, and exactness. In punctuality, inevitable as a clock, inexorable as a railroad engine; holding in decided contempt anything of a contrary character.
