The “young un” heard all remarks with her habitual doleful air, only scanning with keen, furtive glances the ornaments the maid wore in her ears. When at last arrayed in decent, whole clothing, hair cropped short, Miss Ophelia, with some satisfaction, said she looked more Christian-like, and in her mind began to mature plans for her instruction. Sitting down, she began to question her.

“How old are you, Topsy?”

“Dun no, Missis,” said the image, with a grin that showed all her teeth.

“Don’t know how old you are? Didn’t anybody ever tell you? Who was your mother?”

“Never had none!” said the child, with another grin.

“Never had any mother? What do you mean? Where were you born?”

“Never was born!” persisted Topsy, with another goblin grin.

“You mustn’t answer me so, child; I’m not playing with you. Tell me where you were born, and who your father and mother were.”

“Never was born,” reiterated she, more emphatically; “never had no father nor mother, nor nothin’. I was raised by a speculator, with lots of others. Old Aunt Sue took car on us.”

“How long have you lived with your master and mistress?”

“Dun no, Missis.”

“Is it a year, or more, or less?”

“Dun no, Missis.”

“Have you ever heard anything about God, Topsy?”

The child looked bewildered, and grinned.

“Do you know who made you?”

“Nobody, as I knows on,” said the child, with a short laugh. Her eyes twinkled. “I spect I grow’d. Don’t think nobody never made me.”

“Do you know how to sew?” said Miss Ophelia, turning to something tangible.

“No, Missis.”

“What can you do?—what did you do for your master and mistress?”

“Fetch water, wash dishes, rub knives, and wait on folks.”

“Were they good to you?”

“Spect they was,” said the child, scanning Miss Ophelia cunningly.

St. Clare, leaning over the back of the chair, said,

“You find virgin soil there, Cousin; put in your own ideas,—you won’t find many to pull up.”

Miss Ophelia’s ideas of education were very set and definite,—New England of a century ago: to teach them to mind; to teach the catechism, sewing, and reading; and to whip them if they told lies. Whatever floods of light now shine, grandmothers raised some tolerably fair men and women under this regime. At all events, Miss Ophelia knew of nothing else; and applied her mind to her heathen with the best diligence she could.

The child was announced as Miss Ophelia’s girl; and, looked on with no gracious eye in the kitchen, was chiefly confined to her mistress’s chamber. With a self-sacrifice some readers will appreciate, Miss Ophelia resolved, instead of comfortably making her own bed, sweeping and dusting her chamber, as she had hitherto done in scorn of help, to condemn herself to instructing Topsy in these operations,—woe the day!

She took Topsy into her chamber the first morning, and solemnly began a course in the art and mystery of bed-making.

“Now, Topsy, I’m going to show you just how my bed is to be made. I am very particular about my bed. You must learn exactly how to do it.”

“Yes, ma’am,” says Topsy, with a deep sigh and a face of woful earnestness.

“This is the hem of the sheet,—this is the right side, and this the wrong;—will you remember?”

“Yes, ma’am,” with another sigh.

“The under sheet you must bring over the bolster,—so,—and tuck it clear down under the mattress, nice and smooth,—so. Do you see?”

“Yes, ma’am,” with profound attention.

“But the upper sheet must be brought down thus, and tucked firm and smooth at the foot,—the narrow hem at the foot.”

“Yes, ma’am,” said Topsy;—and what Miss Ophelia did not see was, that during the zeal of her manipulations, the young disciple had snatched a pair of gloves and a ribbon, slipped them into her sleeves, and stood with hands dutifully folded.

“Now, Topsy, let’s see you do this,” said Miss Ophelia, pulling off the clothes, and seating herself.

Topsy, with gravity and adroitness, went through the exercise to Miss Ophelia’s satisfaction. But an unlucky flutter of ribbon hung out of her sleeve, and caught Miss Ophelia’s eye. Instantly she pounced upon it.

“What’s this? You naughty, wicked child,—you’ve been stealing this!”

The ribbon was pulled out of Topsy’s sleeve; yet she was not the least disconcerted; she only looked at it with surprised innocence.

“Laws! why, that ar’s Miss Feely’s ribbon, an’t it? How could it a got caught in my sleeve?”

“Topsy, you naughty girl, don’t you tell me a lie,—you stole that ribbon!”

“Missis, I declar for ’t, I didn’t;—never seed it till dis yer blessed minnit.”

“Topsy,” said Miss Ophelia, “don’t you know it’s wicked to tell lies?”

“I never tell no lies, Miss Feely,” said Topsy, with virtuous gravity; “it’s jist the truth I’ve been a tellin now, and an’t nothin else.”

“Topsy, I shall have to whip you, if you tell lies so.”

“Laws, Missis, if you’s to whip all day, couldn’t say no other way,” said Topsy, beginning to blubber. “I never seed dat ar;—it must a got caught. Miss Feeley must have left it on de bed, and it got caught in de clothes, and so got in my sleeve.”

Miss Ophelia, indignant at the barefaced lie, caught and shook the child.

“Don’t you tell me that again!”

The shake brought a glove to the floor from the other sleeve.

“There, you!” said Miss Ophelia; “will you tell me now you didn’t steal the ribbon?”

Topsy confessed to the gloves, but persisted about the ribbon.

“Now, Topsy,” said Miss Ophelia, “if you’ll confess all about it, I won’t whip you this time.”

Thus adjured, Topsy confessed to ribbon and gloves, with woful protestations; and, being pressed further, added,

“Laws, Missis! I took Miss Eva’s red thing she wars on her neck.”

“You did, you naughty child!—What else?”

“I took dem yer-rings,—dem red ones,—out o’ de housemaid’s ears.”

“Go bring them to me this minute, both of ’em.”

“Laws, Missis! I can’t,—they ’s burnt up!”

“Burnt up!—what a story! Go get ’em, or I’ll whip you.”

Topsy, with loud protestations and tears, declared she could not. “They ’s burnt up,—they was.”

“What did you burn ’em for?”

“Cause I ’s wicked,—I is. I ’s mighty wicked, any how. I can’t help it.”

Just then Eva came innocently into the room, with the identical coral necklace on her neck.

“Why, Eva, where did you get your necklace?” said Miss Ophelia.

“Get it? Why, I’ve had it on all day,” said Eva. “Had it on yesterday, too; and, what is funny, Aunty, I had it on all night. I forgot to take it off.”

Miss Ophelia looked perfectly bewildered; the more so, as a housemaid came in that instant, with newly-ironed linen poised on her head, and the coral ear-drops shaking in her ears!

“I’m sure I can’t tell what to do with such a child!” said Miss Ophelia, in despair. “What in the world did you tell me you took those things for, Topsy?”

“Why, Missis said I must ’fess; and I couldn’t think o’ nothin’ else to ’fess,” said Topsy, rubbing her eyes.

“But I didn’t want you to confess things you didn’t do,” said Miss Ophelia; “that’s telling a lie, as much as the other.”

“Laws, now, is it?” said Topsy, with innocent wonder.

“There an’t any such thing as truth in that limb,” said the housemaid, indignantly. “If I was Mas’r St. Clare, I’d whip her till the blood run. I would,—I’d let her catch it!”

“No, no,” said Eva, with an air of command she could at times assume; “you mustn’t talk so. I can’t bear to hear it.”

“Miss Eva, you ’s so good, you don’t know nothin’ how to get along with niggers. There’s no way but to cut ’em well up,” muttered the girl.

“Don’t you say another word of that sort!” and the child’s eye flashed, her cheek deepened its color. The maid was cowed.

Eva stood looking at Topsy. There stood the two children, representatives of the two extremes of society: the fair, high-bred child, with golden head, deep eyes, spiritual brow, and prince-like movements; and her black, keen, subtle, cringing, yet acute neighbor. The Saxon, born of ages of cultivation; the Afric, born of ages of oppression.

Something of such thoughts struggled in Eva’s mind,—dim, instinctive. When Miss Ophelia expatiated on Topsy’s wickedness, the child looked perplexed and sorrowful, and said, sweetly,

“Poor Topsy, why need you steal? You’re going to be taken good care of now. I’m sure I’d rather give you anything of mine, than have you steal it.”

It was the first word of kindness the child had ever heard; the tone struck strangely on that wild heart, and a sparkle like a tear shone in the keen round eye; but it was followed by the short laugh and habitual grin. The ear that has never heard anything but abuse is incredulous of so heavenly a thing as kindness.

But what was to be done with Topsy? Miss Ophelia found the case a puzzler; her rules didn’t seem to apply. She resolved to take time to think of it; and, by way of gaining time, and in hopes of indefinite moral virtues supposed to be inherent in dark closets, she shut Topsy up in one, till she had arranged her ideas further.

“I don’t see,” said Miss Ophelia to St. Clare, “how I’m to manage that child, without whipping her.”

“Well, whip her, then, to your heart’s content; I’ll give you full power to do what you like.”

“Children always have to be whipped,” said Miss Ophelia; “I never heard of bringing them up without.”

“O, well, certainly,” said St. Clare; “do as you think best. Only I’ll make one suggestion: I’ve seen this child whipped with a poker, knocked down with shovel or tongs, whichever came handiest, &c.; and, seeing she is used to that style, I think your whippings will have to be pretty energetic, to make much impression.”

“What is to be done with her, then?” said Miss Ophelia.

“You have started a serious question,” said St. Clare. “What is to be done with a human being that can be governed only by the lash,—that fails? It’s a common state of things down here!”

“I’m sure I don’t know; I never saw such a child as this.”

“Such children are very common among us, and such men and women, too. How are they to be governed?”

“I can’t say,” said Miss Ophelia.

“Nor I,” said St. Clare. “The horrid cruelties and outrages that get into the papers,—such cases as Prue’s,—what do they come from? In many cases, a gradual hardening on both sides,—the owner growing more cruel, the servant more callous. Whipping and abuse are like laudanum; you have to double the dose as the sensibilities decline. I saw this very early when I became an owner; and I resolved never to begin, because I did not know when I should stop,—and I resolved, at least, to protect my own moral nature. The consequence is, my servants act like spoiled children; but I think that better than for us both to be brutalized together. You have talked much of responsibilities in educating, Cousin. I wanted you to try with one child, a specimen of thousands among us.”

“It is your system makes such children,” said Miss Ophelia.

“I know it; but they are made,—they exist,—and what is to be done with them?”

“Well, as it appears a duty, I shall persevere, and do the best I can,” said Miss Ophelia.

She instituted regular hours and employments, and undertook to teach her to read and sew.

In the former, the child was quick. She learned her letters as if by magic, and was soon able to read plain reading; but sewing was a more difficult matter. The creature was as lithe as a cat and as active as a monkey; the confinement of sewing was her abomination; so she broke her needles, threw them slyly out of the window or down in chinks; tangled, broke, and dirtied her thread; or, with a sly movement, threw a spool away altogether. Her motions were as quick as a practised conjurer’s, her command of face quite as great; and though Miss Ophelia could not help feeling so many accidents could not happen in succession, yet she could not detect her without a watchfulness which would leave her no time for anything else.

Topsy soon became noted. Her talent for every species of drollery, grimace, and mimicry,—for dancing, tumbling, climbing, singing, whistling, imitating every sound that hit her fancy,—seemed inexhaustible. In play-hours she invariably had every child in the house at her heels,—not excepting Miss Eva, who appeared fascinated by her wild diablerie, as a dove is sometimes charmed by a glittering serpent. Miss Ophelia was uneasy that Eva should fancy Topsy’s society, and implored St. Clare to forbid it.

“Poh! let the child alone,” said St. Clare. “Topsy will do her good.”

“But so depraved a child,—are you not afraid she will teach her mischief?”

“She can’t teach her mischief; she might teach it to some children, but evil rolls off Eva’s mind like dew off a cabbage-leaf,—not a drop sinks in.”

“Don’t be too sure,” said Miss Ophelia. “I’d never let a child of mine play with Topsy.”

“Well, your children needn’t; but mine may. If Eva could have been spoiled, it would have been done years ago.”

The upper servants at first despised Topsy; they soon altered their opinion. It was discovered that whoever cast an indignity on Topsy was sure to meet some inconvenient accident shortly after: a pair of ear-rings or some cherished trinket missing; an article of dress suddenly ruined; a stumble into a pail of hot water; a libation of slop deluging them from above in full gala dress. When investigation was made, there was nobody found to stand sponsor. Topsy was cited time and again; but always sustained her examinations with edifying innocence and gravity. Nobody doubted who did the things; but no scrap of direct evidence could be found, and Miss Ophelia was too just to proceed without it. The mischiefs were always so nicely timed, too, as further to shelter the aggressor,—vengeances chosen when the victims were in disgrace with their mistress, and any complaint would meet no sympathy. In short, Topsy made the household understand the propriety of letting her alone; and she was let alone.

She was smart and energetic in manual operations, learning everything taught with surprising quickness. With a few lessons, she did the proprieties of Miss Ophelia’s chamber in a way with which even that particular lady could find no fault. Mortal hands could not lay spread smoother, adjust pillows more accurately, sweep and dust and arrange more perfectly, than Topsy, when she chose,—but she didn’t very often choose. If, after three or four days of careful supervision, Miss Ophelia supposed Topsy had fallen into her way, could do without over-looking, and so went off, Topsy held a carnival of confusion for some hours. Instead of making the bed, she amused herself pulling off pillow-cases, butting her woolly head among the pillows, grotesquely ornamented with feathers; climbed the posts, hung head downward; flourished sheets and spreads all over; dressed the bolster in Miss Ophelia’s night-clothes, and enacted performances therewith,—singing, whistling, and making grimaces at herself in the looking-glass; in short, as Miss Ophelia phrased it, “raising Cain” generally.

On one occasion Miss Ophelia found Topsy with her very best scarlet India Canton crape shawl wound round her head for a turban, going on with rehearsals before the glass in great style,—Miss Ophelia having, with unheard-of carelessness, left the key for once in her drawer.

“Topsy!” she would say, at the end of patience, “what does make you act so?”

“Dunno, Missis,—I spects cause I ’s so wicked!”

“I don’t know what I shall do with you, Topsy.”

“Law, Missis, you must whip me; my old Missis allers whipped me. I an’t used to workin’ unless I gets whipped.”

“Why, Topsy, I don’t want to whip you. You can do well, if you’ve a mind to; what is the reason you won’t?”

“Laws, Missis, I ’s used to whippin’; I spects it’s good for me.”

Miss Ophelia tried the recipe, and Topsy made a terrible commotion, screaming, groaning and imploring; though, half an hour afterwards, roosted on some balcony projection, surrounded by admiring “young uns,” she expressed the utmost contempt of the affair.

“Law, Miss Feely whip!—wouldn’t kill a skeeter, her whippins. Oughter see how old Mas’r made de flesh fly; old Mas’r know’d how!”

Topsy made great capital of her own sins and enormities, considering them as something peculiarly distinguishing.

“Law, you niggers,” she would say, “does you know you ’s all sinners? Well, you is—everybody is. White folks is sinners, too,—Miss Feely says so; but I spects niggers is de biggest ones; but lor! ye an’t any on ye up to me. I ’s so awful wicked, there can’t nobody do nothin’ with me. I used to keep old Missis a swarin’ at me half de time. I spects I ’s de wickedest critter in de world;” and Topsy would cut a summerset, and come up brisk and shining on a higher perch, evidently pluming herself on the distinction.

Miss Ophelia busied herself on Sundays, teaching Topsy the catechism. Topsy had uncommon verbal memory, and committed with a fluency that encouraged her instructress.

“What good do you expect it is going to do her?” said St. Clare.

“Why, it always has done children good. It’s what children have to learn,” said Miss Ophelia.

“Understand it or not,” said St. Clare.

“O, children never understand it at the time; but, after they are grown up, it’ll come to them.”

“Mine hasn’t come to me yet,” said St. Clare, “though I’ll testify you put it into me pretty thoroughly when I was a boy.”

“Ah, you were always good at learning, Augustine. I used to have great hopes of you,” said Miss Ophelia.

“Well, haven’t you now?”

“I wish you were as good as you were when you were a boy, Augustine.”

“So do I, that’s a fact, Cousin. Well, go ahead and catechize Topsy; may be you’ll make out something yet.”

Topsy, who had stood like a black statue, hands decently folded, now, at a signal, went on:

“Our first parents, being left to the freedom of their own will, fell from the state wherein they were created.”

Topsy’s eyes twinkled; she looked inquiringly.

“What is it, Topsy?” said Miss Ophelia.

“Please, Missis, was dat ar state Kintuck?”

“What state, Topsy?”

“Dat state dey fell out of. I used to hear Mas’r tell how we came down from Kintuck.”

St. Clare laughed.

“You’ll have to give her a meaning, or she’ll make one,” said he. “There seems a theory of emigration suggested there.”

“O! Augustine, be still,” said Miss Ophelia; “how can I do anything if you will be laughing?”

“Well, I won’t disturb the exercises again, on my honor;” and St. Clare took his paper into the parlor, and sat down, till Topsy finished. They were all very well, only now and then she would transpose important words, and persist in the mistake; and St. Clare, after all his promises, took a wicked pleasure in these, calling Topsy to him to repeat the offending passages, in spite of Miss Ophelia’s remonstrances.

“How do you think I can do anything with the child, if you go on so, Augustine?” she would say.

“Well, it is too bad,—I won’t again; but I do like to hear the droll little image stumble over those big words!”

“But you confirm her in the wrong way.”

“What’s the odds? One word is as good as another to her.”

“You wanted me to bring her up right; and you ought to remember she is a reasonable creature, and be careful of your influence.”

“O, dismal! so I ought; but, as Topsy herself says, ‘I ’s so wicked!’”

In very much this way Topsy’s training proceeded, for a year or two,—Miss Ophelia worrying herself from day to day with her, as with a chronic plague, to whose inflictions one becomes accustomed, as to neuralgia.

St. Clare took the same kind of amusement in the child that a man might in the tricks of a parrot or pointer. Whenever her sins brought her into disgrace elsewhere, she took refuge behind his chair; and St. Clare, one way or other, would make peace. From him she got many a stray picayune, which she laid out in nuts and candies, and distributed with careless generosity to all the children; for Topsy, to do her justice, was good-natured and liberal, and only spiteful in self-defence. She is fairly introduced into our corps de ballet, and will figure, from time to time, in her turn, with other performers.

“The Grass Withereth—the Flower Fadeth”

Life passes, with us all, a day at a time; so it passed with our friend Tom, till two years were gone. Though parted from all his soul held dear, he was never wholly miserable; for, so well is the harp of human feeling strung, that nothing but a crash that breaks every string can wholly mar its harmony. The hours, as they glided, brought their alleviations; and Tom, reading in his one book of one who had “learned in whatsoever state he was, therewith to be content,” found that good and reasonable doctrine quietly shaping his habit of mind.

A letter, in due time, came from young Master George, in a brave, round hand that, Tom said, “might be read most acrost the room.” It brought home tidings,—that his wife, Aunt Chloe, had been hired to a confectioner in Louisville, and was laying by all her gains for his redemption; that all at the house were well; that his cabin stood shut, but was to be set forth with new ornaments when he came back. Tom thought it the most wonderful composition of modern times, carried it in his breast, and even took counsel with Eva whether it might not be framed; only, how to show both sides at once?

Meantime, the friendship between Tom and little Eva had grown with the child’s growth. He loved her as something frail and earthly, yet almost worshipped her as something heavenly and divine. He gazed on her as an Italian sailor on the image of the child Jesus,—with reverence and tenderness; and to humor her graceful fancies, to meet those thousand simple wants that invest childhood like a many-colored rainbow, was his chief delight. In the morning market his eyes were for flowers; the choicest peach or orange slipped into his pocket for her; and the sight that pleased him most was her sunny head at the gate, and her childish cry,—“Well, Uncle Tom, what have you got for me today?”

Nor was Eva less zealous in kind offices. Though a child, she was a beautiful reader. A fine musical ear, a quick poetic fancy, and an instinctive sympathy with what’s grand and noble, made her such a reader of the Bible as Tom had never before heard. She began to read for him; soon her own earnest nature wound itself about the majestic book, and it became dear to her for the strange yearnings it awoke. Most of all she loved the Revelations and the Prophecies,—their dim, wondrous imagery and fervent language, whose meaning she and her simple friend questioned vainly, yet felt alike. They spoke of a glory to be revealed,—a wondrous something yet to come,—and though not understood, they were not profitless; for the soul, a trembling stranger between two dim eternities, must yearn toward the unknown, and the cloudy pillar of inspiration has voices that answer to her expecting nature.

At this time, the whole St. Clare establishment was removed to their villa on Lake Pontchartrain. The heats had driven all who could to the shore and its cool sea-breezes. St. Clare’s villa, an East Indian cottage with light bamboo verandahs, opened into gardens where tropical bloom ran down by winding paths to the lake,—its silvery water rising and falling in sunbeams, never for an hour the same, yet every hour more beautiful.

It was one of those intensely golden sunsets that turns the whole horizon to a blaze, and makes the water another sky. White-winged vessels glided like spirits; little golden stars trembled in the glow. Tom and Eva sat in a mossy arbor at the foot of the garden. It was Sunday evening; Eva’s Bible lay open on her knee. She read,—“And I saw a sea of glass, mingled with fire.”

“Tom,” said Eva, suddenly stopping, and pointing to the lake, “there ’t is.”

“What, Miss Eva?”

“Don’t you see,—there?” She pointed to the glassy water, that, as it rose and fell, reflected the golden sky. “There’s a ‘sea of glass, mingled with fire.’”

“True enough, Miss Eva,” said Tom; and he sang, softly,

“O, had I the wings of the morning,
    I’d fly away to Canaan’s shore;
Bright angels should convey me home,
    To the new Jerusalem.”

“Where do you suppose new Jerusalem is, Uncle Tom?” said Eva.

“O, up in the clouds, Miss Eva.”

“Then I think I see it,” said Eva. “Look in those clouds!—they look like great gates of pearl; and you can see beyond them—far, far off—it’s all gold. Tom, sing about ‘spirits bright.’”

Tom sang the words of the hymn,

“I see a band of spirits bright,
    That taste the glories there;
They all are robed in spotless white,
    And conquering palms they bear.”

“Uncle Tom, I’ve seen them,” said Eva.

If she had told him she had been to heaven, he would not have been surprised.

“They come to me sometimes in my sleep, those spirits,” she murmured; and her eyes grew dreamy, and she hummed, low,

“They are all robed in spotless white,
    And conquering palms they bear.”

“Uncle Tom,” said Eva, “I’m going there.”

“Where, Miss Eva?”

She rose, and pointed her little hand to the sky. The glow of evening lit her golden hair and flushed cheek with an unearthly radiance, and her eyes were bent earnestly upward.

“I’m going there,” she said, “to the spirits bright, Tom; I’m going, before long.”

The faithful old heart felt a sudden thrust. How often, within six months, had he noticed those little hands grow thinner, the skin more transparent, the breath shorter; how soon she flagged now at play. He had heard Miss Ophelia speak often of a cough all her medicaments could not cure; and even now the fever-rose burned in cheek and hand. Yet never till now had the thought come home.

Has there ever been a child like Eva? Yes,—but their names are on grave-stones, and their smiles, their heavenly eyes, their singular words and ways, are among the buried treasures of yearning hearts. When you see that deep spiritual light in the eye,—when the little soul speaks with a wisdom not of childhood,—hope not to keep that child; for the seal of heaven is on it, and the light of immortality looks out.

The colloquy was broken by a hasty call from Miss Ophelia, St. Clare’s northern cousin.

“Eva—Eva!—why, child, the dew is falling; you mustn’t be out there!”

They hastened in.

Miss Ophelia was old and skilled in nursing. She knew well the first guileful footsteps of that soft, insidious disease which, while no fibre seems broken, seals the fairest irrevocably for death. She noted the slight, dry cough, the daily brightening cheek; nor could fever’s lustre deceive her.

She tried to communicate her fears to St. Clare, Eva’s father; but he threw them back with a petulance unlike his careless good-humor.

“Don’t be croaking, Cousin,—I hate it!” he said. “Don’t you see the child is only growing? Children always lose strength when they grow fast.”

“But she has that cough!”

“O! nonsense of that cough!—it is not anything. She has taken a little cold, perhaps.”

“Well, it was just so—” she began.

“O! stop these hobgoblin’ nurse legends. You old hands get so wise a child cannot cough or sneeze but you see ruin at hand. Take care of her; keep her from the night air; don’t let her play too hard; she’ll do well enough.”

So he said; but he grew nervous and restless. He watched Eva feverishly, repeating, with a painful frequency, that “the child was quite well,” that there wasn’t anything in that cough; it was only some little stomach affection such as children often had. He kept by her more than before, took her often to ride, brought home receipts and strengthening mixtures,—“not,” he said, “that the child needed it; but then it would not do her any harm.”

If truth must be told, what struck the deepest pang was the daily maturing of the child’s mind and feelings. Still a child in fanciful grace, she often dropped words of such reach of thought and strange, unworldly wisdom that they seemed inspiration. At such times St. Clare would feel a sudden thrill, clasp her in his arms, as if that fond hold could save her, and his heart rise wild with the determination to keep her, never to let her go.

Her whole soul seemed absorbed in works of love and kindness. She had always been impulsively generous; now there was a touching, womanly thoughtfulness that all observed. She still loved to be with Topsy—Miss Ophelia’s little pupil—and the various colored children; but she was rather a spectator than an actor in their plays. She would sit half an hour laughing at Topsy’s odd tricks; then a shadow would pass across her face; her eyes grew misty, and her thoughts were afar.

“Mamma,” she said, suddenly, to Marie, her mother, one day, “why don’t we teach our servants to read?”

“What a question, child! People never do.”

“Why don’t they?” said Eva.

“Because it is no use for them to read. It don’t help them to work any better, and they are not made for anything else.”

“But they ought to read the Bible, mamma, to learn God’s will.”

“O! they can get that read to them all they need.”

“It seems to me, mamma, the Bible is for every one to read themselves. They need it many times when nobody is by to read.”

“Eva, you are an odd child,” said her mother.

“Miss Ophelia has taught Topsy to read,” continued Eva.

“Yes; and you see how much good it does. Topsy is the worst creature I ever saw!”

“Here’s poor Mammy,” said Eva,—her nurse, who loved the book so and wished she could read. “What will she do when I can’t read to her?”

Marie, busy turning over a drawer, answered, “Of course, by and by, Eva, you will have other things to think of besides reading the Bible round to servants. Not but what it’s proper; I’ve done it myself, when I had health. But when you come to be dressing, and going into company, you won’t have time. See here!” She lifted a casket. “These jewels I’m going to give you when you come out. I wore them to my first ball. I can tell you, Eva, I made a sensation.”

Eva took the case and lifted a diamond necklace. Her large, thoughtful eyes rested on it; plainly her thoughts were elsewhere.

“How sober you look, child!” said Marie.

“Are these worth a great deal of money, mamma?”

“To be sure. Your father sent to France for them. They are worth a small fortune.”

“I wish I had them,” said Eva, “to do what I pleased with!”

“What would you do with them?”

“I’d sell them, and buy a place in the free states, and take all our people there, and hire teachers, to teach them to read and write.”

Marie laughed.

“Set up a boarding-school! Wouldn’t you teach them to play on the piano, and paint on velvet?”

“I’d teach them to read their own Bible, and write their own letters, and read letters that are written to them,” said Eva, steadily. “I know, mamma, it comes very hard on them that they can’t do these things. Tom feels it—Mammy does—a great many do. I think it’s wrong.”

“Come, come, Eva; you are only a child! You don’t know anything about these things,” said Marie. “Besides, your talking makes my head ache.”

Eva stole away; and after that, she assiduously gave her nurse reading lessons.

Foreshadowings


Two days after her young cousin left, Eva, who had been stimulated by his visit beyond her strength, began to fail rapidly. St. Clare at last called in medical advice,—a thing from which he had always shrunk, because it admitted an unwelcome truth. For a day or two she was so unwell as to be confined to the house; and the doctor was called.

Marie St. Clare had taken no notice of the child’s gradually decaying health, because she was completely absorbed in studying out two or three new forms of disease to which she believed she herself was a victim. It was the first principle of Marie’s creed that nobody ever was or could be so great a sufferer as herself; therefore she indignantly repelled any suggestion that any one around her could be sick.

Miss Ophelia had several times tried to awaken her maternal fears.

“I don’t see as anything ails the child,” said Marie; “she runs about, and plays.”

“But she has a cough.”

“Cough! you don’t need to tell me about a cough. I’ve always been subject to a cough, all my days. When I was of Eva’s age, they thought I was in a consumption. Night after night, the nurse used to sit up with me. O! Eva’s cough is not anything.”

“But she gets weak, and is short-breathed.”

“Law! I’ve had that, years and years;—it’s only a nervous affection.”

“But she sweats so, nights!”

“Well, I have, these ten years. Very often, night after night, my clothes will be wringing wet,—there won’t be a dry thread in my night-clothes; and the sheets will be so that the nurse has to hang them up to dry! Eva doesn’t sweat anything like that!”

But now that Eva was visibly prostrated, and a doctor called, Marie all at once took a new turn.

“She knew it,” she said; “she always felt it, that she was destined to be the most miserable of mothers. Here she was, with her wretched health, and her only darling child going down to the grave before her eyes;”—and she routed up servants nights, and rumpussed and scolded, with more energy than ever, on the strength of this new misery.

“My dear Marie, don’t talk so!” said St. Clare. “You ought not to give up the case so, at once.”

“You have not a mother’s feelings! You never could understand me!—you don’t now.”

“It’s true,” said St. Clare, “that Eva is very delicate; that she has grown so rapidly as to exhaust her strength; and that her situation is critical. But just now she is only prostrated by the heat and by the excitement and exertions she made. The physician says there is room for hope.”

“Well, of course, if you can look on the bright side, pray do; it’s a mercy if people haven’t sensitive feelings. I wish I could be as easy as the rest of you!”

And the “rest of them” had good reason to breathe the same prayer; for Marie paraded her new misery as apology for all sorts of inflictions. Poor Eva heard some of these speeches, and nearly cried her little eyes out, in pity for her mamma, and in sorrow that she should make her so much distress.

In a week or two, there was a great improvement of symptoms,—one of those deceitful lulls, by which her inexorable disease so often beguiles the anxious heart. Eva’s step was again in the garden, in the balconies; she played and laughed again; and her father, in a transport, declared they should soon have her as hearty as anybody. Miss Ophelia and the physician alone felt no encouragement from this illusive truce. There was one other heart, too, that felt the same certainty, and that was the little heart of Eva.

What is it that sometimes speaks in the soul so calmly, so clearly, that its earthly time is short? Be it what it may, it rested in the heart of Eva,—a calm, sweet, prophetic certainty that Heaven was near; calm as the light of sunset, sweet as the bright stillness of autumn. There her little heart reposed, only troubled by sorrow for those who loved her so dearly.

Though life was unfolding before her with every brightness that love and wealth could give, she had no regret for herself in dying. In that book which she and her simple old friend had read so much together, she had seen and taken to her young heart the image of One who loved the little child; and, as she gazed and mused, He had ceased to be an image and a picture of the distant past, and come to be a living, all-surrounding reality. His love enfolded her childish heart with more than mortal tenderness; and it was to Him, she said, she was going, and to his home.

But her heart yearned with sad tenderness for all that she was to leave behind. Her father most,—for she had an instinctive perception that she was more in his heart than any other. She loved her mother because she was so loving a creature; and all the selfishness she had seen in her only saddened and perplexed her; for she had a child’s implicit trust that her mother could not do wrong. She felt, too, for those fond, faithful servants, to whom she was as daylight and sunshine. The things she had witnessed of the evils of the system under which they were living had fallen, one by one, into the depths of her thoughtful, pondering heart. She had vague longings to do something for them,—to bless and save not only them, but all in their condition,—longings that contrasted sadly with the feebleness of her little frame.

“Uncle Tom,” she said, one day, when she was reading to her friend, “I can understand why Jesus wanted to die for us.”

“Why, Miss Eva?”

“Because I’ve felt so, too.”

“What is it, Miss Eva?—I don’t understand.”

“I can’t tell you; but, when I saw those poor creatures on the boat,—you know, when you came up,—some had lost their mothers, and some their husbands, and some mothers cried for their little children;—and when I heard about poor Prue,—oh, wasn’t that dreadful!—and a great many other times, I’ve felt that I would be glad to die, if my dying could stop all this misery. I would die for them, Tom, if I could,” said the child, earnestly, laying her little thin hand on his.

Tom looked at the child with awe; and, when she, hearing her father’s voice, glided away, he wiped his eyes many times, as he looked after her.

“It’s jest no use tryin’ to keep Miss Eva here,” he said to the nurse, whom he met a moment after. “She’s got the Lord’s mark in her forehead.”

Late in the afternoon, St. Clare had called Eva to show a little statuette he had been buying for her; but, as she came tripping up the verandah steps, the rays of the sun formed a kind of glory behind her,—white dress, golden hair, glowing cheeks, eyes unnaturally bright with the slow fever that burned in her veins. Her appearance impressed him suddenly and painfully. There is a kind of beauty so intense, yet so fragile, that we cannot bear to look at it. He folded her suddenly in his arms, and almost forgot what he was going to tell her.

“Eva, dear, you are better now-a-days,—are you not?”

“Papa,” said Eva, with sudden firmness, “I’ve had things I wanted to say to you, a great while. I want to say them now, before I get weaker.”

St. Clare trembled as Eva seated herself in his lap. She laid her head on his bosom.

“It’s all no use, papa, to keep it to myself any longer. The time is coming that I am going to leave you. I am going, and never to come back!” and Eva sobbed.

“O, now, my dear little Eva!” said St. Clare, trembling as he spoke, but speaking cheerfully, “you’ve got nervous and low-spirited; you mustn’t indulge such gloomy thoughts. See here, I’ve bought a statuette for you!”

“No, papa,” said Eva, putting it gently away, “don’t deceive yourself!—I am not any better, I know it perfectly well,—and I am going, before long. I am not nervous,—I am not low-spirited. If it were not for you, papa, and my friends, I should be perfectly happy. I want to go,—I long to go!”

“Why, dear child, what has made your poor little heart so sad? You have had everything, to make you happy, that could be given you.”

“I had rather be in heaven; though, only for my friends’ sake, I would be willing to live. There are a great many things here that make me sad, that seem dreadful to me; I had rather be there; but I don’t want to leave you,—it almost breaks my heart!”

“What makes you sad, and seems dreadful, Eva?”

“O, things that are done, and done all the time. I feel sad for our poor people; they love me dearly, and they are all good and kind to me. I wish, papa, they were all free.”

“Why, Eva, child, don’t you think they are well enough off now?”

“O, but, papa, if anything should happen to you, what would become of them? There are very few men like you, papa. And then, think of poor old Prue’s owners! What horrid things people do, and can do!” and Eva shuddered.

“My dear child, you are too sensitive. I’m sorry I ever let you hear such stories.”

“O, that’s what troubles me, papa. You want me to live so happy, and never to have any pain,—never suffer anything,—not even hear a sad story,—when other poor creatures have nothing but pain and sorrow, all their lives;—it seems selfish. I ought to know such things, I ought to feel about them! Such things always sunk into my heart; they went down deep. Papa, isn’t there any way to have all slaves made free?”

“That’s a difficult question, dearest. There’s no doubt that this way is a very bad one; I do myself heartily wish that there were not a slave in the land; but, then, I don’t know what is to be done about it!”

“Papa, you are such a good man, and so noble, and kind, and you always have a way of saying things that is so pleasant,—couldn’t you go all round and try to persuade people to do right about this? When I am dead, papa, then you will think of me, and do it for my sake. I would do it, if I could.”

“When you are dead, Eva,” said St. Clare, passionately. “O, child, don’t talk to me so! You are all I have on earth.”

“Poor old Prue’s child was all that she had,—and yet she had to hear it crying, and she couldn’t help it! Papa, these poor creatures love their children as much as you do me. O! do something for them! There’s our good nurse loves her children; I’ve seen her cry when she talked about them. And Tom loves his children; and it’s dreadful, papa, that such things are happening, all the time!”

“There, there, darling,” said St. Clare, soothingly; “only don’t distress yourself,—don’t talk of dying,—and I will do anything you wish.”

“And promise me, dear father, that Tom shall have his freedom as soon as”—she stopped, and said, in a hesitating tone—“I am gone!”

“Yes, dear, I will do anything in the world,—anything you could ask me to.”

“Dear papa,” said the child, laying her burning cheek against his, “how I wish we could go together!”

“Where, dearest?” said St. Clare.

“To our Saviour’s home; it’s so sweet and peaceful there,—it is all so loving there!” The child spoke unconsciously, as of a place where she had often been. “Don’t you want to go, papa?” she said.

St. Clare drew her closer to him, but was silent.

“You will come to me,” said the child, speaking in a voice of calm certainty which she often used unconsciously.

“I shall come after you. I shall not forget you.”

The shadows of the solemn evening closed round them deeper and deeper, as St. Clare sat silently holding the little frail form to his bosom. He saw no more the deep eyes, but the voice came over him as a spirit voice; and, as in a sort of judgment vision, his whole past life rose in a moment before his eyes: his mother’s prayers and hymns; his own early yearnings and aspirings for good; and, between them and this hour, years of worldliness and scepticism, and what man calls respectable living. We can think much, very much, in a moment.

As it grew darker, he took his child to her bed-room; and, when she was prepared for rest, he sent away the attendants, and rocked her in his arms, and sung to her till she was asleep.

The Little Evangelist

It was Sunday afternoon. St. Clare lay on a bamboo lounge in the verandah, solacing himself with a cigar. Marie reclined on a sofa, opposite the window, closely secluded, under an awning of transparent gauze, from the outrages of the mosquitos, languidly holding an elegantly bound prayer-book, and taking a succession of short naps with it open in her hand.

Miss Ophelia, who had hunted up a small Methodist meeting within riding distance, had gone out, with Uncle Tom as driver; and Eva had accompanied them.

“I say, Augustine,” said Marie, after dozing a while, “I must send to the city after my old Doctor Posey; I’m sure I’ve got the complaint of the heart.”

“Well; why need you send for him? This doctor that attends Eva seems skilful.”

“I would not trust him in a critical case,” said Marie. “My distress about Eva, and the exertions I have made with that dear child, have developed what I have long suspected.”

“If it’s particularly agreeable to you to have heart disease, why, I’ll try and maintain you have it,” said St. Clare.

A carriage drove up before the verandah, and Eva and Miss Ophelia alighted. Miss Ophelia marched straight to her chamber to put away her bonnet and shawl, as was always her manner, while Eva, at St. Clare’s call, sat on his knee, telling of the services.

They soon heard loud exclamations from Miss Ophelia’s room, and violent reproof addressed to somebody.

“What new witchcraft has Tops been brewing?” asked St. Clare. “That commotion is of her raising, I’ll be bound!”

In a moment Miss Ophelia, in high indignation, came dragging the culprit along.

“Come out here, now!” she said. “I will tell your master!”

“What’s the case now?” asked Augustine.

“The case is, that I cannot be plagued with this child any longer! It’s past all bearing; flesh and blood cannot endure it! Here, I locked her up, and gave her a hymn to study; and what does she do, but spy out where I put my key, and has gone to my bureau, and got a bonnet-trimming, and cut it all to pieces to make dolls’ jackets! I never saw anything like it, in my life!”

“I told you, Cousin,” said Marie, “that you’d find out these creatures can’t be brought up without severity. If I had my way, now, I’d send that child out, and have her thoroughly whipped; I’d have her whipped till she couldn’t stand!”

“I don’t doubt it,” said St. Clare. “Tell me of the lovely rule of woman! I never saw above a dozen women that wouldn’t half kill a horse, or a servant, either, if they had their own way with them!—let alone a man.”

“There is no use in this shilly-shally way of yours, St. Clare!” said Marie. “Cousin is a woman of sense, and she sees it now, as plain as I do.”

“I wouldn’t have the child treated so, for the world,” said Miss Ophelia, though her housekeeper’s indignation had been pretty actively roused. “But I am sure, Augustine, I don’t know what to do. I’ve taught and taught; I’ve talked till I’m tired; I’ve whipped her; I’ve punished her in every way I can think of, and she’s just what she was at first.”

“Come here, Tops, you monkey!” said St. Clare.

Topsy came up; her round, hard eyes glittering and blinking with a mixture of apprehensiveness and their usual odd drollery.

“What makes you behave so?” said St. Clare, amused in spite of himself.

“Spects it’s my wicked heart,” said Topsy, demurely; “Miss Feely says so.”

“Don’t you see how much Miss Ophelia has done for you? She says she has done everything she can think of.”

“Lor, yes, Mas’r! old Missis used to say so, too. She whipped me a heap harder, and used to pull my har, and knock my head agin the door; but it didn’t do me no good! I spects, if they ’s to pull every spear o’ har out o’ my head, it wouldn’t do no good, neither,—I ’s so wicked! Laws! I ’s nothin but a nigger, no ways!”

“Well, I shall have to give her up,” said Miss Ophelia; “I can’t have that trouble any longer.”

“Well, I’d just like to ask one question,” said St. Clare. “Why, if your Gospel is not strong enough to save one heathen child, that you can have at home here, all to yourself, what’s the use of sending one or two poor missionaries off with it among thousands of just such? I suppose this child is about a fair sample of what thousands of your heathen are.”

Miss Ophelia did not immediately answer; and Eva, who had stood a silent spectator, made a silent sign to Topsy to follow her. They went into a little glass-room at the corner of the verandah, which St. Clare used as a reading-room.

“What’s Eva going about, now?” said St. Clare. He lifted a curtain that covered the glass-door, and, laying his finger on his lips, made a silent gesture to Miss Ophelia to look. There sat the two children on the floor, side faces towards them: Topsy with her usual air of careless drollery; opposite, Eva, her whole face fervent with feeling, tears in her large eyes.

“What does make you so bad, Topsy? Why won’t you try and be good? Don’t you love anybody, Topsy?”

“Donno nothing ’bout love; I loves candy and sich, that’s all,” said Topsy.

“But you love your father and mother?”

“Never had none, ye know. I telled ye that, Miss Eva.”

“O, I know,” said Eva, sadly; “but hadn’t you any brother, or sister, or aunt, or—”

“No, none on ’em,—never had nothing nor nobody.”

“But, Topsy, if you’d only try to be good, you might—”

“Couldn’t never be nothin’ but a nigger, if I was ever so good,” said Topsy. “If I could be skinned, and come white, I’d try then.”

“But people can love you, if you are black, Topsy. Miss Ophelia would love you, if you were good.”

Topsy gave the short, blunt laugh that expressed her incredulity.

“Don’t you think so?” said Eva.

“No; she can’t bar me, ’cause I’m a nigger!—she’d ’s soon have a toad touch her! There can’t nobody love niggers, and niggers can’t do nothin’! I don’t care,” said Topsy, beginning to whistle.

“O, Topsy, poor child, I love you!” said Eva, with a sudden burst, laying her little thin, white hand on Topsy’s shoulder. “I love you, because you haven’t had any father, or mother, or friends;—because you’ve been a poor, abused child! I love you, and I want you to be good. I am very unwell, Topsy, and I think I shan’t live a great while; and it really grieves me, to have you be so naughty. I wish you would try to be good, for my sake;—it’s only a little while I shall be with you.”

The round, keen eyes of the black child were overcast with tears; large, bright drops fell on the little white hand. She laid her head down between her knees, and wept and sobbed; while the beautiful child, bending over her, looked like some bright angel stooping to reclaim a sinner.

“Poor Topsy!” said Eva, “don’t you know that Jesus loves all alike? He is just as willing to love you, as me. He loves you just as I do,—only more, because he is better. He will help you to be good; and you can go to Heaven at last, and be an angel forever, just as much as if you were white. Only think of it, Topsy!—you can be one of those spirits bright, Uncle Tom sings about.”

“O, dear Miss Eva, dear Miss Eva!” said the child; “I will try, I will try; I never did care nothin’ about it before.”

St. Clare dropped the curtain. “It puts me in mind of mother,” he said to Miss Ophelia. “It is true what she told me; if we want to give sight to the blind, we must be willing to do as Christ did,—call them to us, and put our hands on them.”

“I’ve always had a prejudice against negroes,” said Miss Ophelia, low. “And it’s a fact, I never could bear to have that child touch me; but, I don’t think she knew it.”

“Trust any child to find that out,” said St. Clare. “There’s no keeping it from them. I believe that all the trying in the world to benefit a child, and all the substantial favors you can do them, will never excite one emotion of gratitude, while that feeling of repugnance remains in the heart;—it’s a queer kind of a fact,—but so it is.”

“I don’t know how I can help it,” said Miss Ophelia; “they are disagreeable to me,—this child in particular,—how can I help feeling so?”

“Eva does, it seems.”

“Well, she’s so loving! After all, though, she’s no more than Christ-like,” said Miss Ophelia. “I wish I were like her. She might teach me a lesson.”

“It wouldn’t be the first time a little child had been used to instruct an old disciple, if it were so,” said St. Clare.

Death

Weep not for those whom the veil of the tomb,
In life’s early morning, hath hid from our eyes.

Eva’s bed-room was a spacious apartment, opening, like all the rooms, upon the broad verandah. One door communicated with her father and mother’s chamber; the other, with Miss Ophelia’s. St. Clare had gratified his own eye and taste in furnishing it for her—rose-colored and white muslin draperies; a Paris matting, bordered with rose-buds; light bamboo bedstead, chairs, and lounges, in graceful patterns; above the bed an alabaster bracket with an angel, wings drooped, holding forth a crown of myrtle; over all, gauzy rose-striped curtains, to bar the mosquitos. A little bamboo table, with a Parian lily-vase always filled; an alabaster writing-stand for her childish efforts; on the marble mantle a fair statuette of Jesus receiving little children, with marble vases on either hand, for which it was Tom’s pride to bring morning bouquets. Paintings of children smiled from the walls. Nowhere could the eye turn without images of childhood, of beauty, and of peace; and her little eyes, when they opened in the morning light, always fell upon something that turned the heart to soothing and beautiful thoughts.

The deceitful strength which had borne her awhile was passing. More seldom was her light step heard in the verandah, and oftener she was found reclined upon a little lounge by the open window, her large, deep eyes fixed on the rising and falling of the lake.

One afternoon, thus reclining, her Bible half open, her transparent fingers lying listlessly between the leaves, she heard, in the verandah, her mother’s voice in sharp tones.

“What now, you baggage!—what new piece of mischief? You’ve been picking the flowers, hey?” A smart slap followed.

“Law, Missis! they’s for Miss Eva,” came the answer, in a voice she knew—Topsy’s.

“Miss Eva! A pretty excuse!—you suppose she wants your flowers, you good-for-nothing nigger! Get along with you!”

Eva started, and, in a moment, was in the verandah.

“O, don’t, mother! I should like the flowers; do give them to me; I want them!”

“Why, Eva, your room is full now.”

“I can’t have too many,” said Eva. “Topsy, bring them here.”

Topsy came up with unwonted hesitation and bashfulness, unlike her eldritch boldness. It was a singular bouquet—a scarlet geranium and a single white japonica, with glossy leaves; tied with a studied eye to contrast.

“It’s a beautiful bouquet!” said Eva. “Topsy, you arrange flowers very prettily. Here is a vase I haven’t any flowers for. I wish you’d arrange something every day for it.”

“Well, that’s odd!” said Marie. “What in the world do you want that for?”

“Never mind, mamma; you’d as lief as not Topsy should do it,—had you not?”

“Of course, anything you please, dear! Topsy, you hear your young mistress;—see that you mind.”

Topsy courtesied shortly, and turned away; and Eva saw a tear roll down the dark cheek.

“You see, mamma, I knew poor Topsy wanted to do something for me,” said Eva.

“O, nonsense! it’s only because she likes mischief. She knows she mustn’t pick flowers; so she does it. But, if you fancy to have her pluck them, so be it.”

“Mamma, I think Topsy is different. She’s trying to be a good girl.”

“She’ll have to try a good while before she gets to be good,” said Marie, carelessly.

“Well, you know, mamma, poor Topsy! everything has always been against her.”

“Not since she’s been here, I’m sure. If she hasn’t been talked to, and preached to, and everything done!—and she’s just so ugly, and always will be; you can’t make anything of the creature!”

“But, mamma, it’s so different, to be brought up as I’ve been—with so many friends, so many things to make me good and happy—than to be brought up as she has been, all the time, till she came here!”

“Most likely,” said Marie, yawning. “Dear me, how hot it is!”

“Mamma, you believe, don’t you, that Topsy could become an angel, as well as any of us, if she were a Christian?”

“Topsy! what a ridiculous idea! Nobody but you would think of it. I suppose she could, though.”

“But, mamma, isn’t God her Father as much as ours? Isn’t Jesus her Saviour?”

“Well, that may be. I suppose God made everybody. Where is my smelling-bottle?”

“It’s such a pity—oh! such a pity!” said Eva, looking out on the lake, half to herself.

“What’s a pity?” said Marie.

“That any one who could be a bright angel, and live with angels, should go all down, down, down, and nobody help them!—oh dear!”

“Well, we can’t help it; it’s no use worrying, Eva. We ought to be thankful for our own advantages.”

“I hardly can be,” said Eva; “I’m so sorry for poor folks that haven’t any.”

“That’s odd enough,” said Marie. “I’m sure my religion makes me thankful for my advantages.”

“Mamma,” said Eva, “I want to have some of my hair cut off,—a good deal of it.”

“What for?”

“I want to give some away to my friends, while I am able to give it to them myself. Won’t you ask aunty to come and cut it for me?”

Miss Ophelia came with her scissors; and St. Clare entered with fruit.

“Papa, I just want aunty to cut off some of my hair;—there’s too much, and it makes my head hot. Besides, I want to give some of it away.”

“Take care,—don’t spoil the looks of it!” said he. “Cut underneath; Eva’s curls are my pride.”

“O, papa!” said Eva, sadly.

“Yes; and I want them kept handsome against the time I take you up to your uncle’s, to see Cousin Henrique,” said St. Clare, gayly.

“I shall never go there, papa;—I am going to a better country. O, do believe me! Don’t you see, papa, I get weaker every day?”

“Why do you insist that I shall believe such a cruel thing, Eva?” said her father.

“Only because it is true, papa; and, if you will believe it now, perhaps you will feel about it as I do.”

He stood gloomily eying the long, beautiful curls, as, one by one, they were laid in her lap. She raised them, twined them round her thin fingers, and looked, from time to time, anxiously at him.

“It’s just what I’ve been foreboding!” said Marie. “It has preyed upon my health, bringing me downward to the grave, though nobody regards it. I have seen this, long. St. Clare, you will see, after a while, that I was right.”

“Which will afford you great consolation, no doubt!” said St. Clare, with a dry bitterness.

Eva’s clear, comprehending gaze went from one to the other, as of a soul half loosed from earth. She beckoned her father; he sat down by her.

“Papa, my strength fades away every day, and I know I must go. There are things I must say and do; and you are so unwilling I should speak. But it must come; there’s no putting it off. Do be willing I should speak now!”

“My child, I am willing!” said St. Clare, covering his eyes with one hand, and taking hers with the other.

“Then, I want to see all our people together. I have some things I must say to them.”

Miss Ophelia sent; soon they were all convened. Eva lay back upon her pillows; her hair hung loosely; the crimson of her cheek contrasted painfully with the intense whiteness of face and slender hands; her great, soul-like eyes fixed earnestly upon every one. The spiritual face, the severed locks lying by her, the father’s averted head, Marie’s sobs—these smote at once upon the feelings of a susceptible race. They sighed, shook their heads; there was a deep silence like that of a funeral.

“I sent for you all, my dear friends,” said Eva, “because I love you. I love you all; and I have something to say to you which I want you always to remember. I am going to leave you. In a few more weeks you will see me no more—”

A burst of groans and sobs broke from all; her slender voice was lost. She waited, and then, with a tone that checked them,—

“If you love me, you must not interrupt me so. Listen. I want to speak to you about your souls. Many of you, I am afraid, are very careless. You are thinking only of this world. I want you to remember there is a beautiful world where Jesus is. I am going there; and you can go there. It is for you as much as for me. But, if you want to go there, you must not live idle, careless, thoughtless lives. You must be Christians; you must remember that each one of you can become angels, and be angels forever. If you want to be Christians, Jesus will help you. You must pray to him; you must read—”

She checked herself; a piteous look came into her face.

“O dear! you can’t read—poor souls!” She hid her face and sobbed; their smothered sobs answered from the floor where many knelt.

“Never mind,” she said, raising her face, smiling through tears. “I have prayed for you; and I know Jesus will help you, even if you can’t read. Try to do the best you can; pray every day; ask Him to help you; and get the Bible read to you whenever you can; and I think I shall see you all in heaven.”

“Amen,” murmured Tom and some of the elder ones; the younger, wholly overcome, sobbed with bowed heads.

“I know you all love me.”

“Yes; oh, yes! indeed we do! Lord bless her!” broke involuntarily from many lips.

“Yes, I know it! There isn’t one of you that hasn’t always been very kind to me; and I want to give you something, that, when you look at it, you shall always remember me. I’m going to give all of you a curl of my hair; and, when you look at it, think that I loved you, and am gone to heaven; and that I want to see you all there.”

They gathered about her, with tears and sobs, and took from her hands what seemed a last pledge of love. They fell on their knees, kissed the hem of her garment; the elder poured forth endearments, mingled with prayers and blessings. Miss Ophelia, fearing for her little patient, signed to each to pass out.

At last all were gone but Tom. She held forth a shining tress.

“Here, Uncle Tom; a beautiful one for you. O, I am so happy, Uncle Tom, to think I shall see you in heaven,—for I’m sure I shall.”

Tom took it with tears streaming down his dark cheeks; he could only look up where he had always looked for help.

Miss Ophelia thought all gone; but, turning, found Topsy lingering, wiping her eyes.

“Where did you start up from?” said Miss Ophelia.

“I was here,” said Topsy. “O, Miss Eva, I’ve been a bad girl; but won’t you give me one, too?”

“Yes, poor Topsy! to be sure I will. There—every time you look at that, think that I love you, and wanted you to be a good girl.”

“O, Miss Eva, I is tryin’!” said Topsy, earnestly; “but, Lor, it’s so hard to be good! ’Pears like I an’t used to it, no ways!”

“Jesus knows it, Topsy; he is sorry for you; he will help you.”

Topsy, with her eyes hid in her apron, was passed from the room; as she went, she hid the precious curl in her bosom.

All being gone, Miss Ophelia shut the door. That worthy lady had wiped away many tears of her own; but concern for the consequence of so much excitement was uppermost.

St. Clare had sat all the while in one attitude, his hand shading his eyes.

“Papa!” said Eva, gently, laying her hand on his.

He started and shivered, but made no answer.

“Dear papa!”

“I cannot,” said St. Clare, rising,—“I cannot have it so! The Almighty hath dealt very bitterly with me!”

“Augustine! has not God a right to do what he will with his own?” said Miss Ophelia.

“Perhaps so; but that doesn’t make it easier to bear,” said he, with a dry, hard, tearless manner, turning away.

“Papa, you break my heart!” said Eva, throwing herself into his arms. “You must not feel so!”

Her violent sobbing alarmed them; his thought turned at once.

“There, Eva,—there, dearest! Hush! hush! I was wrong; I was wicked. I will feel any way, do any way,—only don’t distress yourself. I will be resigned; I was wicked to speak as I did.”

She lay like a wearied dove in his arms; he bent over her, soothing her by every tender word he could think of. Marie went into her room and fell into hysterics.

“You didn’t give me a curl, Eva,” said her father, with a sad smile.

“They are all yours, papa,” said she, smiling; “yours and mamma’s; and you must give dear aunty as many as she wants. I only gave them to our poor people myself, because, you know, papa, they might be forgotten when I am gone; and I hoped it might help them remember. You are a Christian, are you not, papa?” she added, doubtfully.

“Why do you ask me?”

“I don’t know. You are so good, I don’t see how you can help it.”

“What is being a Christian, Eva?”

“Loving Christ most of all,” said Eva.

“Do you, Eva?”

“Certainly I do.”

“You never saw him,” said St. Clare.

“That makes no difference,” said Eva. “I believe him, and in a few days I shall see him;” and the young face grew fervent, radiant with joy.

St. Clare said no more. He had seen such a feeling in his mother; but no chord within him vibrated to it.

After this she declined rapidly. Her beautiful room became avowedly a sick room. Miss Ophelia, day and night, performed the nurse’s duty with that adroitness and exactness which all had smiled at in common days, but now blessed. With clear head, punctual remembrance of every prescription, and tranquil hand, she kept every disagreeable incident from sight. Those who had shrugged at her “setnesses” owned her the very person needed.

Uncle Tom was much in Eva’s room. The child suffered from nervous restlessness, and it relieved her to be carried; and Tom’s greatest delight was to bear her, resting on a pillow, up and down the room, or out upon the verandah; and, when fresh lake-breezes blew in the morning, he would walk with her under the orange-trees, or, sitting in their old seats, sing their favorite hymns. Her father often did the same; but when he was weary, Eva would say,

“O, papa, let Tom take me. Poor fellow! it pleases him; and you know it’s all he can do now, and he wants to do something!”

“So do I, Eva!” said her father.

“Well, papa, you can do everything, and are everything to me. You read to me—you sit up nights—and Tom has only this one thing, and his singing; and I know, too, he does it easier than you. He carries me so strong!”

The desire to do something pervaded all the servants; each, in their way, did what they could. The farewell voyage of the little spirit was so bright and placid—so softly wafted toward heavenly shores—that it was hard to realize death approached. She felt no pain, only tranquil weakness, daily and almost insensibly increasing; and she was so beautiful, so loving, so trustful, so happy, that one yielded to the soothing air of innocence and peace that breathed around her. A strange calm fell on St. Clare. It was not hope; that was impossible. It was not resignation; it was only a resting in the beautiful present, wishing to think of no future—like that hush we feel in the bright mild woods of autumn, when the hectic flush is on the trees, and the last flowers linger by the brook, and we joy the more for knowing it will soon pass away.

To Tom she said what she would not burden her father with—those mysterious intimations the soul feels as the cords begin to unbind. At last he would not sleep in his room, but lay all night in the outer verandah, ready at every call.

“Uncle Tom, what alive have you taken to sleeping anywhere and everywhere, like a dog, for?” said Miss Ophelia, finding him stretched by her door near eleven o’clock, as she went to bolt it. “I thought you was one of the orderly sort.”

“I do, Miss Feely,” said Tom, mysteriously. “I do, but now—”

“Well, what now?”

“We mustn’t speak loud; Mas’r St. Clare won’t hear on’t; but, Miss Feely, you know there must be somebody watchin’ for the bridegroom.”

“What do you mean, Tom?”

“You know it says in Scripture, ‘At midnight there was a great cry made. Behold, the bridegroom cometh.’ That’s what I’m spectin’ now, every night, Miss Feely,—and I couldn’t sleep out o’ hearin’, no ways.”

“Why, Uncle Tom, what makes you think so?”

“Miss Eva, she talks to me. The Lord, he sends his messenger in the soul. I must be thar; for when that ar blessed child goes into the kingdom, they’ll open the door so wide, we’ll all get a look in at the glory, Miss Feely.”

“Did Miss Eva say she felt more unwell than usual to-night?”

“No; but she telled me, this mornin’, she was comin’ nearer; thar’s them that tells it to the child. It’s the angels,—‘it’s the trumpet sound afore the break o’ day,’” said Tom, quoting a hymn.

Eva had been unusually bright that afternoon; she sat raised in bed, looked over her trinkets, and named the friends for each; her manner more animated, her voice more natural, than for weeks. St. Clare, kissing her for the night, had said to Miss Ophelia, with a lighter heart than for many days, “Cousin, we may keep her with us, after all; she is certainly better.”

But at midnight—strange, mystic hour, when the veil between the frail present and the eternal future grows thin—came the messenger.

A quick step—Miss Ophelia’s; she had resolved to sit up; and, at the turn of the night, had discerned what experienced nurses call “a change.” The outer door was opened quickly; Tom, watching, was on the alert.

“Go for the doctor, Tom! lose not a moment,” said she; and, stepping across, she rapped at St. Clare’s door.

“Cousin,” she said, “I wish you would come.”

Those words fell on his heart like clods upon a coffin. Why did they? He was in the room in an instant, bending over Eva, who still slept.

What was it he saw that made his heart stand still? Thou knowest, reader, who hast seen that same expression on the face dearest to thee—that look indescribable, hopeless, unmistakable, which says thy beloved is no longer thine. Yet, on the child’s face, no ghastly imprint; rather a high and almost sublime expression—the overshadowing presence of spiritual natures, the dawning of immortal life in that childish soul.

They stood so still that even the watch seemed too loud. In a few moments, Tom returned with the doctor. He entered, gave one look, and stood silent as the rest.

“When did this change take place?” he whispered to Miss Ophelia.

“About the turn of the night.”

Marie, roused by the doctor’s entrance, hurried in.

“Augustine! Cousin!—O!—what—”

“Hush!” said St. Clare, hoarsely; “she is dying!”

Lights; footsteps; anxious faces thronging the verandah, looking tearfully through the glass; but St. Clare saw only that look on the little sleeper’s face.

“O, if she would only wake, and speak once more!” He stooped: “Eva, darling!”

The large blue eyes unclosed; a smile passed; she tried to raise her head and speak.

“Do you know me, Eva?”

“Dear papa,” said the child, with a last effort, throwing her arms about his neck; in a moment they dropped again. A spasm of mortal agony crossed the face; she struggled for breath, and threw up her little hands.

“O, God, this is dreadful!” said St. Clare, turning away in agony, wringing Tom’s hand unconsciously. “O, Tom, my boy, it is killing me!”

Tom held his master’s hands between his own; with tears streaming down his cheeks, he looked up for help where he had always been used to look.

“Pray that this may be cut short!” said St. Clare. “This wrings my heart.”

“O, bless the Lord! it’s over,—it’s over, dear Mas’r!” said Tom. “Look at her.”

She lay panting, as one exhausted; the large, clear eyes rolled up and fixed. Ah, what said those eyes, that spoke so much of heaven! Earth was past—and earthly pain. So solemn, so mysterious, was the triumphant brightness of that face that even sorrow’s sobs were checked. They pressed around her, breathless.

“Eva,” said St. Clare, gently.

She did not hear.

“O, Eva, tell us what you see! What is it?” said her father.

A bright, glorious smile passed over her face, and she said, brokenly, “O! love,—joy,—peace!” gave one sigh, and passed from death unto life.

Farewell, beloved child! the bright, eternal doors have closed after thee; those who watched thy entrance shall wake to the cold gray sky of daily life, and thou—gone forever. But for thee, little one, all tears are wiped away.

“This Is the Last of Earth”

The statuettes and pictures in Eva’s room were shrouded in white napkins; hushed breathings and muffled footfalls were heard there, and light stole in solemnly through windows darkened by closed blinds.

The bed was draped in white; and there, beneath the drooping angel-figure, lay a little sleeping form,—sleeping never to waken. She was robed in one of the simple white dresses she had been wont to wear when living; the rose-colored light through the curtains cast over the icy coldness of death a warm glow. The heavy eyelashes drooped softly; the head was turned a little to one side, as if in natural sleep; but over every lineament was diffused that high celestial expression, that mingling of rapture and repose, which showed it was no earthly or temporary sleep, but the long, sacred rest which “He giveth to his beloved.”

There is no death to such as thou, dear Eva! neither darkness nor shadow of death; only such a bright fading as when the morning-star melts in the golden dawn. Thine is the victory without the battle,—the crown without the conflict.

So did St. Clare think, as, with folded arms, he stood and gazed. Ah! who shall say what he did think? From the hour that voices had said, in the dying chamber, “she is gone,” it had been all a dreary mist, a heavy dimness of anguish. They had asked him when the funeral should be, and where they should lay her; he had answered, impatiently, that he cared not.

The chamber had been arranged; while Miss Ophelia presided over order and neatness, soft, poetic touches had taken from the death-room its grim and ghastly air. There were flowers,—all white, delicate and fragrant. Eva’s little table, covered with white, bore her favorite vase, with a single white moss rose-bud. The folds of drapery, the fall of the curtains, had been nicelily adjusted. Even now, while St. Clare stood as in a dream, a light-footed girl came in with a basket of white flowers, placed a fair cape jessamine in the small hands, and disposed other blossoms around the couch.

The door opened again, and Topsy, her eyes swelled with crying, appeared, holding something under her apron. The girl made a quick forbidding gesture.

“You must go out,” she whispered; “you haven’t any business here.”

“O, do let me! I brought a flower,—such a pretty one!” said Topsy, holding up a half-blown tea rose-bud. “Do let me put just one there.”

“Get along!” was the answer, more decidedly.

“Let her stay!” said St. Clare, suddenly stamping his foot. “She shall come.”

Topsy came forward, laid her offering at the feet of the corpse; then, with a wild and bitter cry, threw herself on the floor alongside the bed, and wept and moaned aloud.

Miss Ophelia hastened in, and tried to raise and silence her; but in vain.

“O, Miss Eva! oh, Miss Eva! I wish I ’s dead, too,—I do!”

There was a piercing wildness in the cry. The blood flushed into St. Clare’s white, marble-like face; the first tears he had shed since Eva died stood in his eyes.

“Get up, child,” said Miss Ophelia, in a softened voice; “don’t cry so. Miss Eva is gone to heaven; she is an angel.”

“But I can’t see her!” sobbed Topsy. “I never shall see her!”

“She said she loved me,” she added,—“she did! O, dear! oh, dear! there an’t nobody left now,—there an’t!”

“That’s true enough,” said St. Clare; “but do,” he said to Miss Ophelia, “see if you can’t comfort the poor creature.”

“I jist wish I hadn’t never been born,” moaned Topsy. “I didn’t want to be born, no ways; and I don’t see no use on ’t.”

Miss Ophelia raised her gently, but firmly, and took her from the room; and, as she did so, tears fell from her eyes.

“Topsy, you poor child,” she said, leading her into her room, “don’t give up! I can love you, though I am not like that dear little child. I hope I’ve learnt something of the love of Christ from her. I can love you; I do, and I’ll try to help you to grow up a good Christian girl.”

Her voice was more than her words, and more than that were the honest tears that fell down her face. From that hour, she acquired an influence over the mind of the destitute child that she never lost.

“O, my Eva, whose little hour on earth did so much of good,” thought St. Clare, “what account have I to give for my long years?”

There were soft whisperings and footfalls, as one after another stole in to look at the dead; then came the little coffin; then the funeral. Carriages drove to the door; there were white scarfs and ribbons, and mourners in crape; there were words read from the Bible, and prayers offered. St. Clare lived, and walked, and moved, as one who has shed every tear; to the last he saw only one thing,—that golden head in the coffin; then he saw the cloth spread over it, the lid closed; and he walked, when he was put beside the others, down to a little place at the bottom of the garden; and there, by the mossy seat where she and Tom had talked, and sung, and read so often, was the little grave. He stood,—looked vacantly down; he saw them lower the little coffin; he heard, dimly, the solemn words, “I am the resurrection and the Life; he that believeth in me, though he were dead, yet shall he live;” and, as the earth was cast in and filled up the little grave, he could not realize that it was his Eva they were hiding from his sight.

Nor was it!—not Eva, but only the frail seed of that bright, immortal form with which she shall yet come forth, in the day of the Lord Jesus!

And then all were gone; the mourners went back to the place which should know her no more. Marie’s room was darkened; she lay on the bed, sobbing and moaning in uncontrollable grief, calling every moment for the attentions of all her servants. Of course, they had no time to cry,—why should they? The grief was her grief; and she was fully convinced that nobody on earth did, could, or would feel it as she did.

“St. Clare did not shed a tear,” she said; “he didn’t sympathize with me; it’s wonderful how hard-hearted and unfeeling he is, when he must know how I suffer.”

So much are people the slave of their eye and ear, that many really thought Missis the principal sufferer, especially as she had hysterical spasms, sent for the doctor, and at last declared herself dying; and, in the running and scampering, and bringing up hot bottles, and heating flannels, and chafing, and fussing, there was quite a diversion.

Tom, however, had a feeling at his own heart that drew him to his master. He followed him wistfully; and when he saw him sitting, so pale and quiet, in Eva’s room, holding before his eyes her little open Bible,—yet seeing no letter or word,—there was more sorrow to Tom in that still, fixed, tearless eye, than in all Marie’s moans and lamentations.

In a few days they were back in the city; Augustine, with the restlessness of grief, longing for another scene to change the current of his thoughts. He left the house and garden, with its little grave, and returned to New Orleans; he walked the streets busily, strove to fill the chasm in his heart with hurry and bustle. People who saw him in the street, or met him at the cafe, knew of his loss only by the weed on his hat; there he was, smiling and talking, reading the newspaper, speculating on politics, attending to business; and who could see that all this smiling outside was but a hollowed shell over a heart that was a dark and silent sepulchre?

“Mr. St. Clare is a singular man,” said Marie to Miss Ophelia, complainingly. “I used to think, if there was anything in the world he did love, it was our dear little Eva; but he seems to be forgetting her very easily. I cannot ever get him to talk about her. I really did think he would show more feeling!”

“Still waters run deepest, they used to tell me,” said Miss Ophelia, oracularly.

“O, I don’t believe in such things; it’s all talk. If people have feeling, they will show it,—they can’t help it; but, then, it’s a great misfortune to have feeling. I’d rather have been made like St. Clare. My feelings prey upon me so!”

“Well, at all events, he has no consideration for me,” said Marie. “He hasn’t spoken one word of sympathy, and he must know how much more a mother feels than any man can.”

“The heart knoweth its own bitterness,” said Miss Ophelia, gravely.

“That’s just what I think. I know just what I feel,—nobody else seems to. Eva used to, but she is gone!” And Marie lay back, and sobbed. She was one of those, in whose eyes whatever is lost and gone assumes a value it never had in possession.

While they spoke in the parlor, another conversation went on in St. Clare’s library.

Tom, who uneasily followed his master, had seen him go there hours before; after vainly waiting, he made an errand in. St. Clare lay on his lounge, at the further end of the room, his face down; Eva’s Bible lay open at a little distance. Tom stood by the sofa, hesitating; St. Clare suddenly raised himself. The honest face, so full of grief, with such an imploring expression of affection and sympathy, struck him. He laid his hand on Tom’s, and bowed his forehead on it.
