“I say, Sambo, you go to spilin’ the hands, I’ll tell Mas’r o’ you,” said Quimbo, busy at the mill, from which he had viciously driven two or three tired women waiting to grind.

“And I’ll tell him ye won’t let the women come to the mills, yo old nigger!” said Sambo. “Yo jes keep to yo own row.”

Tom was hungry with the day’s journey, almost faint for want of food.

“Thar, yo!” said Quimbo, throwing down a coarse bag that held a peck of corn; “thar, nigger, grab, take car on ’t,—yo won’t get no more, dis yer week.”

Tom waited till a late hour for a place at the mills; then, moved by the utter weariness of two women trying to grind their corn, he ground for them, put together the decaying brands of the fire where many had baked before, and then went about getting his own supper. Small as it was, this deed of charity woke an answering touch in their hearts; an expression of womanly kindness came over their hard faces; they mixed his cake for him, and tended its baking. Tom sat by the firelight and drew out his Bible,—for he had need of comfort.

“What’s that?” said one of the women.

“A Bible,” said Tom.

“Good Lord! han’t seen un since I was in Kentuck.”

“Was you raised in Kentuck?” said Tom, with interest.

“Yes, and well raised, too; never ’spected to come to dis yer!” she sighed.

“What’s dat ar book, any way?” said the other.

“Why, the Bible.”

“Laws a me! what’s dat?” said the woman.

“Do tell! you never hearn on ’t?” said the first. “I used to har Missis a readin’ on ’t, sometimes, in Kentuck; but, laws o’ me! we don’t har nothin’ here but crackin’ and swarin’.”

“Read a piece, anyways!” said the first, curiously, seeing Tom attentively poring over it.

Tom read,—“Come unto Me, all ye that labor and are heavy laden, and I will give you rest.”

“Them’s good words, enough,” said the woman; “who says ’em?”

“The Lord,” said Tom.

“I jest wish I know’d whar to find Him,” said she. “I would go; ’pears like I never should get rested again. My flesh is fairly sore, and I tremble all over, every day, and Sambo’s allers a jawin’ at me, ’cause I doesn’t pick faster; and nights it’s most midnight ’fore I can get my supper; and den ’pears like I don’t turn over and shut my eyes, ’fore I hear de horn blow to get up, and at it agin in de mornin’. If I knew whar de Lor was, I’d tell him.”

“He’s here, he’s everywhere,” said Tom.

“Lor, you an’t gwine to make me believe dat ar! I know de Lord an’t here,” said the woman. “‘Tan’t no use talking, though. I’s jest gwine to camp down, and sleep while I ken.”

The women went off to their cabins, and Tom sat alone by the smouldering fire that flickered up redly in his face. The silver, fair-browed moon rose in the purple sky, and looked down, calm and silent, as God looks on the scene of misery and oppression,—looked calmly on the lone black man, as he sat, with his arms folded, and his Bible on his knee.

“Is God HERE?” Ah, how is it possible for the untaught heart to keep its faith, unswerving, in the face of dire misrule and palpable, unrebuked injustice? In that simple heart waged a fierce conflict; the crushing sense of wrong; the foreshadowing of a whole life of future misery; the wreck of all past hopes, like dead corpses of wife, and child, and friend, mournfully tossing before the soul, as the half-drowned mariner sees the dead rise from the dark wave! Ah, was it easy here to believe and hold fast the great password of Christian faith, that “God IS, and is the REWARDER of them that diligently seek Him”?

Tom rose, disconsolate, and stumbled into the cabin allotted to him. The floor was strewn with weary sleepers; the foul air almost repelled him; but the heavy night-dews were chill, his limbs weary; wrapping about him a tattered blanket, his only bed-clothing, he stretched himself in the straw and fell asleep.

In dreams, a gentle voice came over his ear. He was sitting on the mossy seat in the garden by Lake Pontchartrain; and Eva, with her serious eyes bent downward, was reading to him from the Bible; and he heard her read.

“When thou passest through the waters, I will be with thee, and the rivers they shall not overflow thee; when thou walkest through the fire, thou shalt not be burned, neither shall the flame kindle upon thee; for I am the Lord thy God, the Holy One of Israel, thy Saviour.”

Gradually the words seemed to melt and fade, as in a divine music; the child raised her deep eyes, and fixed them lovingly on him, and rays of warmth and comfort seemed to go from them to his heart; and, as if wafted on the music, she rose on shining wings, from which flakes and spangles of gold fell off like stars, and she was gone.

Tom woke. Was it a dream? Let it pass for one. But who shall say that that sweet young spirit, which in life so yearned to comfort the distressed, was forbidden of God to assume this ministry after death?

It is a beautiful belief,
    That ever round our head
Are hovering, on angel wings,
    The spirits of the dead.

Cassy

“And behold, the tears of such as were oppressed, and they had no comforter; and on the side of their oppressors there was power, but they had no comforter.”—ECCL. 4:1

It took but a short time to familiarize Tom with all that was to be hoped or feared in his new way of life. An expert and faithful workman, quiet, prompt, and peaceable, he resolved, by unremitting diligence, to avert, at least from himself, some portion of the evils around him. He saw enough of abuse and misery to make him sick and weary; yet he toiled on with religious patience, committing himself to Him that judgeth righteously, not without hope that some way of escape might yet be opened.

Legree, taking a silent note of Tom’s availability, rated him as a first-class hand; and yet he felt for him the native antipathy of bad to good. He saw plainly that, when his violence fell on the helpless, Tom took notice of it; for, so subtle is the atmosphere of opinion, that it will make itself felt without words. He had purchased Tom with a view of making him, in time, a sort of overseer; the first, second, and third requisite for that place, in his view, was hardness. Finding Tom not hard to his hand, he resolved forthwith to harden him; and, some few weeks after Tom had been on the place, he determined to commence.

One morning, as the hands were mustered for the field, Tom noticed, with surprise, a new comer among them. A woman, tall and slenderly formed, with remarkably delicate hands and feet, dressed with neatness, walked erect beside him in the dim gray of dawn. Her face, that might once have been beautiful, was seamed with lines of pain and of proud endurance; her whole form was emaciated; but her eye—so large, so heavily black, overshadowed by long lashes—held a wild, mournful despair; beneath the scorn that sat on the flexible lip and in every proud motion, there lay in that eye a deep, settled night of anguish. Where she came from, Tom did not know. To the gang, however, she was known; and there was much looking and turning of heads, with a smothered exultation among the miserable, ragged, half-starved creatures.

“Got to come to it, at last,—glad of it!” said one.

“He! he! he! you’ll know how good it is, Misse!” said another.

The woman took no notice, but walked on with the same expression of angry scorn. Tom, who had always lived among the refined, felt intuitively that she belonged by nature to that class; how or why fallen to these degrading circumstances, he could not tell. She neither looked at him nor spoke, though all the way to the field she kept close at his side.

At work, Tom often glanced towards her. A native adroitness made the task easy to her; she picked fast and clean, and with an air that despised alike the work and the humiliation of doing it.

In the course of the day Tom worked near a mulatto woman, bought in the same lot with himself. She was evidently in great suffering; he often heard her praying, as she wavered and trembled, and seemed about to fall. Silently, as he came near, he transferred several handfuls of cotton from his own sack to hers.

“O, don’t, don’t!” said the woman, looking surprised; “it’ll get you into trouble.”

Just then one of the drivers, Sambo, came up. He seemed to have a special spite against this woman. Flourishing his whip—“What dis yer, foolin’?”—and, with the word, kicking her with his heavy cowhide, he struck Tom across the face.

Tom resumed his task; but the woman, already at the last point of exhaustion, fainted.

“I’ll bring her to!” said the driver, with a brutal grin. “I’ll give her something better than camphire!” Taking a pin from his sleeve, he buried it to the head in her flesh. The woman groaned, and half rose. “Get up, you beast, and work, will yer, or I’ll show yer a trick more!”

For a few moments, an unnatural strength seemed to seize her, and she worked with desperate eagerness.

“See dat ye keep to dat ar,” said he, “or yer’ll wish yer’s dead, tonight, I reckin!”

“That I do now!” Tom heard her say; and again,—“O, Lord, how long! O, Lord, why don’t you help us?”

At the risk of all he might suffer, Tom came forward, and put all the cotton in his sack into the woman’s.

“O, you mustn’t! you donno what they’ll do to ye!” said she.

“I can bar it,” said Tom, “better ’n you.” In a moment he was at his place again.

The stranger woman had, in the course of her work, come near enough to hear. She raised her heavy black eyes, fixed them for a second on him; then, taking a quantity of cotton from her basket, placed it in his.

“You know nothing about this place,” she said, with her lip’s scornful curl. “When you’ve been here a month, you’ll be done helping anybody; you’ll find it hard enough to take care of your own skin!”

“The Lord forbid, Missis!” said Tom, using instinctively the respectful form proper to the high-bred among whom he had lived.

“The Lord never visits these parts,” said the woman, bitterly; and went nimbly forward with her work.

But her action had been seen by the driver across the field, and, flourishing his whip, he came up.

“What! what!” he cried, with an air of triumph. “You a foolin’? Go ’long! yer under me now,—mind yerself, or yer’ll cotch it!”

A glance like sheet-lightning flashed from those black eyes. Facing him, with quivering lip and dilated nostril, she drew herself up, and fixed on him a blaze of rage and scorn.

“Dog!” she said, “touch me, if you dare! I’ve power enough, yet, to have you torn by the dogs,—burnt alive,—cut to inches! I’ve only to say the word!”

“What de devil you here for, den?” stammered the man, cowed, retreating a step or two. “Didn’t mean no harm, Misse Cassy!”

“Keep your distance, then!” she answered.

He was greatly inclined to attend to something at the other end of the field, and started off in quick time.

The woman turned to her work, and labored with a despatch that astonished Tom. She seemed to work by magic. Before the day was through, her basket was filled, crowded down, and piled, and she had several times put largely into Tom’s. Long after dusk, the weary train, with baskets on their heads, defiled up to the building where the cotton was stored and weighed. Legree was there, busily conversing with his two drivers, Sambo and Quimbo; there was ugly talk of “breakin’ in” hands that had “notions,” and a brutal jest that a soft-spoken fellow should get his hand in by flogging a sick wench. “I do the weighing!” said Legree, when there was mention that certain baskets had been filled by friendly hands.

Slowly the dispirited creatures wound into the room, and, with crouching reluctance, presented their baskets.

Tom’s was weighed and approved; he looked, with an anxious glance, for the success of the woman he had befriended.

Tottering, she came forward and delivered her basket. It was of full weight, as Legree well perceived; but, affecting anger, he said,

“What, you lazy beast! short again! stand aside,—you’ll catch it, pretty soon!”

She gave a groan of utter despair, and sat down on a board.

The person who had been called Misse Cassy now came forward, and, with a haughty, negligent air, delivered her basket. As she did so, Legree looked in her eyes with a sneering yet inquiring glance.

She fixed her black eyes steadily on him; her lips moved slightly, and she said something in French. What it was no one knew; but Legree’s face became perfectly demoniacal as she spoke. He half raised his hand, as if to strike,—a gesture she regarded with fierce disdain, turning away.

“And now,” said Legree, “come here, you Tom. You see, I telled ye I didn’t buy ye jest for the common work; I mean to promote ye, and make a driver of ye; and tonight ye may jest as well begin to get yer hand in. Now, ye jest take this yer gal and flog her; ye’ve seen enough on’t to know how.”

“I beg Mas’r’s pardon,” said Tom; “hopes Mas’r won’t set me at that. It’s what I an’t used to,—never did,—and can’t do, no way possible.”

“Ye’ll larn a pretty smart chance o’ things ye never did know, before I’ve done with ye!” said Legree; and, taking up a cowhide, he struck Tom a heavy blow across the cheek, following by a shower of blows.

“There!” he said, stopping to rest; “now, will ye tell me ye can’t do it?”

“Yes, Mas’r,” said Tom, putting up his hand to wipe the blood that trickled down his face. “I’m willin’ to work, night and day, and work while there’s life and breath in me; but this yer thing I can’t feel it right to do;—and, Mas’r, I never shall do it,—never!”

The mild, smooth voice and habitually respectful manner had made Legree think Tom cowardly and easy to subdue. When he spoke these last words, a thrill of amazement went through all. The poor woman clasped her hands, and said, “O Lord!” All involuntarily looked at each other, drawing in their breath, as if to prepare for the storm about to burst.

Legree looked stupefied; then broke forth,—“What! ye blasted black beast! tell me ye don’t think it right to do what I tell ye! What have any of you cussed cattle to do with thinking what’s right? I’ll put a stop to it! May be ye think ye’r a gentleman master, Tom, to be a tellin’ your master what’s right, and what ain’t! So you pretend it’s wrong to flog the gal!”

“I think so, Mas’r,” said Tom. “The poor crittur’s sick and feeble; ’t would be downright cruel, and it’s what I never will do, nor begin to. Mas’r, if you mean to kill me, kill me; but, as to my raising my hand agin any one here, I never shall,—I’ll die first!”

Legree shook with rage; his greenish eyes glared; but, like some ferocious beast that plays with its victim before devouring, he kept back his impulse, and jeered.

“Well, here’s a pious dog, at last, let down among us sinners!—a saint, a gentleman, to talk to us about our sins! Powerful holy critter, he must be! Here, you rascal, you make believe to be so pious,—didn’t you never hear, out o’ yer Bible, ‘Servants, obey yer masters’? An’t I yer master? Didn’t I pay down twelve hundred dollars, cash, for all there is inside yer old cussed black shell? An’t yer mine, now, body and soul?” He gave Tom a violent kick with his heavy boot. “Tell me!”

In the very depth of bodily suffering, bowed by oppression, that question shot a gleam of joy and triumph through Tom’s soul. He suddenly stretched himself up, and, looking earnestly to heaven, while tears and blood mingled on his face, he exclaimed,

“No! no! no! my soul an’t yours, Mas’r! You haven’t bought it,—ye can’t buy it! It’s been bought and paid for, by one that is able to keep it;—no matter, no matter, you can’t harm me!”

“I can’t!” sneered Legree. “We’ll see,—we’ll see! Here, Sambo, Quimbo, give this dog such a breakin’ in as he won’t get over, this month!”

The two gigantic negroes, with fiendish exultation in their faces, laid hold of Tom. The poor woman screamed with apprehension; all rose, as by a general impulse, while they dragged him unresisting from the place.

After Tom’s brutal whipping, Cassy secretly brings him water and dresses his wounds in the gin-house; hardened by years of sale and abuse and the loss of her children, she rages that God has forsaken them, but at Tom’s urging she reads of Christ’s forgiveness and, though despairing still, begins to be moved. She hints at a long, bitter history that has made her what she is.

The Tokens

“And slight, withal, may be the things that bring
Back on the heart the weight which it would fling
Aside forever; it may be a sound,
A flower, the wind, the ocean, which shall wound,—
Striking the electric chain wherewith we’re darkly bound.”

Childe Harold’s Pilgrimage, Can. 4.

The sitting-room of Legree’s establishment was a long, low, mouldering place with a wide hearth and a brazier of glowing charcoal. Once showy paper now hung in mildewed rags; damp and decay made a sickening odor; beer-stains and chalk scrawls scarred the walls. Saddles, bridles, coats, and whips lay in confused heaps, and the great dogs had made lairs among them. Though the night was not cold, the room was chill and vault-like; the red of the charcoal showed more squalor than comfort.

Legree, mixing punch from a cracked pitcher, grumbled to himself.

“Plague on that driver to kick up a row with the new hands! The fellow won’t be fit to work for a week,—right in the press of the season!”

“Yes, just like you,” said a voice behind him.

He started. It was Cassy, who had stolen upon his soliloquy.

“Hah! you she-devil! you’ve come back, have you?”

“Yes, I have,” she said, coolly; “come to have my own way, too!”

“You lie, you jade! I’ll be up to my word. Either behave yourself, or stay down to the quarters, and fare and work with the rest.”

“I’d rather, ten thousand times,” said the woman, “live in the dirtiest hole at the quarters, than be under your hoof!”

“But you are under my hoof, for all that,” said he, turning on her with a savage grin. “That’s one comfort. So, sit down here on my knee, my dear, and hear to reason,” he added, catching at her wrist.

“Simon Legree, take care!” she said, with a sharp flash of her eye,—so wild a light as to be almost appalling. “You’re afraid of me, Simon,” she added, low and deliberate; “and you’ve reason to be! But be careful, for I’ve got the devil in me!”

He pushed her off, and looked uncomfortable.

“After all, Cassy,” he muttered, “why can’t you be friends with me, as you used to?”

“Used to!” she said, bitterly, and stopped, choked by a surge of feeling.

Long had Cassy kept over him that strange control a strong, impassioned woman will hold even over a brutal man; but of late, galled past endurance, her temper would break into fits of raving that made her terrible to him; for, like most godless men, he was superstitious, and dreaded the insane. When he brought into the house Emmeline, the young girl he had lately bought, the smouldering embers of womanly feeling flashed up in Cassy’s worn heart; she took part with the girl; fierce quarrel followed; he swore she should go to the field; she, with proud scorn, declared she would, and worked there a day to show how little she feared his threats.

The outrage on poor Tom that afternoon had kindled her yet more. She had followed to upbraid him.

“I wish, Cassy,” said Legree, “you’d behave yourself decently.”

“You talk about behaving decently! And what have you been doing?—you, that haven’t even sense enough to keep from spoiling one of your best hands, right in the most pressing season, just for your devilish temper!”

“I was a fool, it’s a fact, to let any such brangle come up,” he said; “but, when the boy set up his will, he had to be broke in.”

“I reckon you won’t break him in!”

“Won’t I?” cried Legree, starting up. “I’d like to know if I won’t? He’ll be the first nigger that ever came it round me! I’ll break every bone in his body, but he shall give up!”

The door opened, and one of the drivers came forward, bowing, and held out a small paper.

“What’s that, you dog?” said Legree.

“It’s a witch thing, Mas’r! Something that niggers gets from witches. Keeps ’em from feelin’ when they’s flogged. He had it tied round his neck with a black string.”

Legree took the paper uneasily, and opened it.

Out dropped a silver dollar, and a long, shining curl of fair hair,—hair which, like a living thing, twined itself about his fingers.

“Damnation!” he screamed, stamping, tearing at the hair as if it burned him. “Where did this come from? Take it off!—burn it up!—burn it up!” He tore it from his hand and flung it into the charcoal. “What did you bring it to me for?”

The driver stared, aghast. Cassy, who had been turning to go, stopped and looked in wonder.

“Don’t you bring me any more of your devilish things!” he shouted, shaking his fist; and, picking up the silver dollar, he sent it through a window-pane into the dark.

The fellow slunk away. Legree sat down again, ashamed of his panic, and sullenly sipped his punch. Cassy slipped noiselessly out, to minister, as we have told, to poor Tom.

And what ailed Legree? What in a simple curl of fair hair could appall a man familiar with every cruelty? We must turn back. There had been a time when he had been rocked on a mother’s bosom,—cradled with prayers and hymns, his brow once wet in holy baptism. A fair-haired New England woman had led her only son, at Sabbath-bell, to worship; with long, unwearied love she had trained him. But, born of a hard-tempered sire, he followed the father’s steps—boisterous, tyrannical—despising counsel, breaking loose for the sea. He came home but once. Then the mother, with that yearning of a heart that must love and has nothing else to love, clung to him with passionate prayers to win him from sin to his soul’s good. That was his day of grace. He relented,—almost persuaded; but sin got the victory. He drank, swore, grew wilder; and, one night, when she knelt in the last agony of despair at his feet, he spurned her,—flung her senseless on the floor, and fled to his ship with curses. The next he heard was a letter thrust into his hand at a midnight revel; a long, curling lock fell from it, and twined about his fingers. The letter told him she was dead; dying, she blessed and forgave him. He burned the hair and burned the letter; and, as they hissed in the flame, he shuddered with thoughts of everlasting fires. He tried to drink and revel the memory away; but often, in the deep night, he saw that pale mother rise by his bed, and felt the soft twining of that hair till the cold sweat oozed and he sprang up in horror. Ye who wonder to hear, in the same evangel, that God is love and that God is a consuming fire—see ye not how, to the soul resolved in evil, perfect love is the most fearful torture?

“Blast it!” muttered Legree, tasting his glass. “Where did he get that? If it didn’t look just like—whoo! I thought I’d forgot that. Hang it! I’m lonesome! I’ll call Em. She hates me—the monkey! I don’t care,—I’ll make her come!”

He stepped into the great entry, where a once superb winding stair now rose bare and dirty among boxes and litter. Moonlight, through a broken fanlight, fell sickly; the air struck like a vault. He stopped, listening. A voice was singing—wild, pathetic—strangely ghostlike in that dreary old house.

“O there’ll be mourning, mourning, mourning,
O there’ll be mourning, at the judgment-seat of Christ!”

“Blast the girl!” he muttered. “I’ll choke her.—Em! Em!” he called, harshly. Only the walls mocked him. The sweet voice went on:

“Parents and children there shall part!
Parents and children there shall part!
    Shall part to meet no more!”

And clear and loud swelled through the empty halls the refrain,

“O there’ll be mourning, mourning, mourning,
O there’ll be mourning, at the judgment-seat of Christ!”

Legree stopped. Great drops stood on his forehead; his heart beat heavy. He thought he saw something white glimmer before him, and shuddered lest the form of his dead mother should rise.

“I know one thing,” he said, stumbling back into the sitting-room, and dropping into his chair; “I’ll let that fellow alone, after this! What did I want of his cussed paper? I b’lieve I’m bewitched! Where did he get that hair? It couldn’t have been that! I burnt that up,—I know I did! It would be a joke, if hair could rise from the dead!”

Ah, Legree! that golden tress was charmed; each hair held a spell of terror and remorse, binding thy cruel hands from uttermost evil on the helpless.

“I say!” he cried, stamping and whistling to the dogs, “wake up, some of you, and keep me company!” They only opened a sleepy eye and dozed again.

“I’ll have the drivers up here, to sing and dance one of their hell-dances, and keep off these notions,” he said; and, putting on his hat, he went out upon the verandah, and blew the horn with which he was wont to summon them.

He was often, in his gracious humors, used to warm these worthies with whiskey, and then amuse himself by setting them to sing, dance, or fight, as his mood took him.

It was between one and two o’clock when Cassy, returning from her tender offices to Tom, heard wild shrieking, whooping, and singing from the sitting-room, with the barking of dogs and a general uproar. She mounted the steps and looked in. Legree and the two black drivers, in furious intoxication, were staggering, whooping, upsetting chairs, making all manner of ludicrous and horrid grimaces.

She rested her small, slender hand on the blind, and looked fixedly on them; there was a world of anguish, scorn, and fierce bitterness in her dark eyes.

“Would it be a sin to rid the world of such a wretch?” she said to herself.

She turned hurriedly away, passed to a back door, glided up the stairs, and tapped at Emmeline’s door.

Emmeline and Cassy

Cassy entered the room, and found Emmeline sitting, pale with fear, in the furthest corner. As she came in, the girl started; but, seeing who it was, rushed forward, caught her arm, and said, "O Cassy, is it you? I'm so glad you've come! I was afraid it was—. O, you don't know what a horrid noise there has been, down stairs, all this evening!"

"I ought to know," said Cassy, dryly. "I've heard it often enough."

"O Cassy! couldn't we get away from this place? I don't care where,—into the swamp among the snakes,—anywhere! Couldn't we get somewhere away from here?"

"Nowhere, but into our graves," said Cassy.

"Did you ever try?"

"I've seen enough of trying and what comes of it," said Cassy.

"I'd be willing to live in the swamps, and gnaw the bark from trees. I an't afraid of snakes! I'd rather have one near me than him," said Emmeline, eagerly.

"There have been a good many here of your opinion," said Cassy; "but you couldn't stay in the swamps,—you'd be tracked by the dogs, and brought back, and then—then—"

"What would he do?" said the girl, looking, with breathless interest, into her face.

"What wouldn't he do, you'd better ask," said Cassy. "He's learned his trade well, among the pirates in the West Indies. You wouldn't sleep much, if I should tell you things I've seen,—things that he tells of, sometimes, for good jokes. I've heard screams here that I haven't been able to get out of my head for weeks and weeks. There's a place, away down by the quarters, where you can see a black, blasted tree, and the ground all covered with black ashes. Ask any one what was done there, and see if they dare to tell you."

"O! what do you mean?"

"I won't tell you. I hate to think of it. And the Lord only knows what we may see tomorrow, if that poor fellow holds out as he's begun."

"Horrid!" said Emmeline, every drop of blood receding from her cheeks. "O, Cassy, do tell me what I shall do!"

"What I've done. Do the best you can,—do what you must,—and make it up in hating and cursing."

"He wanted to make me drink some of his hateful brandy," said Emmeline; "and I hate it so—"

"You'd better drink," said Cassy. "I hated it, too; and now I can't live without it. One must have something;—things don't look so dreadful, when you take that."

"Mother used to tell me never to touch any such thing," said Emmeline.

"Mother told you!" said Cassy, with a thrilling and bitter emphasis. "What use is it for mothers to say anything? You are all to be bought and paid for, and your souls belong to whoever gets you. That's the way it goes. I say, drink brandy; drink all you can, and it'll make things come easier."

"O, Cassy! do pity me!"

"Pity you!—don't I? Haven't I a daughter,—Lord knows where she is, and whose she is, now,—going the way her mother went, before her, I suppose, and that her children must go, after her! There's no end to the curse—forever!"

"I wish I'd never been born!" said Emmeline, wringing her hands.

"That's an old wish with me," said Cassy. "I've got used to wishing that. I'd die, if I dared to," she added, looking out into the darkness, with that still, fixed despair which was the habitual expression of her face when at rest.

"It would be wicked to kill one's self," said Emmeline.

"I don't know why,—no wickeder than things we live and do, day after day. But the sisters told me things, when I was in the convent, that make me afraid to die. If it would only be the end of us, why, then—"

Emmeline turned away, and hid her face in her hands.

While this was passing above, Legree, overcome with his carouse, had sunk to sleep in the room below. He was not an habitual drunkard. His coarse, strong nature craved, and could endure, continual stimulation; but a deep, underlying cautiousness commonly kept him from losing control.

This night, however, in his feverish efforts to banish those elements of woe and remorse which woke within him, he had indulged more than common; and, when his sable attendants were discharged, he fell heavily on a settle and slept.

O! how dares the bad soul to enter the shadowy world of sleep? Legree dreamed. In his heavy, feverish slumber, a veiled form stood beside him, and laid a cold, soft hand upon him. He thought he knew who it was; he shuddered, though the face was veiled. Then he felt that hair twining round his fingers; then it slid smoothly round his neck, and tightened; he could not draw his breath; then voices whispered,—whispers that chilled him. He hung on the edge of a frightful abyss; dark hands stretched up, pulling him over; Cassy came behind him, laughing, and pushed him. Then the solemn veiled figure rose and drew aside the veil. It was his mother; she turned away from him, and he fell, down, down, amid shrieks, and groans, and demon laughter,—and Legree awoke.

Calmly the rosy hue of dawn was stealing into the room. The morning star stood, with its solemn, holy eye of light, looking down on the man of sin. O, with what freshness, what solemnity and beauty, is each new day born!—but the bold, bad man heard it not. He woke with an oath and a curse; stumbling forward, he poured out a tumbler of brandy, and drank half.

"I've had a h—l of a night!" he said to Cassy, who just then entered.

"You'll get plenty of the same sort, by and by," said she, dryly.

"What do you mean, you minx?"

"You'll find out, one of these days," returned Cassy. "Now, Simon, I've one piece of advice to give you."

"The devil, you have!"

"My advice is," said Cassy, steadily, as she began adjusting some things about the room, "that you let Tom alone."

"What business is 't of yours?"

"What? To be sure, I don't know what it should be. If you want to pay twelve hundred for a fellow, and use him right up in the press of the season, just to serve your own spite, it's no business of mine. I've done what I could for him."

"You have? What business have you meddling in my matters?"

"None, to be sure. I've saved you some thousands of dollars, at different times, by taking care of your hands,—that's all the thanks I get. If your crop comes shorter than others into market, you won't mind, I suppose? And you'll pay down, like a lady, won't you? I think I see you doing it!"

He had but one form of ambition,—to bring in the heaviest crop; and Cassy, with woman's tact, touched the string that could be made to vibrate.

"Well, I'll let him off at what he's got," said Legree; "but he shall beg my pardon, and promise better fashions."

"That he won't do," said Cassy.

"Won't,—eh?"

"No, he won't."

"I'd like to know why, Mistress," said Legree, in extreme scorn.

"Because he's done right, and he knows it, and won't say he's done wrong."

"Who a cuss cares what he knows? The nigger shall say what I please, or—"

"Or you'll lose, by keeping him out of the field, just at this very press."

"He will give up,—course, he will; don't I know what niggers is? He'll beg like a dog, this morning."

"He won't, Simon; you don't know this kind. You may kill him by inches,—you won't get the first word of confession out of him."

"We'll see,—where is he?" said Legree, going out.

"In the waste-room of the gin-house," said Cassy.

Legree sallied forth with a degree of misgiving unusual with him. His dream, mingled with Cassy's suggestions, had touched that spot of superstitious dread in a fierce, unbelieving soul. He resolved that nobody should witness his encounter, and, if he could not subdue by bullying, to defer his vengeance for a more convenient season.

The solemn light of dawn had looked in through the rude window of the shed where Tom was lying; and, as if descending on that star-beam, came the words, "I am the root and offspring of David, and the bright and morning star." The mysterious warnings of Cassy had roused, not discouraged, his soul. He did not know but the day of his death was dawning; and his heart throbbed with solemn joy and desire, as he thought that the wondrous all—the great white throne, the white-robed multitude, the crowns, the palms, the harps—might break upon his vision before that sun should set. Therefore, without shuddering or trembling, he heard the voice of his persecutor drawing near.

"Well, my boy," said Legree, with a contemptuous kick, "how do you find yourself? Didn't I tell yer I could larn yer a thing or two? How do yer like it—eh? How did yer whaling agree with yer, Tom? An't quite so crank as ye was last night. Ye couldn't treat a poor sinner, now, to a bit of sermon, could ye,—eh?"

Tom answered nothing.

"Get up, you beast!" said Legree, kicking him again.

It was hard for one so bruised and faint; as Tom made efforts, Legree laughed brutally.

"What makes ye so spry, this morning, Tom? Cotched cold, may be, last night."

Tom had gained his feet, and confronted his master with a steady, unmoved front.

"The devil, you can!" said Legree, looking him over. "I believe you haven't got enough yet. Now, Tom, get right down on yer knees and beg my pardon for yer shines last night."

Tom did not move.

"Down, you dog!" said Legree, striking him with his riding-whip.

"Mas'r Legree," said Tom, "I can't do it. I did only what I thought was right. I shall do just so again, if ever the time comes. I never will do a cruel thing, come what may."

"Yes, but ye don't know what may come, Master Tom. Ye think what you've got is something. I tell you 't an't anything,—nothing 't all. How would ye like to be tied to a tree, and have a slow fire lit up around ye;—wouldn't that be pleasant,—eh, Tom?"

"Mas'r," said Tom, "I know ye can do dreadful things; but,"—he stretched himself upward and clasped his hands,—"but, after ye've killed the body, there an't no more ye can do. And O, there's all ETERNITY to come, after that!"

ETERNITY!—the word thrilled through the black man's soul with light and power; it thrilled through the sinner's soul, too, like the bite of a scorpion. Legree gnashed on him with his teeth, but rage kept him silent; and Tom, like a man disenthralled, spoke, clear and cheerful:

"Mas'r Legree, as ye bought me, I'll be a true and faithful servant to ye. I'll give ye all the work of my hands, all my time, all my strength; but my soul I won't give up to mortal man. I will hold on to the Lord, and put his commands before all,—die or live; you may be sure on 't. Mas'r Legree, I ain't a grain afeard to die. I'd as soon die as not. Ye may whip me, starve me, burn me,—it'll only send me sooner where I want to go."

"I'll make ye give out, though, 'fore I've done!" said Legree, in a rage.

"I shall have help," said Tom; "you'll never do it."

"Who the devil's going to help you?" said Legree, scornfully.

"The Lord Almighty," said Tom.

"D—n you!" said Legree; with one blow of his fist he felled Tom to the earth.

A cold, soft hand fell on Legree's at this moment. He turned,—it was Cassy's. The touch recalled his dream; through the chambers of his brain flashed the fearful images of the night.

"Will you be a fool?" said Cassy, in French. "Let him go! Let me alone to get him fit to be in the field again. Isn't it just as I told you?"

They say the rhinoceros and the alligator, though mailed, have each a spot where they are vulnerable; and fierce, reckless reprobates have commonly this point in superstitious dread. Legree turned away, determined to let the point go for the time.

"Well, have it your own way," he said, doggedly, to Cassy.

"Hark, ye!" he said to Tom; "I won't deal with ye now, because the business is pressing, and I want all my hands; but I never forget. I'll score it against ye, and sometime I'll have my pay out o' yer old black hide,—mind ye!"

Legree turned, and went out.

"There you go," said Cassy, looking darkly after him; "your reckoning's to come, yet!—My poor fellow, how are you?"

"The Lord God hath sent his angel, and shut the lion's mouth, for this time," said Tom.

"For this time, to be sure," said Cassy; "but now you've got his ill will upon you,—to follow you day in, day out, hanging like a dog on your throat,—sucking your blood, bleeding away your life, drop by drop. I know the man."

Liberty

"No matter with what solemnities he may have been devoted upon the altar of slavery, the moment he touches the sacred soil of Britain, the altar and the God sink together in the dust, and he stands redeemed, regenerated, and disenthralled, by the irresistible genius of universal emancipation."—_Curran_.

A while we must leave Tom in the hands of his persecutors, while we turn to pursue the fortunes of George and his wife, whom we left in friendly hands, in a farmhouse on the road-side.

Tom Loker we left groaning and touzling in a most immaculately clean Quaker bed, under the motherly supervision of a grave, soft-voiced woman, whose snowy kerchief and rustling brown silk moved to and fro with noiseless precision.

"The devil!" says Tom Loker, giving a great throw to the bedclothes.

"I must request thee, Thomas, not to use such language," said the Quaker woman, as she quietly rearranged the bed.

"Well, I won't, granny, if I can help it," said Tom; "but it is enough to make a fellow swear,—so cursedly hot!"

"I wish, friend, thee would leave off cursing and swearing, and think upon thy ways," she returned, tucking him in, chrysalis-wise.

"What the devil," said Tom, "should I think of _them_ for? Last thing ever _I_ want to think of—hang it all!"

He flounced over, disarranging everything, then, after a pause, muttered, sullenly,

"That fellow and gal are here, I s'pose."

"They are so," said the other.

"They'd better be off up to the lake," said Tom; "the quicker the better."

"Probably they will do so," she answered, knitting peacefully.

"And hark ye," said Tom; "we've got correspondents in Sandusky, that watch the boats for us. I don't care if I tell, now. I hope they _will_ get away, just to spite—him! But about the gal,—tell 'em to dress her up some way, so's to alter her. Her description's out in Sandusky."

"We will attend to that matter," she said, with characteristic composure.

As we at this place take leave of Tom Loker, we may as well say, that, having lain three weeks at the Quaker dwelling, sick with a rheumatic fever, which set in, in company with his other afflictions, Tom arose from his bed a somewhat sadder and wiser man; and, in place of slave-catching, betook himself to life in one of the new settlements, where his talents developed themselves more happily in trapping bears, wolves, and other inhabitants of the forest. Tom always spoke reverently of the Quakers. "Nice people," he would say; "wanted to convert me, but couldn't come it, exactly. But, tell ye what, stranger, they do fix up a sick fellow first rate,—no mistake. Make jist the tallest kind o' broth and knicknacks."

As he had informed them that their party would be looked for in Sandusky, it was thought prudent to divide them. Jim, a fellow fugitive who had been sheltered at the same Quaker farm, with his old mother, was forwarded separately; and a night or two after, George and Eliza, with their child, were driven privately into Sandusky, and lodged beneath a hospital roof, preparatory to taking their last passage on the lake.

Their night was now far spent, and the morning star of liberty rose fair before them!—electric word! What is it? What is freedom to a nation, but freedom to the individuals in it? What is freedom to that young man, who sits there, with his arms folded over his broad chest, the tint of African blood in his cheek, its dark fires in his eyes,—what is freedom to George Harris? To your fathers, freedom was the right of a nation to be a nation. To him, it is the right of a man to be a man, and not a brute; the right to call the wife of his bosom his wife, and to protect her from lawless violence; the right to protect and educate his child; the right to have a home of his own, a religion of his own, a character of his own, unsubject to the will of another. All these thoughts were rolling and seething in George's breast, as he leaned his head on his hand, watching his wife, as she was adapting to her slender and pretty form the articles of man's attire, in which it was deemed safest she should make her escape.

"Now for it," said she, as she stood before the glass, and shook down her silky abundance of black curly hair. "I say, George, it's almost a pity, isn't it," she said, playfully holding up a lock,—"pity it's all got to come off?"

George smiled sadly, and made no answer.

Eliza turned to the glass, and the scissors glittered as one long lock after another was detached from her head.

"There, now, that'll do," she said, taking up a hair-brush; "now for a few fancy touches."

"There, an't I a pretty young fellow?" she said, turning around to her husband, laughing and blushing at the same time.

"You always will be pretty, do what you will," said George.

"What does make you so sober?" said Eliza, kneeling on one knee, and laying her hand on his. "We are only within twenty-four hours of Canada, they say. Only a day and a night on the lake, and then—oh, then!—"

"O, Eliza!" said George, drawing her towards him; "that is it! Now my fate is all narrowing down to a point. To come so near, to be almost in sight, and then lose all. I should never live under it, Eliza."

"Don't fear," said his wife, hopefully. "The good Lord would not have brought us so far, if he didn't mean to carry us through. I seem to feel him with us, George."

"You are a blessed woman, Eliza!" said George, clasping her with a convulsive grasp. "But,—oh, tell me! can this great mercy be for us? Will these years and years of misery come to an end?—shall we be free?"

"I am sure of it, George," said Eliza, looking upward, while tears of hope and enthusiasm shone on her long, dark lashes. "I feel it in me, that God is going to bring us out of bondage, this very day."

"I will believe you, Eliza," said George, rising suddenly up, "I will believe,—come let's be off. Well, indeed," said he, holding her off at arm's length, and looking admiringly at her, "you _are_ a pretty little fellow. That crop of little, short curls, is quite becoming. Put on your cap. So—a little to one side. I never saw you look quite so pretty. But, it's almost time for the carriage;—I wonder if the woman has got Harry rigged?"

The door opened, and a respectable, middle-aged woman entered, leading little Harry, dressed in girl's clothes.

"What a pretty girl he makes," said Eliza, turning him round. "We call him Harriet, you see;—don't the name come nicely?"

The child stood gravely regarding his mother in her new and strange attire, observing a profound silence, and occasionally drawing deep sighs, and peeping at her from under his dark curls.

"Does Harry know mamma?" said Eliza, stretching her hands toward him.

The child clung shyly to the woman.

"Come, Eliza," said George, "why do you try to coax him, when you know that he has got to be kept away from you?"

"I know it's foolish," said Eliza; "yet, I can't bear to have him turn away from me. But come,—where's my cloak? Here,—how is it men put on cloaks, George?"

"You must wear it so," said her husband, throwing it over his shoulders.

"So, then," said Eliza, imitating the motion,—"and I must stamp, and take long steps, and try to look saucy."

"Don't exert yourself," said George. "There is, now and then, a modest young man; and I think it would be easier for you to act that character. And those gloves,—keep them on pretty strictly; your slender paw might bring us all out."

"I've heard," said the woman, "that there have been men down, warning all the packet captains against a man and woman, with a little boy."

"They have!" said George. "Well, if we see any such people, we can tell them."

A hack now drove to the door, and the friendly family who had received the fugitives crowded around them with farewell greetings.

The disguises the party had assumed were in accordance with the hints of Tom Loker. The woman, being fortunately about crossing the lake to return to the Canadian settlement whither they were fleeing, had consented to appear as the aunt of little Harry; and, to attach him to her, he had been kept two days under her sole charge, with much petting, seed-cakes, and candy.

The hack drove to the wharf. The two young men, as they appeared, walked up the plank into the boat, Eliza gallantly giving her arm to the woman, and George attending to their baggage.

George was standing at the captain's office, settling for his party, when he overheard two men talking by his side.

"I've watched every one that came on board," said one, "and I know they're not on this boat."

"You would scarcely know the woman from a white one," said the other. "The man is a very light mulatto; he has a brand in one of his hands."

The hand with which George was taking the tickets and change trembled a little; but he turned coolly around, fixed an unconcerned glance on the face of the speaker, and walked leisurely toward another part of the boat, where Eliza stood waiting for him.

The woman, with little Harry, sought the seclusion of the ladies' cabin, where the dark beauty of the supposed little girl drew many flattering comments from the passengers.

The bell rang out its farewell peal; the plank was drawn. George had the satisfaction to see the suspicious talkers walk down to the shore; and he drew a long sigh of relief, when the boat had put a returnless distance between them.

It was a superb day. The blue waves of Lake Erie danced, rippling and sparkling, in the sun-light. A fresh breeze blew from the shore, and the lordly boat ploughed her way right gallantly onward. O, what an untold world there is in one human heart! Who thought, as George walked calmly up and down the deck of the steamer, with his shy companion at his side, of all that was burning in his bosom? The good that seemed approaching seemed too good, too fair, even to be a reality; and he felt a jealous dread, every moment of the day, that something would rise to snatch it from him.

But the boat swept on. Hours fleeted, and, at last, clear and full rose the blessed English shores; shores charmed by a mighty spell,—with one touch to dissolve every incantation of slavery, no matter in what language pronounced, or by what national power confirmed.

George and his wife stood arm in arm, as the boat neared the small town of Amherstberg, in Canada. His breath grew thick and short; a mist gathered before his eyes; he silently pressed the little hand that lay trembling on his arm. The bell rang; the boat stopped. Scarcely seeing what he did, he looked out his baggage, and gathered his little party. The little company were landed on the shore. They stood still till the boat had cleared; and then, with tears and embracings, the husband and wife, with their wondering child in their arms, knelt down and lifted up their hearts to God!

"'T was something like the burst from death to life;
    From the grave's cerements to the robes of heaven;...
When Mercy's hand hath turned the golden key,
And Mercy's voice hath said, _Rejoice, thy soul is free."

The little party were soon guided to the hospitable abode of a good missionary, whom Christian charity has placed here as a shepherd to the outcast and wandering, who are constantly finding an asylum on this shore.

Who can speak the blessedness of that first day of freedom? Is not the _sense_ of liberty a higher and a finer one than any of the five? To move, speak and breathe,—go out and come in unwatched, and free from danger! Who can speak the blessings of that rest which comes down on the free man's pillow, under laws which insure to him the rights that God has given to man? How fair and precious to that mother was that sleeping child's face, endeared by the memory of a thousand dangers! How impossible was it to sleep, in the exuberant possession of such blessedness! And yet, these two had not one acre of ground,—not a roof that they could call their own,—they had spent their all, to the last dollar. They had nothing more than the birds of the air, or the flowers of the field,—yet they could not sleep for joy.

"O, ye who take freedom from man, with what words shall ye answer it to God?"

The Victory

"Thanks be unto God, who giveth us the victory."

Have not many of us, in the weary way of life, felt how far easier it were to die than to live? Death may come with a thrill that braces courage; but to live—to wear on, day by day, in mean, bitter, harassing servitude; to have every nerve dampened, every feeling smothered—this slow heart-martyrdom is the searching test of man and woman.

When Tom first stood face to face with his persecutor and thought his hour come, the vision of Jesus and heaven seemed but a step beyond, and he felt he could bear anything. But, when the excitement passed, came back the pain of his bruised limbs, the sense of his degraded, hopeless estate; and the day went heavily.

Long before his wounds were healed, Legree drove him to the field. Day after day of pain and weariness followed, with every indignity malice could devise. There was no leisure for the Bible; in the height of the season Legree pressed all hands through Sundays and week-days alike. At first Tom read a verse or two by the fire's flicker; but soon he came home so exhausted his head swam, his eyes failed, and he sank down with the rest.

Is it strange that the peace and trust which had upborne him should give way to darkness? The gloomiest problem met him ever—souls crushed, evil triumphant, and God silent. Weeks and months he wrestled so. He prayed that God would send deliverance, watched day after day in vague hope of some hand stretched to redeem; and, when none came, bitter thoughts rose that it was vain to serve God, that God had forgotten him. Sometimes he saw Cassy, and now and then, when summoned to the house, the dejected Emmeline; but there was no time to commune with any.

One evening, in utter dejection, he sat by a few decaying brands, his coarse supper baking. He fed the flicker, drew his worn Bible; the marked passages that had so often thrilled him lay there—the courage of patriarchs, seers and witnesses. But the failing eye and weary sense answered not to the mighty touch. Heavily sighing, he put it in his pocket.

A coarse laugh roused him. Legree stood opposite.

"Well, old boy," he said, "you find your religion don't work, it seems! I thought I should get that through your wool, at last!"

Tom was silent.

"You were a fool," said Legree. "I meant to do well by you when I bought you. You might have been better off than Sambo, had easy times; instead of being cut up and thrashed, you might have lorded it round, and cut up the other niggers; had now and then a good warming of whiskey punch. Come, Tom, don't you think you'd better be reasonable?—heave that ar old pack of trash in the fire, and join my church!"

"The Lord forbid!" said Tom, fervently.

"You see the Lord an't going to help you; if he had been, he wouldn't have let me get you! This yer religion is a mess of lying trumpery, Tom. Ye'd better hold to me; I'm somebody, and can do something!"

"No, Mas'r," said Tom; "I'll hold on. The Lord may help me, or not help; but I'll hold to him, and believe him to the last!"

"The more fool you!" Legree spat, spurned him with his foot. "Never mind; I'll chase you down, yet, and bring you under—you'll see!" And he turned away.

When a heavy weight presses the soul to the lowest level it can endure, there comes a desperate effort of every nerve to throw it off; and the heaviest anguish often precedes a return tide of courage. So now. The atheistic taunts drove Tom's desponding soul to the ebb; faith still held, but numb and despairing. He sat, as one stunned. Suddenly all around faded, and a vision rose—One crowned with thorns, buffeted and bleeding. Tom gazed in awe at the majestic patience; the deep pathetic eyes thrilled him. With floods of emotion he stretched his hands and fell upon his knees—when, gradually, the vision changed: the thorns became rays of glory; and, in splendor inconceivable, he saw that same face bending compassionately toward him, and a voice said, "He that overcometh shall sit down with me on my throne, even as I also overcame, and am set down with my Father on his throne."

How long he lay he knew not. When he came to himself, the fire was out; the chill dews had drenched him. But the dread crisis was past. Hunger, cold, degradation, disappointment—they were as nothing. From his deepest soul he loosed every earthly hope, and offered his will a sacrifice to the Infinite. He looked up to the silent stars, and the solitude rang with the words of a hymn he had sung often, but never so:

"The earth shall be dissolved like snow,
    The sun shall cease to shine;
But God, who called me here below,
    Shall be forever mine.

"And when this mortal life shall fail,
    And flesh and sense shall cease,
I shall possess within the veil
    A life of joy and peace.

"When we've been there ten thousand years,
    Bright shining like the sun,
We've no less days to sing God's praise
    Than when we first begun."

Such relations are common among the religious histories of the slaves. Who shall measure what the All-pervading Spirit may do to encourage desponding souls of the desolate? If the poor forgotten one believes Jesus hath appeared and spoken to him, who shall contradict? Did He not proclaim to bind up the broken-hearted, and set at liberty them that are bruised?

When the dim gray of dawn woke them to the field, among the tattered and shivering was one who walked with exultant tread; firmer than the ground was his faith in almighty, eternal love. Ah, Legree, try all your forces now! Agony, want, loss of all things, shall but hasten him to be made a king and a priest unto God.

From that time, a sphere of peace encompassed the lowly heart. Past bleeding regrets; past hope's fluctuations; the bent and struggling will was merged in the Divine. Life's remaining voyage seemed short; eternal blessedness near and vivid; and life's uttermost woes fell unharming.

All noticed the change. Cheerfulness and alertness returned; no insult ruffled him.

"What the devil's got into Tom?" said Legree to Sambo. "A while ago he was down in the mouth, and now he's peart as a cricket."

"Dunno, Mas'r; gwine to run off, mebbe."

"Like to see him try that," said Legree, with a savage grin.

That night, riding home, he turned his horse to the quarters. It was a superb moonlight; China-tree shadows lay pencilled on the turf; the very stillness seemed almost unholy to disturb. He paused: some one was singing. A musical tenor rose:

"When I can read my title clear
    To mansions in the skies,
I'll bid farewell to every fear,
    And wipe my weeping eyes.

"Should earth against my soul engage,
    And hellish darts be hurled,
Then I can smile at Satan's rage,
    And face a frowning world.

"Let cares like a wild deluge come,
    And storms of sorrow fall,
May I but safely reach my home,
    My God, my Heaven, my All."

"So ho!" muttered Legree. "He thinks so, does he? How I hate these cursed Methodist hymns!" He burst out, raised his riding-whip. "How dare you get up this row, when you ought to be in bed? Shut yer old black gash, and get along in with you!"

"Yes, Mas'r," said Tom, with ready cheerfulness, rising.

Provoked beyond measure at his evident happiness, Legree belabored him over head and shoulders.

"There, you dog," he said, "see if you'll feel so comfortable, after that!"

But the blows fell only on the outer man. Tom stood submissive; yet Legree felt his power was somehow gone. As Tom disappeared, a vivid flash crossed his dark mind: he understood it was GOD who stood between him and his victim, and he blasphemed. That submissive, silent man, whom taunts and stripes could not disturb, roused within him the old demoniac cry, "What have we to do with thee, thou Jesus of Nazareth?—art thou come to torment us before the time?"

Tom's soul overflowed with compassion for the poor wretches around him. It seemed his own sorrows were over; out of a strange treasury of peace he longed to pour relief. Opportunities were scant; yet on the way to the fields, at the rows, home again, chances came to help the weary and disheartened. At first, the brutalized scarce comprehended; but, week after week, month after month, the strange, silent, patient man—who bore everyone's burden, sought help from none; stood aside for all and came last; shared his little all; in cold nights gave his tattered blanket to some shivering, sick woman; filled the baskets of the weaker at risk of coming short himself; and, though pursued with unrelenting cruelty, never uttered a word of reviling—this man began to have power over them. When the pressing season passed, and Sundays were allowed, many gathered to hear of Jesus. They would gladly have met to pray and sing; but Legree forbade, and broke up attempts with oaths and execrations; so the blessed news crept from lip to lip.

Who shall speak the simple joy with which some, to whom life was a joyless journey to a dark unknown, heard of a compassionate Redeemer and a heavenly home? The poor mulatto, whose faith had been all but crushed by cruelty and wrong, felt her soul raised by the hymns and Scriptures Tom breathed in her ear between tasks; and even the half-crazed, wandering mind of Cassy was soothed by his unobtrusive influence.

Stung to madness by life's agonies, Cassy had often resolved on retribution. One night, after the cabin slept, Tom saw her face at the chink that served for a window. She made a silent gesture.

Tom came out. It was broad, calm moonlight. The light, as it fell on Cassy's large, black eyes, showed a wild glare unlike her wonted fixed despair.

"Come here, Father Tom," she said, laying her small hand on his wrist, drawing him with a force like steel. "Come here,—I've news for you."

"What, Misse Cassy?" said Tom, anxiously.

"Tom, wouldn't you like your liberty?"

"I shall have it, Misse, in God's time," said Tom.

"Ay, but you may have it tonight," she said, with sudden energy. "Come on. He's asleep—sound. I put enough in his brandy to keep him so. I wish I'd had more,—I shouldn't have wanted you. But come; the back door is unlocked; there's an axe there,—his room door is open; I'll show you the way. I'd a done it myself, only my arms are so weak. Come along!"

"Not for ten thousand worlds, Misse!" said Tom, firmly, stopping and holding her back.

"But think of all these poor creatures," said Cassy. "We might set them all free, go to the swamps, find an island, live by ourselves. Any life is better than this."

"No!" said Tom. "No! good never comes of wickedness. I'd sooner chop my right hand off!"

"Then I shall do it," said Cassy, turning.

"O, Misse Cassy!" cried Tom, flinging himself before her, "for the dear Lord's sake that died for ye, don't sell your precious soul to the devil, that way! Nothing but evil will come of it. The Lord hasn't called us to wrath. We must suffer, and wait his time."

"Wait!" said Cassy. "Haven't I waited?—waited till my head is dizzy and my heart sick? What has he made me suffer? What has he made hundreds suffer? Isn't he wringing the life-blood out of you? I'm called on; they call me! His time's come, and I'll have his heart's blood!"

"No, no, no!" said Tom, holding her small hands clenched with spasmodic violence. "No, ye poor, lost soul, that ye mustn't do. The dear, blessed Lord never shed no blood but his own; and that he poured out for us when we was enemies. Lord, help us to follow his steps, and love our enemies."

"Love!" said Cassy, with a fierce glare; "love such enemies! It isn't in flesh and blood."

"No, Misse, it isn't," said Tom, looking up; "but He gives it to us, and that's the victory. When we can love and pray over all and through all, the battle's past, and the victory's come,—glory be to God!"

And this, O Africa! latest called of nations—called to the crown of thorns, the scourge, the bloody sweat—this is to be thy victory; by this shalt thou reign with Christ when his kingdom shall come on earth.

The deep fervor of Tom's feeling, the softness of his voice, his tears, fell like dew on the wild, unsettled spirit. A softness gathered over the lurid fires of her eye; she looked down; the muscles of her hands relaxed.

"Evil spirits follow me," she said, in a low voice; "O! Father Tom, I can't pray,—I wish I could. I never have prayed since my children were sold! What you say must be right; but when I try, I can only hate and curse. I can't pray!"

"Poor soul!" said Tom, compassionately. "Satan desires to have ye, and sift ye as wheat. I pray the Lord for ye. O! Misse Cassy, turn to the dear Lord Jesus. He came to bind up the broken-hearted, and comfort all that mourn."

Cassy stood silent; large, heavy tears dropped from her eyes.

"Misse Cassy," said Tom, hesitating, "if ye only could get away from here,—if the thing was possible,—I'd 'vise ye and Emmeline to do it; that is, if ye could go without blood-guiltiness,—not otherwise."

"Would you try it with us, Father Tom?"

"No," said Tom; "time was when I would; but the Lord's given me a work among these yer poor souls, and I'll stay with 'em, and bear my cross with 'em, till the end. It's different with you; it's a snare to you,—it's more'n you can stand,—and you'd better go, if you can."

"I know no way but through the grave," said Cassy. "There's no beast or bird but finds a home somewhere; even snakes and alligators have their places to lie down and be quiet; but there's no place for us. Down in the darkest swamps their dogs will hunt us out. Everybody and everything is against us; even the very beasts side against us,—and where shall we go?"

Tom was silent; at length he said,

"Him that saved Daniel in the den of lions,—that saved the children in the fiery furnace,—Him that walked on the sea, and bade the winds be still,—He's alive yet; and I've faith to believe he can deliver you. Try it, and I'll pray, with all my might, for you."

By what strange law of mind is it that an idea long trodden under foot suddenly sparkles out as a discovered jewel? Cassy had often revolved schemes of escape and dismissed them as hopeless; but now, in a flash, a plan arose, so simple and feasible as to awaken instant hope.

"Father Tom, I'll try it!" she said, suddenly.

"Amen!" said Tom; "the Lord help ye!"

The Stratagem

“The way of the wicked is as darkness; he knoweth not at what he stumbleth.”

The garret of the house that Legree occupied was a great, desolate space, dusty, hung with cobwebs, and littered with cast-off lumber. A former opulent family had left behind tall, high-backed chairs and dusty tables in mouldering rooms, and one or two immense packing-boxes in which the furniture had come stood against the sides of the garret. A small window, with dingy panes, let in a scant, uncertain light. Ghostly enough in itself, it wanted not for legends to increase its terrors. Some years before, a woman who had incurred Legree’s displeasure had been confined there for weeks. What passed there, the negroes whispered darkly; but it was known her body was one day taken down and buried; and afterward, oaths and cursings, and the sound of violent blows, were said to ring through that old garret, mingled with wailings and groans. Once, when Legree overheard something of this, he flew into a passion, and swore the next teller of tales should have a chance to know what was there; he would chain them there a week. The hint suppressed talk, though it did not disturb the story’s credit in the least.

Gradually the staircase to the garret, and even the passage to it, were avoided; the legend was falling into desuetude. It suddenly occurred to Cassy to make use of the superstitious excitability, so great in Legree, for the purpose of her liberation and that of her fellow-sufferer, Emmeline.

Cassy’s sleeping-room was directly under the garret. One day, without consulting Legree, she took it on herself, with some ostentation, to change all the furniture of the room to one at a distance. The under-servants, called to effect the movement, were bustling, when Legree returned from a ride.

“Hallo! you Cass! what’s in the wind now?”

“Nothing; only I choose to have another room,” said Cassy, doggedly.

“And what for, pray?”

“I choose to.”

“The devil you do! and what for?”

“I’d like to get some sleep, now and then.”

“Sleep! well, what hinders your sleeping?”

“I could tell, I suppose, if you want to hear,” said Cassy, dryly.

“Speak out, you minx!”

“O! nothing. I suppose it wouldn’t disturb you! Only groans, and people scuffing, and rolling round on the garret floor, half the night, from twelve to morning!”

“People up garret!” said Legree, uneasily, forcing a laugh. “Who are they, Cassy?”

She raised her sharp, black eyes, and looked him in the face with an expression that went through his bones. “To be sure, Simon, who are they? I’d like to have you tell me. You don’t know, I suppose!”

With an oath, Legree struck at her with his riding-whip; she glided aside, passed through the door, and, looking back, said, “If you’ll sleep in that room, you’ll know all about it. Perhaps you’d better try it!” She shut and locked the door.

Legree blustered, swore, threatened to break it down; then walked uneasily into the sitting-room. Cassy saw her shaft had struck home, and, from that hour, with exquisite address, she never ceased to continue the train of influences she had begun.

In a knot-hole of the garret, she inserted the neck of an old bottle, so that, with the least wind, most doleful wailing sounds proceeded from it, increasing, in a high wind, to a perfect shriek—such as, to credulous, superstitious ears, might well seem the voice of horror and despair.

From time to time the servants heard these sounds, and the memory of the old legend revived. A creeping horror seemed to fill the house; and, though none dared breathe it to Legree, he found himself encompassed by it, as by an atmosphere.

The influence of Cassy over him was strange. He was her owner, tyrant, tormentor; she, wholly in his hands: yet the most brutal man cannot live constantly within a strong female influence and not be greatly controlled by it. When he first bought her, delicately bred, he crushed her, without scruple, beneath his foot. But time, debasing influences, and despair hardened womanhood within her; fiercer fires waked; she had become in a measure his mistress, and he alternately tyrannized over and dreaded her. Of late, a partial insanity had given a weird, unsettled cast to her words and manner that harassed him the more.

A night or two after, Legree sat in the old sitting-room by a flickering wood fire that threw uncertain glances round. It was a stormy, windy night; windows rattling, shutters flapping, wind carousing down the chimney and puffing out smoke and ashes, as if a legion of spirits were after them. He had been casting up accounts and reading newspapers, while Cassy sat in the corner, sullenly looking into the fire. Seeing an old book on the table, which he had noticed in her hands earlier, he took it up,—one of those coarse collections of bloody murders, ghostly legends, and supernatural visitations, strangely fascinating to any one that once begins to read. He poohed and pished, yet turned page after page, till, finally, he threw it down with an oath.

“You don’t believe in ghosts, do you, Cass?” said he, taking the tongs and settling the fire. “I thought you’d more sense than to let noises scare you.”

“No matter what I believe,” said Cassy, sullenly.

“Fellows used to try to frighten me with their yarns at sea,” said Legree. “Never come it round me that way. I’m too tough for any such trash, tell ye.”

Cassy sat looking intensely at him from the shadow. There was that strange light in her eyes which always impressed him with uneasiness.

“Them noises was nothing but rats and the wind,” said Legree. “Rats will make a devil of a noise; I used to hear ’em sometimes down in the hold; and wind,—Lord’s sake! ye can make anything out o’ wind.”

She made no answer; she fixed her glittering eyes on him with that unearthly expression.

“Come, speak out, woman,—don’t you think so?”

“Can rats walk down stairs, and come walking through the entry, and open a door when you’ve locked it and set a chair against it?” said Cassy; “and come walk, walk, walking right up to your bed, and put out their hand, so?”

She kept her eyes on him; he stared like a man in a nightmare, till—when she finished by laying her hand, icy cold, on his—he sprung back, with an oath.

“Woman! what do you mean? Nobody did?”

“O, no,—of course not,—did I say they did?” she answered, with a smile of chilling derision.

“But—did—have you really seen?—Come, Cass, what is it, now,—speak out!”

“You may sleep there, yourself, if you want to know.”

“Did it come from the garret, Cassy?”

“It,—what?”

“Why, what you told of—”

“I didn’t tell you anything,” said Cassy, doggedly.

He walked up and down, uneasily. “I’ll have this yer thing examined. I’ll look into it, this very night. I’ll take my pistols—”

“Do,” said Cassy; “sleep in that room. I’d like to see you doing it. Fire your pistols,—do!”

He stamped and swore.

“Don’t swear,” said Cassy; “nobody knows who may be hearing you. Hark! What was that?”

“What?” said Legree, starting.

A heavy old Dutch clock in the corner began, and slowly struck twelve. For some reason, he neither spoke nor moved; a vague horror fell on him; Cassy, with a keen, sneering glitter in her eyes, counted the strokes.

“Twelve o’clock; well now we’ll see,” said she, turning, opening the door into the passage, and standing as if listening. “Hark! What’s that?” said she, raising her finger.

“It’s only the wind,” said Legree. “Don’t you hear how cursedly it blows?”

“Simon, come here,” said Cassy, in a whisper, laying her hand on his and leading him to the foot of the stairs: “do you know what that is? Hark!”

A wild shriek came pealing down the stairway—from the garret. His knees knocked together; his face grew white.

“Hadn’t you better get your pistols?” said Cassy, with a sneer that froze his blood. “It’s time this thing was looked into, you know. I’d like to have you go up now; they’re at it.”

“I won’t go!” said Legree, with an oath.

“Why not? There an’t any such thing as ghosts, you know! Come!” And she flitted up the winding stairway, laughing, and looking back. “Come on.”

“I believe you are the devil!” said Legree. “Come back you hag,—come back, Cass! You shan’t go!”

She laughed wildly and fled on. He heard her open the entry doors that led to the garret. A wild gust of wind swept down, extinguishing the candle he held, and with it,—fearful, unearthly,—the screams; they seemed shrieked in his very ear. He fled frantically into the parlor. In a few moments Cassy followed,—pale, calm, cold as an avenging spirit, and with that same fearful light in her eye.

“I hope you are satisfied,” said she.

“Blast you, Cass!”

“What for? I only went up and shut the doors. What’s the matter with that garret, Simon, do you suppose?”

“None of your business!”

“O, it an’t? Well,” said Cassy, “at any rate, I’m glad I don’t sleep under it.”

Anticipating the rising wind, that evening Cassy had opened the garret window; of course, the moment the doors were opened, the draft had swept down and extinguished the light. This may serve as a specimen of the game she played till he would sooner have put his head into a lion’s mouth than explore that garret.

Meanwhile, in the night, when all else slept, she slowly and carefully accumulated there provisions sufficient for some time; she transferred, article by article, the greater part of her own and Emmeline’s wardrobe. All being arranged, they only waited a fitting opportunity.

By cajoling Legree, and taking advantage of a good-natured interval, she had got him to take her to a neighboring town on the Red River. With memory sharpened to preternatural clearness, she marked every turn in the road and formed an estimate of the time to traverse it.

When all was matured, we look behind the scenes and see the final coup d’état. Near evening: Legree had been absent on a ride. For many days Cassy had been unusually gracious and accommodating; they had seemed on the best of terms. Now behold her and Emmeline in the latter’s room, busy over two small bundles.

“There, these will be large enough,” said Cassy. “Now put on your bonnet, and let’s start; it’s just about the right time.”

“Why, they can see us yet,” said Emmeline.

“I mean they shall,” said Cassy, coolly. “Don’t you know they must have their chase, at any rate? The way is this:—we will steal out by the back door, and run down by the quarters. One of the overseers will be sure to see us. They will give chase, and we will get into the swamp; then they can’t follow us further till they go and give the alarm, and turn out the dogs, and so on; and, while they are blundering round and tumbling over each other, as they always do, you and I will slip along to the creek that runs back of the house, and wade in it till we get opposite the back door. That will put the dogs all at fault; scent won’t lie in the water. Every one will run out to look after us, and then we’ll whip in at the back door, and up into the garret, where I’ve a nice bed in one of the great boxes. We must stay there a good while; he will raise heaven and earth after us. He’ll muster old overseers from the plantations, and have a great hunt; they’ll go over every inch of ground in that swamp. He makes it his boast that nobody ever got away from him. So let him hunt at his leisure.”

“Cassy, how well you have planned it!” said Emmeline. “Who ever would have thought of it, but you?”

There was neither pleasure nor exultation in Cassy’s eyes,—only a despairing firmness.

“Come,” she said, reaching her hand.

The two fugitives glided noiselessly from the house, and flitted, through gathering shadows, along by the quarters. A crescent moon delayed night a little. As Cassy expected, when near the verge of the swamps that encircled the plantation, they heard a voice calling them to stop. It was not an underling, but Legree himself, pursuing with violent execrations. At the sound, the feebler spirit of Emmeline gave way; she clutched Cassy’s arm. “O, Cassy, I’m going to faint!”

“If you do, I’ll kill you!” said Cassy, drawing a small, glittering stiletto, and flashing it before the girl’s eyes.

The diversion wrought its purpose. Emmeline did not faint, but plunged, with Cassy, into a part of the labyrinth so deep and dark that it was perfectly hopeless for Legree to think of following without assistance.

“Well,” said he, chuckling brutally, “at any rate, they’ve got themselves into a trap now—the baggage! They’re safe enough. They shall sweat for it!”

He came to the quarters as the hands were returning from work. “There’s two runaways in the swamps. I’ll give five dollars to any nigger as catches ’em. Turn out the dogs! All hands!”

The sensation was immediate. Many sprang forward, officiously, for the reward or from cringing habit. Pine-knots were kindled; the dogs were uncoupled; their hoarse, savage bay added to the animation.

“Shall we shoot ’em, if can’t cotch ’em?” asked one, to whom the master brought out a rifle.

“You may fire on Cass, if you like; it’s time she was gone to the devil; but the gal, not,” said Legree. “And now, boys, be spry and smart. Five dollars for him that gets ’em; and a glass of spirits to every one of you, anyhow.”

With the glare of blazing torches, with whoop and shout and savage yell of man and beast, the band proceeded down to the swamp, followed, at some distance, by every servant in the house. The establishment was, in consequence, wholly deserted, when Cassy and Emmeline glided in the back way. The whooping and shouts still filled the air; and, looking from the sitting-room windows, they could see the troop, with their flambeaux, just dispersing along the swamp’s edge.

“See there!” said Emmeline. “The hunt is begun! Hark! the dogs! If we were only there, our chances wouldn’t be worth a picayune. O, for pity’s sake, do let’s hide ourselves. Quick!”

“There’s no occasion for hurry,” said Cassy, coolly; “they are all out after the hunt,—that’s the amusement of the evening! We’ll go up stairs, by and by. Meanwhile,”—and she took deliberately a key from the pocket of a coat Legree had thrown down in his hurry,—“meanwhile I shall take something to pay our passage.”

She unlocked the desk, took out a roll of bills, and counted rapidly.

“O, don’t let’s do that!” said Emmeline.

“Don’t! why not? Would you have us starve in the swamps, or have that that will pay our way to the free states? Money will do anything, girl.” She put the money in her bosom.

“It would be stealing,” said Emmeline, in a distressed whisper.

“Stealing!” said Cassy, with a scornful laugh. “They who steal body and soul needn’t talk to us. Every one of these bills is stolen,—stolen from poor, starving, sweating creatures, who must go to the devil at last, for his profit. Let him talk about stealing! But come, we may as well go up garret; I’ve got a stock of candles there, and some books to pass away the time. You may be pretty sure they won’t come there to inquire after us. If they do, I’ll play ghost for them.”

When Emmeline reached the garret, she found an immense box, in which some heavy pieces of furniture had once been brought, turned on its side, the opening facing the eaves. Cassy lit a small lamp; creeping round under the slant, they established themselves in it. It was spread with a couple of small mattresses and some pillows; a box near by was stored with candles, provisions, and all the clothing necessary, which Cassy had arranged into bundles of an astonishingly small compass.

“There,” said Cassy, fixing the lamp into a small hook she had driven into the side; “this is to be our home for the present. How do you like it?”

“Are you sure they won’t come and search the garret?”

“I’d like to see Simon Legree doing that,” said Cassy. “No, indeed; he’ll be too glad to keep away. As to the servants, they would any of them stand and be shot, sooner than show their faces here.”
