Somewhat reassured, Emmeline settled back on her pillow.

“What did you mean, Cassy, by saying you would kill me?” she asked, simply.

“I meant to stop your fainting,” said Cassy, “and I did do it. And now I tell you, Emmeline, you must make up your mind not to faint, let what will come; there’s no sort of need of it. If I had not stopped you, that wretch might have had his hands on you now.”

Emmeline shuddered.

They remained some time in silence; Cassy busied herself with a French book; Emmeline, overcome, fell into a doze and slept. She was awakened by loud shouts and outcries, the tramp of horses’ feet, and the baying of dogs. She started up with a faint shriek.

“Only the hunt coming back,” said Cassy, coolly; “never fear. Look out of this knot-hole. Don’t you see ’em all down there? Simon has to give up, for this night. Look, how muddy his horse is, flouncing about in the swamp; the dogs, too, look crestfallen. Ah, my good sir, you’ll have to try the race again and again,—the game isn’t there.”

“O, don’t speak a word!” said Emmeline; “what if they should hear you?”

“If they do hear anything, it will make them very particular to keep away,” said Cassy. “No danger; we may make any noise we please, and it will only add to the effect.”

At length the stillness of midnight settled down over the house. Legree, cursing his ill luck, and vowing dire vengeance on the morrow, went to bed.

The Martyr

“Deem not the just by Heaven forgot!
Though life its common gifts deny,—
Though, with a crushed and bleeding heart,
And spurned of man, he goes to die!
For God hath marked each sorrowing day,
And numbered every bitter tear,
And heaven’s long years of bliss shall pay
For all his children suffer here.”

The longest way must have its close; the gloomiest night wears on to a morning. We have walked with our humble friend through ease and indulgence, through heart-breaking separations, through a sunny island where flowers hid chains, and at last through blackness where the unseen firmament has blazed with stars. The morning-star now stands over the mountains; strange gales, not of earth, show that the gates of day are unclosing.

The escape of Cassy and Emmeline, two fugitive women, stung Legree, the plantation master, to fury; and, as was to be expected, it fell on the defenceless head of Tom, his slave. When he told the hands the tidings, he marked a light in Tom’s eye and that he did not join the muster to pursue. Remembering other times when Tom had proved inflexible in deeds of inhumanity, he forbore, in his haste, to grapple with him then.

Tom remained behind with a few who had learned of him to pray, and they prayed for the fugitives’ escape.

Legree’s baffled return gathered his long hatred into deadly form. Had not this man braved him—steadily, resistlessly—ever since he bought him? Was there not a spirit in him which, silent as it was, burned on him like fires of perdition?

“I hate him!” said Legree, that night, sitting up in his bed. “I hate him! And isn’t he MINE? Can’t I do what I like with him?” He clenched his fist, as if rending something.

But Tom was a faithful, valuable hand; and that, though it sharpened hatred, held him a little in. He would, next day, say nothing—yet; gather men with dogs and guns; surround the swamp; hunt systematically. If it failed, he would summon Tom before him; he would break him down—or—there was a dire whisper to which his soul assented.

“Well,” said Cassy, peering from the garret through the knot-hole, “the hunt’s going to begin again, today!”

Horsemen curveted before the house; two leashes of strange dogs strained and bayed. Overseers from neighboring plantations, and rough companions from a tavern-bar, a hard-favored set, drank Legree’s profuse brandy; negroes, detailed for the sport, were plied, that even this might be made a holiday.

Listening, the morning air blowing to her ear, Cassy heard the men divide the ground, discuss dogs, give orders about firing, and what to do in case of capture. She drew back, lifted her clasped hands. “O, great Almighty God! we are all sinners; but what have we done, more than all the rest of the world, that we should be treated so?”

“If it wasn’t for you, child,” she said to Emmeline, “I’d go out to them; and I’d thank any one that would shoot me down; for what use will freedom be to me? Can it give me back my children, or make me what I used to be?”

Emmeline, half afraid of these dark moods, took her hand with a gentle motion.

“Don’t!” said Cassy, trying to draw it away. “You’ll get me to loving you; and I never mean to love anything, again!”

“Poor Cassy! don’t feel so! If the Lord gives us liberty, perhaps he’ll give you back your daughter; at any rate, I’ll be like a daughter to you. I know I’ll never see my poor old mother again! I shall love you, Cassy, whether you love me or not!”

The child-like spirit conquered. Cassy put an arm round her, stroked her soft hair; and Emmeline wondered at the beauty of those magnificent eyes, now soft with tears.

“O, Em!” said Cassy, “I’ve hungered for my children, and thirsted for them! Here! here!”—she struck her breast—“it’s all desolate, all empty! If God would give me back my children, then I could pray.”

“You must trust him, Cassy,” said Emmeline; “he is our Father!”

“His wrath is upon us,” said Cassy. “He has turned away in anger.”

“No, Cassy! He will be good to us! Let us hope in Him,—I always have had hope.”

The hunt was long, animated, thorough, but unsuccessful; and, with grave, ironic exultation, Cassy looked down on Legree as, weary, he alighted from his horse.

“Now, Quimbo,” said Legree, flinging himself down in the sitting-room, “you jest go and walk that Tom up here, right away! The old cuss is at the bottom of this whole matter; and I’ll have it out of his old black hide!”

Sambo and Quimbo, his brutal drivers, hated Tom with a servile envy sharpened by their master’s displeasure, and the errand was welcome. Tom heard with a forewarning heart. He knew the fugitives’ concealment, knew the deadly character of the man and his despotic power; but he felt strong in God to meet death rather than betray the helpless.

He set down his basket, looked up, and said, “Into thy hands I commend my spirit! Thou hast redeemed me, oh Lord God of truth!” Then he quietly yielded to Quimbo’s brutal grasp.

“Ay, ay!” said the giant, dragging him. “Ye’ll cotch it, now! See how ye’ll look, now, helpin’ Mas’r’s niggers to run away!”

Those savage words never reached that ear; a Higher Voice said, “Fear not them that kill the body, and, after that, have no more that they can do.” Nerve and bone vibrated, as if touched by the finger of God. The huts, the rows, the whole scene of his degradation seemed to whirl by like landscape past a rushing car. His soul throbbed,—home in sight,—release at hand.

“Well, Tom!” said Legree, seizing him by the collar, teeth set with determined rage, “do you know I’ve made up my mind to KILL YOU?”

“It’s very likely, Mas’r,” said Tom, calmly.

“I have—done—just—that—thing, Tom, unless you’ll tell me what you know about these yer gals!”

Tom stood silent.

“D’ye hear?” roared Legree, stamping. “Speak!”

“I han’t got nothing to tell, Mas’r,” said Tom, with slow, firm utterance.

“Do you dare to tell me, ye old black Christian, ye don’t know?”

Tom was silent.

“Speak!” thundered Legree, striking him. “Do you know anything?”

“I know, Mas’r; but I can’t tell anything. I can die!”

Legree drew in a long breath; then, holding him by the arm, his face close, said, terribly, “Hark ’e, Tom!—ye think, ’cause I’ve let you off before, I don’t mean what I say; but, this time, I’ve made up my mind, and counted the cost. You’ve always stood it out again’ me: now, I’ll conquer ye, or kill ye!—one or t’ other. I’ll count every drop of blood there is in you, and take ’em, one by one, till ye give up!”

Tom looked up and answered, “Mas’r, if you was sick, or in trouble, or dying, and I could save ye, I’d give ye my heart’s blood; and, if taking every drop of blood in this poor old body would save your precious soul, I’d give ’em freely, as the Lord gave his for me. O, Mas’r! don’t bring this great sin on your soul! It will hurt you more than ’t will me! Do the worst you can, my troubles’ll be over soon; but, if ye don’t repent, yours won’t never end!”

For a moment—like heavenly music in a lull—there was silence; the tick of the old clock measured the last moments of mercy and probation to that hardened heart. One hesitating pause—one relenting thrill—and the evil spirit rushed back seven-fold. Foaming, Legree smote his victim to the ground.

Scenes of blood and cruelty are shocking to our ear and heart. What man has nerve to do, man has not nerve to hear; and yet, O my country! these things are done under the shadow of thy laws! But of old there was One whose suffering changed an instrument of torture and shame into a symbol of glory and immortal life; and, where His spirit is, neither stripes nor blood can make the Christian’s last struggle less than glorious.

Was Tom alone, that long night, while his brave, loving spirit bore up, in an old shed, against buffeting and brutal stripes?

Nay! There stood by him One,—seen by him alone,—“like unto the Son of God.”

The tempter stood by him, too,—furious, despotic will,—every moment pressing him to shun agony by betraying the innocent. But the brave, true heart was firm on the Eternal Rock. Like his Master, he knew that, if he saved others, himself he could not save; nor could utmost extremity wring from him words, save prayers and holy trust.

“He’s most gone, Mas’r,” said Sambo, touched, in spite of himself, by the patience of his victim.

“Pay away, till he gives up! Give it to him!—give it to him!” shouted Legree. “I’ll take every drop of blood he has, unless he confesses!”

Tom opened his eyes and looked on his master. “Ye poor miserable critter!” he said; “there ain’t no more ye can do! I forgive ye, with all my soul!” He fainted away.

“I b’lieve, my soul, he’s done for, finally,” said Legree, stooping to look. “Yes, he is! Well, his mouth’s shut up, at last,—that’s one comfort!”

Yes, Legree; but who shall shut up that voice in thy soul?—that soul, past repentance, past prayer, past hope?

Yet Tom was not quite gone. His wondrous words and pious prayers had struck upon the hearts of the imbruted blacks who had been instruments of cruelty. When Legree withdrew, they took him down, washed his wounds, made a rude bed of refuse cotton, and one, stealing to the house, begged a drink of brandy, as if for himself, and poured it down Tom’s throat.

“O, Tom!” said Quimbo, “we’s been awful wicked to ye!”

“I forgive ye, with all my heart!” said Tom, faintly.

“Sartin, we’s been doin’ a drefful wicked thing!” said Sambo. “Hopes Mas’r’ll have to ’count for it, and not we.”

“O, Tom! do tell us who is Jesus, anyhow?” said Sambo; “Jesus, that’s been a standin’ by you so, all this night!—Who is he?”

The word roused the failing spirit. He poured forth a few energetic sentences of that wondrous One,—his life, his death, his everlasting presence, and power to save.

They wept,—both the two savage men.

“Why didn’t I never hear this before?” said Sambo. “But I do believe!—I can’t help it! Lord Jesus, have mercy on us!”

“Poor critters!” said Tom. “I’d be willing to bar all I have, if it’ll only bring ye to Christ! O, Lord! give me these two more souls, I pray!”

That prayer was answered.

The Young Master

Two days after, a young man drove a light wagon up the avenue of China trees, flung the reins on the horse's neck, sprang out, and inquired for the owner.

It was George Shelby; and how he came there must be briefly told.

Miss Ophelia's letter to Mrs. Shelby, by some unlucky detention at a remote post-office, lay for months before it reached its mark; and, ere it was received, Tom had vanished into the distant swamps of Red River. Mrs. Shelby read with sore anxiety; but immediate action was impossible. Her husband lay delirious in the crisis of a fever; and Master George, grown from boy to tall youth, was her sole reliance in ordering his father's affairs. Guided by the name of the St. Clare lawyer, which Miss Ophelia had thoughtfully sent, they wrote; but the answer came—he knew nothing. Tom had been sold at public auction; the money was paid; there the knowledge ended. Then Mr. Shelby died; and the absorbing pressure of settling large and tangled estates fell, by his appointment, on his wife as sole executrix. With characteristic energy, she and George set themselves to reduce all to order; yet neither could rest easy. Six months later, business taking him down the river, George resolved to go to New Orleans, push inquiry in person, and, if possible, restore their old friend.

After weary search, by the merest chance, he met a man who knew; and, money in his pocket, he took steamboat for Red River, resolved to find and re-purchase Tom.

He was soon introduced into the house, where he found Legree in the sitting-room. Legree received him with a kind of surly hospitality.

"I understand," said the young man, "that you bought, in New Orleans, a boy, named Tom. He used to be on my father's place, and I came to see if I couldn't buy him back."

Legree's brow darkened, and he broke out, passionately: "Yes, I did buy such a fellow,—and a h—l of a bargain I had of it, too! The most rebellious, saucy, impudent dog! Set up my niggers to run away; got off two gals, worth eight hundred or a thousand apiece. He owned to that; and, when I bid him tell me where they was, he up and said he knew, but he wouldn't tell; and stood to it, though I gave him the cussedest flogging I ever gave nigger yet. I b'lieve he's trying to die; but I don't know as he'll make it out."

"Where is he?" said George, impetuously. "Let me see him." His cheek crimsoned, and his eye flashed; but he prudently held in his words.

"He's in dat ar shed," said a little fellow, who stood holding George's horse.

Legree kicked the boy, and swore at him; but George, without reply, strode to the spot.

Tom had lain two days since the fatal night,—not suffering; every nerve of pain was blunted and destroyed. For the most part, he was in a quiet stupor; for the strong, well-knit frame did not at once release the imprisoned spirit. By stealth, in the darkness, poor desolated creatures had come, filching from their scanty rest, to repay, with the cup of cold water given with full hearts, some of those ministrations of love he had so abundantly bestowed. Tears had fallen on the honest, insensible face,—tears of late repentance in poor, ignorant heathen, whom his dying patience had awakened,—and bitter prayers to a late-found Saviour, whom they scarce knew more than by the name, yet whom the yearning, ignorant heart never implores in vain. Cassy, gliding from her concealment, and, by overhearing, learning the sacrifice made for her and Emmeline, had dared detection to come; and, moved by the last few words his affectionate soul had strength to breathe, the long winter of despair—the ice of years—broke; and the dark, despairing woman wept and prayed.

When George entered, his head reeled, and his heart sickened.

"Is it possible,—is it possible?" said he, kneeling. "Uncle Tom, my poor, poor old friend!"

Something in the voice pierced the dying ear. He moved his head gently, smiled, and said,

"Jesus can make a dying-bed
Feel soft as down pillows are."

Tears, that did honor to his manly heart, fell from the young man's eyes, as he bent over him.

"O, dear Uncle Tom! do wake,—do speak once more! Look up! Here's Mas'r George,—your own little Mas'r George. Don't you know me?"

"Mas'r George!" said Tom, opening his eyes, feebly. "Mas'r George!" He looked bewildered. Slowly the idea filled his soul; the vacant eye fixed and brightened; the whole face lighted; the hard hands clasped; tears ran down.

"Bless the Lord! it is,—it is,—it's all I wanted! They haven't forgot me. It warms my soul; it does my heart good! Now I shall die content! Bless the Lord, on my soul!"

"You shan't die! you mustn't die, nor think of it! I've come to buy you, and take you home," cried George, with impetuous vehemence.

"O, Mas'r George, ye're too late. The Lord's bought me, and is going to take me home,—and I long to go. Heaven is better than Kintuck."

"O, don't die! It'll kill me!—it'll break my heart to think what you've suffered,—and lying in this old shed, here! Poor, poor fellow!"

"Don't call me poor fellow!" said Tom, solemnly. "I have been poor fellow; but that's all past and gone, now. I'm right in the door, going into glory! O, Mas'r George! Heaven has come! I've got the victory!—the Lord Jesus has given it to me! Glory be to His name!"

George sat, awe-struck, gazing in silence, at the force with which these broken sentences were uttered.

Tom grasped his hand, and went on: "Ye mustn't, now, tell Chloe, poor soul! how ye found me;—'t would be so drefful to her. Only tell her ye found me going into glory; and that I couldn't stay for no one. And tell her the Lord's stood by me everywhere and al'ays, and made everything light and easy. And oh, the poor chil'en, and the baby;—my old heart's been most broke for 'em, time and agin! Tell 'em all to follow me—follow me! Give my love to Mas'r, and dear good Missis, and everybody in the place! Ye don't know! 'Pears like I loves 'em all! I loves every creature everywhar!—it's nothing but love! O, Mas'r George! what a thing 't is to be a Christian!"

At this moment, Legree sauntered to the door, looked in with a dogged, affected carelessness, and turned away.

"The old Satan!" said George, in his indignation. "It's a comfort to think the devil will pay him for this, some of these days!"

"O, don't!—oh, ye mustn't!" said Tom, grasping his hand; "he's a poor mis'able critter! it's awful to think on 't! Oh, if he only could repent, the Lord would forgive him now; but I'm 'feared he never will!"

"I hope he won't!" said George; "I never want to see him in heaven!"

"Hush, Mas'r George!—it worries me! Don't feel so! He an't done me no real harm,—only opened the gate of the kingdom for me; that's all!"

The sudden flush of strength, lent by joy at meeting his young master, ebbed. A sinking fell on him; he closed his eyes; and that mysterious, sublime change passed over his face that told of other worlds. He drew long, deep breaths; the broad chest rose and fell heavily; the expression was that of a conqueror.

"Who,—who,—who shall separate us from the love of Christ?" he said, in a voice that contended with mortal weakness; and, with a smile, he fell asleep.

George sat fixed in solemn awe. The place seemed holy; and, as he closed the lifeless eyes, and rose up from the dead, one thought possessed him,—that expressed by his simple old friend,—"What a thing it is to be a Christian!"

He turned: Legree stood, sullenly, behind him. The presence was simply loathsome; the fierceness of youthful passion was checked; he only longed to be rid of him with fewest words. Fixing his keen dark eyes on Legree, he said, pointing to the dead, "You have got all you ever can of him. What shall I pay you for the body? I will take it away, and bury it decently."

"I don't sell dead niggers," said Legree, doggedly. "You are welcome to bury him where and when you like."

"Boys," said George, authoritatively, to two or three negroes who were looking on, "help me lift him up, and carry him to my wagon; and get me a spade."

One ran for a spade; the others assisted George to bear the body. He spread his cloak in the wagon, disposed the body carefully in it, moved the seat to give room; then, turning, with forced composure, fixed his eyes on Legree.

"I have not, as yet, said to you what I think of this most atrocious affair;—this is not the time and place. But, sir, this innocent blood shall have justice. I will proclaim this murder. I will go to the very first magistrate, and expose you."

"Do!" said Legree, snapping his fingers, scornfully. "I'd like to see you doing it. Where you going to get witnesses?—how you going to prove it?—Come, now!"

George saw, at once, the force of the defiance. There was not a white person on the place; and, in southern courts, colored testimony is nothing. He felt as if he could have rent the heavens with his heart's indignant cry for justice; but in vain.

"After all, what a fuss, for a dead nigger!" said Legree.

The word was as fire to powder. Prudence was never a cardinal virtue of the Kentucky boy. George turned, and, with one indignant blow, knocked Legree flat upon his face; and, standing over him, blazing with wrath and defiance, he might have personified his great namesake triumphing over the dragon. Some men are bettered by being knocked down; they conceive a respect for the hand that lays them fairly in the dust. Legree was of this sort. Rising, brushing his clothes, he eyed the slowly-retreating wagon with consideration, and held his peace till it was out of sight.

Beyond the plantation bounds, George had marked a dry, sandy knoll, shaded by a few trees; there they made the grave.

"Shall we take off the cloak, Mas'r?" said the negroes, when the place was ready.

"No, no,—bury it with him! It's all I can give you, now, poor Tom, and you shall have it."

They laid him in; the men shovelled away, silently; they banked it, and spread green turf.

"You may go, boys," said George, slipping a quarter into each hand. They lingered.

"If young Mas'r would please buy us—" said one.

"We'd serve him so faithful!" said the other.

"Hard times here, Mas'r!" said the first. "Do, Mas'r, buy us, please!"

"I can't!—I can't!" said George, with difficulty, motioning them off; "it's impossible!"

They looked dejected, and walked away in silence.

"Witness, eternal God!" said George, kneeling on the grave of his poor friend; "oh, witness, that, from this hour, I will do what one man can to drive out this curse of slavery from my land!"

There is no monument to mark his rest. He needs none. His Lord knows where he lies, and will raise him up, immortal, to appear with Him in His glory. Pity him not! Such a life and death are not for pity. Not in the riches of omnipotence is the chief glory of God, but in self-denying, suffering love; and blessed are they whom He calls into fellowship with Him, bearing their cross after Him with patience. "Blessed are they that mourn, for they shall be comforted."

As ghost rumors spread, Cassy and Emmeline slip from the garret in disguise—Cassy as a Creole lady, Emmeline as her maid—reach the riverboats, and there encounter George Shelby and a French lady, Madame de Thoux, who reveals herself as George Harris’s sister; hearing names and papers, Cassy first suspects that Eliza may be her long-lost child. Legree’s terror intensifies, and that night the house is found open as two white figures vanish down the avenue.

The rest of our story is soon told. George Shelby, interested, as any other young man might be, by the romance of the incident, no less than by feelings of humanity, sent to Cassy the bill of sale of Eliza; its date and name all corresponded with her knowledge of facts, and she felt no doubt of her child’s identity. It remained only to trace the fugitives’ path.

Madame de Thoux and Cassy, thus curiously joined by fortune, went straightway to Canada, making inquiry among the stations where fugitives were located. At Amherstberg they found the missionary who had sheltered George and Eliza on their first landing, and through him traced the family to Montreal.

George and Eliza had now been five years free. George, in a worthy machinist’s shop, had found constant occupation, earning a competent support; the family had been increased by another daughter. Little Harry—a fine, bold boy—was at a good school, making rapid progress.

So much was the Amherstberg pastor moved by the statements of Madame de Thoux and Cassy, that, at the former’s solicitation, he accompanied them to Montreal in their search,—she bearing all the expense.

The scene changes to a small, neat tenement on the outskirts of Montreal; evening. A cheerful fire on the hearth; a tea-table spread; in one corner a green-covered table, with an open desk, pens and paper, and a shelf of well-chosen books. This was George’s study. The same zeal for improvement which had led him to snatch the arts of reading and writing, amid toil and discouragement, still made him devote leisure to self-cultivation. He sat making notes from a volume of the family library.

“Come, George,” says Eliza, “you’ve been gone all day. Do put down that book, and let’s talk, while I’m getting tea,—do.”

And little Eliza seconds it by toddling up and striving to pull away the book, to install herself on his knee.

“O, you little witch!” says George, yielding, as, in such circumstances, man always must.

“That’s right,” says Eliza, with a matronly air, yet contented and happy as woman need be.

“Harry, my boy, how did you come on in that sum, to-day?” says George, laying his hand on his son’s head.

“I did it, every bit of it, myself, father; and nobody helped me!”

“That’s right,” says his father; “depend on yourself, my son. You have a better chance than ever your poor father had.”

There is a rap at the door. Eliza opens it; the delighted “Why! this you?” calls up her husband; and the good pastor of Amherstberg is welcomed. Two ladies are with him, to whom Eliza offers seats.

Truth to say, the honest pastor had arranged a little programme for the development of this affair, and all, on the way, had prudently exhorted each other to keep to order. Conceive, then, his consternation, when, just as he had motioned the ladies to be seated, and was wiping his lips for his introductory speech, Madame de Thoux threw her arms round George’s neck, and, letting all out at once, cried,

“O, George! don’t you know me? I’m your sister Emily.”

Cassy had seated herself more composedly, and would have carried her part well, had not little Eliza suddenly appeared before her in exact shape and curl as her own child had been when last she saw her. The little thing peered up; Cassy caught her to her bosom, saying, in what, at the moment, she really believed,

“Darling, I’m your mother!”

In fine, it was troublesome to do it all in order; but the good pastor at last succeeded in composing them, and delivered the speech he had intended; and so well, that his whole audience were sobbing about him in a manner to satisfy any orator.

They knelt together; and the good man prayed,—for there are feelings so agitated and tumultuous that they can find rest only by being poured into the bosom of Almighty love,—and, rising, the new-found family embraced with a holy trust in Him who, through perils and by unknown ways, had brought them together.

The missionary’s note-book among the Canadian fugitives contains truth stranger than fiction. When a system prevails that whirls and scatters families as the wind the autumn leaves, these shores of refuge, like the eternal shore, often unite in glad communion hearts long mourning each other as lost. Deeds of heroism are wrought, when, defying torture and braving death, the fugitive threads back to that dark land to bring away sister, mother, or wife. One young man, twice recaptured and shamefully scourged, escaped again, and wrote that he would go a third time to bring his sister. Is this man a hero, or a criminal? Would not you do as much?

But to return to our friends, now seated about the social board, decidedly companionable; only that Cassy, who keeps little Eliza on her lap, now and then squeezes her in a way that astonishes the child, and obstinately refuses to stuff her with cake, alleging, to the little one’s wonder, that she has something better than cake.

Indeed, in two or three days such a change passed over Cassy that our readers would scarce know her. The despairing, haggard look gave place to gentle trust. She sank at once into the bosom of the family, took the little ones into her heart, as that for which it long had waited. Her love flowed even more naturally to the little Eliza than to her own daughter, for she was the very image of the lost child,—a flowery bond through whom grew up acquaintanceship and affection. Eliza’s steady, consistent piety, formed by constant reading of the sacred word, made her a proper guide to her mother’s shattered and wearied mind. Cassy yielded, with her whole soul, to every good influence, and became a devout and tender Christian.

After a day or two, Madame de Thoux told her brother more particularly of her affairs. The death of her husband had left her an ample fortune, which she generously offered to share. When she asked George how best to apply it, he answered,

“Give me an education, Emily; that has always been my heart’s desire. Then, I can do all the rest.”

On mature deliberation, it was decided that the whole family should go, for some years, to France; whither they sailed, carrying Emmeline with them. The latter’s good looks won the affection of the first mate; shortly after entering port she became his wife. George remained four years at a French university, where, with unintermitted zeal, he obtained a thorough education. Political troubles at last led the family again to seek an asylum in this country.

George’s feelings and views, as an educated man, may be best gathered from a letter to a friend:

“I feel somewhat at a loss as to my future course. True, as you have said, I might mingle in the circles of the whites; my shade of color is slight, and that of my wife and children scarce perceptible. Well—perhaps, on sufferance, I might. But, to tell you the truth, I have no wish to.

“My sympathies are not for my father’s race, but for my mother’s. To him I was no more than a fine dog or horse: to my poor heart-broken mother I was a child. When I think of all she suffered; of my own early sufferings; of the distresses and struggles of my heroic wife; of my sister, sold in the New Orleans market,—though I hope to have no unchristian sentiments, yet I may be excused for saying, I have no wish to pass for an American, or to identify myself with them.

“The desire and yearning of my soul is for an African nationality. I want a people that shall have a tangible, separate existence of its own; and where am I to look for it? Not in Hayti; for in Hayti they had nothing to start with. A stream cannot rise above its fountain. The race that formed the character of the Haytiens was a worn-out, effeminate one; the subject race will be centuries in rising.

“Where, then, shall I look? On the shores of Africa I see a republic,—formed of picked men, who by energy and self-education have individually raised themselves above slavery. Having gone through feebleness, this republic has become an acknowledged nation, owned by France and England. There it is my wish to go, and find myself a people.

“I grant Liberia may have been played off, in the hands of our oppressors, against us; may have been used unjustifiably to retard emancipation. But is there not a God above man’s schemes? May He not have overruled their designs, and founded for us a nation by them?

“In these days, a nation is born in a day. A nation starts now with the great problems of republican life wrought out to its hand;—it has not to discover, but only to apply. Let us then all take hold, with all our might, and see what we can do; and the whole splendid continent of Africa opens before us and our children. Our nation shall roll the tide of civilization and Christianity along its shores, and plant there mighty republics, that, growing with tropical rapidity, shall be for coming ages.

“Do you say I am deserting my enslaved brethren? I think not. If I forget them one hour, one moment of my life, so may God forget me! But what can I do for them here? Can I break their chains? No—not as an individual; but let me go and form part of a nation that shall have a voice in the councils of nations, and then we can speak. A nation has a right to argue, remonstrate, implore, and present the cause of its race,—which an individual has not.

“If Europe becomes a grand council of free nations,—as I trust in God it will,—and if, as France and England have done, they acknowledge our position; then, in the great congress of nations, we will present the cause of our enslaved and suffering race; and it cannot be that free, enlightened America will not then desire to wipe from her escutcheon that bar sinister which disgraces her among nations, and is as truly a curse to her as to the enslaved.

“You will tell me, our race have equal rights to mingle in the American republic as the Irishman, the German, the Swede. Granted, they have; and they who deny it are false to professed principles of human equality. We have more than the rights of common men—we have the claim of an injured race for reparation. But then, I do not want it. I want a country, a nation, of my own. I think the African race has peculiarities, yet to be unfolded in the light of civilization and Christianity, which, if not the same with those of the Anglo-Saxon, may prove, morally, of even a higher type.

“I trust the development of Africa is to be essentially Christian. If not a dominant and commanding race, they are, at least, an affectionate, magnanimous, forgiving one. Called in the furnace of oppression, they must bind to their hearts that sublime doctrine of love and forgiveness, through which alone they are to conquer, and which it is to be their mission to spread over Africa.

“In myself I am feeble for this,—full half the blood in my veins is the hot and hasty Saxon; but I have an eloquent preacher ever by my side, in my beautiful wife. When I wander, her gentler spirit restores me, and keeps before my eyes the Christian calling of our race. As a Christian patriot, as a teacher of Christianity, I go to my country,—my chosen, my glorious Africa!—and to her, in my heart, I sometimes apply those splendid words: ‘Whereas thou hast been forsaken and hated, so that no man went through thee; I will make thee an eternal excellence, a joy of many generations!’

“You will call me an enthusiast; say I have not counted the cost. But I have considered it. I go to Liberia, not as an Elysium of romance, but as to a field of work. I expect to work with both hands,—to work hard; against all sorts of difficulties; and to work till I die. This is what I go for; and in this I am quite sure I shall not be disappointed.

“Whatever you may think of my determination, do not divorce me from your confidence; and think that, in whatever I do, I act with a heart wholly given to my people.

“GEORGE HARRIS.”

George, with his wife, children, sister, and mother, embarked for Africa a few weeks after. If we are not mistaken, the world will yet hear from him there.

Of our other characters we have little to add, save a word of Miss Ophelia and Topsy, and a farewell chapter to George Shelby. Miss Ophelia took Topsy home to Vermont,—to the surprise of that grave deliberative body, “our folks.” They thought it an odd addition to a well-trained household; but so thorough was Miss Ophelia in conscientious duty to her élève, that the child grew rapidly in grace and favor with family and neighborhood. At womanhood, by her own request, she was baptized and became a member of the Christian church; and showed such intelligence, activity, zeal, and desire to do good, that she was approved as a missionary to a station in Africa; and we have heard that the same ingenuity which, when a child, made her multiform and restless, is now employed, in safer and wholesomer ways, in teaching the children of her own country.

P.S.—Some inquiries, set on foot by Madame de Thoux, have recently resulted in the discovery of Cassy’s son. Being a young man of energy, he had escaped, years before his mother, and been received and educated by friends of the oppressed in the North. He will soon follow his family to Africa.

George Shelby had written home merely a line, naming the day he might be expected. Of the death-scene of his old friend he had not the heart to write. He tried, choked, tore up the sheet, wiped his eyes, and fled to be alone. Meanwhile there was a pleased bustle through the Shelby mansion in expectation of young Mas'r George. Mrs. Shelby sat in her comfortable parlor, a cheerful hickory fire dispelling the late autumn chill; a supper-table, glittering with plate and cut glass, was set out, over which Aunt Chloe, Tom's wife, presided.

Arrayed in a new calico, with clean white apron and high, well-starched turban, her black face glowing, she lingered with needless punctiliousness about the table.

"Laws, now! won't it look natural to him?" she said. "Thar,—I set his plate just whar he likes it, round by the fire. Mas'r George allers wants de warm seat. O, go way!—why didn't Sally get out de best tea-pot,—de little new one, Mas'r George got for Missis, Christmas? I'll have it out! And Missis has heard from Mas'r George?"

"Yes, Chloe; but only a line, just to say he would be home to-night, if he could,—that's all."

"Didn't say nothin' 'bout my old man, s'pose?" she went on, fidgeting with the tea-cups.

"No. He said he would tell all when he got home."

"Jes like Mas'r George,—he's allers so ferce for tellin' everything hisself," said Chloe. "I's got jist de very pattern my old man liked so much, a bakin'; jist sich as I gin him de mornin' he was took off.—Lord bless us!"

The rattling of wheels was heard.

"Mas'r George!" cried Aunt Chloe, starting to the window.

Mrs. Shelby ran to the door, and was folded in her son's arms. Aunt Chloe strained her eyes into the darkness.

"O, poor Aunt Chloe!" said George, stopping compassionately, and taking her hard, black hand between both his; "I'd have given all my fortune to have brought him with me, but he's gone to a better country."

There was a passionate exclamation from Mrs. Shelby; Aunt Chloe said nothing.

In the supper-room, the money still lay on the table—the very bills in which Chloe's wages had been paid while hired out, which she had insisted on preserving to show her husband as proof of her capability, and of which she was so proud. "Thar," said she, gathering it up with a trembling hand, and holding it to her mistress, "don't never want to see nor hear on 't again. Jist as I knew 't would be,—sold, and murdered on dem ar' old plantations!" She turned to go. Mrs. Shelby followed softly, drew her down, and sat beside her.

"My poor, good Chloe!" said she.

Chloe leaned her head on her mistress' shoulder, and sobbed, "O Missis! 'scuse me, my heart's broke,—dat's all!"

"I know it is," said Mrs. Shelby, as her tears fell fast; "and I cannot heal it, but Jesus can. 'He healeth the broken-hearted, and bindeth up their wounds.'"

They sat long, all weeping together; and George, sitting down beside the mourner, took her hand, and with simple pathos repeated the triumphant scene of her husband's death, and his last messages of love.

About a month after, one morning, all the servants on the estate were convened in the great hall to hear a few words from their young master. To the surprise of all, he appeared with a bundle of papers, and read successively a certificate of freedom to every one on the place, presenting each, amid sobs, tears, and shouts.

Many pressed around, earnestly begging not to be sent away, tendering back their free papers.

"We don't want to be no freer than we are. We's allers had all we wanted. We don't want to leave de ole place, and Mas'r and Missis, and de rest!"

"My good friends," said George, when he could be heard, "there'll be no need for you to leave me. The place wants as many hands to work it as it did before. We need the same about the house that we did before. But you are now free men and free women. I shall pay you wages for your work, such as we shall agree on. The advantage is, that in case of my getting in debt, or dying,—things that might happen,—you cannot now be taken up and sold. I expect to carry on the estate, and to teach you what, perhaps, it will take you some time to learn,—how to use the rights I give you as free men and women. I expect you to be good, and willing to learn; and I trust in God that I shall be faithful, and willing to teach. And now, my friends, look up, and thank God for the blessing of freedom."

An aged, patriarchal negro, gray and blind, now rose, and, lifting his trembling hand, said, "Let us give thanks unto the Lord!" All kneeled by one consent; a more touching and hearty _Te Deum_ never ascended to heaven.

On rising, another struck up a Methodist hymn, of which the burden was,

"The year of Jubilee is come,—
Return, ye ransomed sinners, home."

"One thing more," said George, staying the congratulations; "you all remember our good old Uncle Tom?"

He gave a short narration of the scene of his death, and of his loving farewell to all on the place, and added, "It was on his grave, my friends, that I resolved, before God, that I would never own another slave, while it was possible to free him; that nobody, through me, should ever run the risk of being parted from home and friends, and dying on a lonely plantation, as he died. So, when you rejoice in your freedom, think that you owe it to that good old soul, and pay it back in kindness to his wife and children. Think of your freedom, every time you see UNCLE TOM'S CABIN; and let it be a memorial to put you all in mind to follow in his steps, and be honest and faithful and Christian as he was."

Concluding Remarks

The writer has been often asked whether this narrative be true; she gives one general answer. The separate incidents are, to a very great extent, authentic, occurring either under her own observation, or that of personal friends; counterparts of almost all the characters have been seen; many sayings are word for word as heard or reported. The personal appearance and character of Eliza were drawn from life; the incorruptible fidelity, piety, and honesty of Uncle Tom had more than one development to her knowledge. The mother's crossing the Ohio on the ice is a well-known fact. The story of "old Prue" fell under the observation of her brother, then a collecting-clerk in New Orleans. From the same source came the planter Legree: her brother wrote, after visiting his plantation, that the man made him feel of his fist, "calloused with knocking down niggers," and that he drew a long breath, as if escaped from an ogre's den.

That Tom's tragical fate has had its parallels, there are living witnesses all over our land to testify. Let it be remembered that, in all southern states, no person of colored lineage can testify in a suit against a white; it is easy to see how a case may occur wherever a man's passions outweigh his interests, and a slave has principle enough to resist his will. There is, actually, nothing to protect the slave's life, but the character of the master. To say such cases are rare is to forget that, were New England laws so framed that a master could now and then torture an apprentice to death, we should not be satisfied to be told they are "no sample of general practice." This injustice is an inherent one in the slave system,—it cannot exist without it.

The public and shameless sale of beautiful mulatto and quadroon girls has been notorious since the capture of the schooner Pearl. Hon. Horace Mann, counsel for the defendants, related that among the seventy-six who attempted escape in 1848 was a young girl doomed for the New Orleans market; friends offered a great ransom, but the trader was inexorable; God smote her with death on the passage. Two sisters, when pleaded for, were bantered with promises of fine dresses and furniture; "Yes," said the pleader, "that may do very well in this life, but what will become of them in the next?" They were sent to New Orleans, and afterwards redeemed at enormous cost. Is it not plain that the histories of Emmeline and Cassy may have many counterparts?

Justice, too, obliges the author to say that the fairness and generosity ascribed to St. Clare are not without a parallel. A few years since, a young southern gentleman, in Cincinnati with a favorite servant named Nathan, who had waited on him from a boy, lost the man to a Quaker's protection. In high anger he visited the Quaker; but, being candid, was quieted by arguments he had never heard. He said, "If, to my face, he says it is his desire to be free, I will liberate him." An interview was procured, and Nathan was asked whether he had ever had cause to complain. "No, Mas'r; you've always been good to me." "Why then leave me?" "Mas'r may die, and then who get me?—I'd rather be a free man." "Nathan, in your place, I think I should feel very much so, myself. You are free." He made out free papers, deposited money to help him start, and left a sensible letter of advice. Such nobility saves us from utter despair of our kind; but are such characters common anywhere?

For many years the author avoided the subject, trusting advancing light would live it down; but, after the act of 1850, when she heard Christian and humane people recommending the remanding of fugitives as a citizen's duty, she could only think, They cannot know what slavery is. Hence her desire to exhibit it in a living dramatic reality. She has shown it fairly, in its best and worst phases. In its best aspect, perhaps she has been successful; but, oh! who shall say what yet remains untold in that valley and shadow of death?

To you, generous men and women of the South, tried by severer discipline, is her appeal. In your secret souls, have you not felt woes and evils, in this accursed system, far beyond what is here shadowed? Is man ever to be trusted with wholly irresponsible power? Does not the slave code, by denying the slave all legal right of testimony, make every owner an irresponsible despot? If there be a public sentiment of honor and humanity among you, is there not also another among the ruffian and debased? And cannot the ruffian own as many slaves as the purest? Are the honorable the majority anywhere?

American law calls the slave-trade piracy; yet a trade as systematic as any on the African coast inevitably attends American slavery, with heartbreaks and horrors untold. There are those living who know the mothers whom this traffic has driven to the murder of their children, and themselves to death as a refuge from woes worse than death. Nothing of tragedy can be written or conceived that equals the frightful reality, daily and hourly enacting beneath the shadow of American law and of the cross of Christ.

And now, men and women of America, is this to be trifled with, apologized for, passed in silence? And you, mothers of America,—by the sacred love you bear your child; by the motherly pity with which you guide his years; by the sick hour; by those dying eyes you can never forget; by the desolation of the empty cradle,—pity the mother who has all your affections and not one legal right to protect the child of her bosom! Is this a thing to be defended?

Say not the free states have nothing to do with it. They have defended, encouraged, participated; merchants in northern cities temporarily own and sell again; the traffic in souls is made an equivalent to money. Northern men, mothers, Christians, must look to the evil among themselves. What can any individual do? This every one can do,—see to it that they feel right. An atmosphere of sympathy encircles every soul; the man or woman who feels strongly, healthily, justly, on the great interests of humanity, is a constant benefactor. And you can pray. You pray for the heathen abroad; pray also for the heathen at home, and for those distressed Christians whose adherence to the morals of Christianity is, in many cases, an impossibility, unless grace from above give them the courage of martyrdom.

On our free shores are emerging poor, shattered remnants of families, escaped by providences from the surge of slavery, feeble in knowledge, shaken in moral constitution. Do we owe them nothing? Shall church and school-house be shut? Some say, "Let them go to Africa." That God has provided a refuge in Africa is notable; but that is no reason for the church to throw off the responsibility her profession demands. Receive them to the educating advantages of Christian republican society and schools; when they have attained some maturity, then assist them to those shores where they may put in practice what they have learned. A body of men at the North have done this, and the country has seen former slaves rapidly acquire property, reputation, and education. On the authority of Professor C. E. Stowe, cases in Cincinnati show men, full black and three-fourths, worth from ten to thirty thousand dollars, all earned by themselves, often after paying heavy sums for freedom. The writer remembers an aged washerwoman whose daughter, by thrift and self-denial, raised nine hundred dollars for her husband's freedom, paying it as she raised it; he died before the last hundred, and she never recovered the money. The colored man, by Ohio law, cannot be a voter, and was till lately denied the right of testimony; yet we see men, but yesterday from shackles, rise by a self-educating force to highly respectable stations,—Pennington among clergymen, and Douglass and Ward among editors. If, with every discouragement, they have done thus much, how much more might they do if the Christian church would act towards them in the spirit of her Lord!

This is an age when nations tremble. A mighty influence is abroad, surging the world as with an earthquake. O Church of Christ, read the signs of the times! Is not this power the Spirit of Him whose kingdom is to come? But who may abide the day of his appearing? "for that day shall burn as an oven: and he shall appear as a swift witness against those that oppress the hireling in his wages, the widow and the fatherless, and that turn aside the stranger in his right: and he shall break in pieces the oppressor." Christians! every time you pray that the kingdom may come, forget not that prophecy links, in dread fellowship, the day of vengeance with the year of his redeemed.

A day of grace is yet held out to us. Both North and South have been guilty before God; and the Christian church has a heavy account to answer. Not by combining together to protect injustice and cruelty, and making a common capital of sin, is this Union to be saved,—but by repentance, justice and mercy; for, not surer is the eternal law by which the millstone sinks in the ocean, than that stronger law, by which injustice and cruelty shall bring on nations the wrath of Almighty God!
